**Summary.** The research presents the first on-chip radio-frequency maser using optically pumped pentacene at room temperature. By integrating the gain medium into a compact LC resonator and implementing active feedback control over the circuit's quality factor, the authors achieved stable microwave emission and demonstrated ultra-sensitive magnetic field detection ($\sim 10 	ext{ fT}/\sqrt{	ext{Hz}}$). This establishes a highly integrated platform for portable quantum sensing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses solid-state quantum emitters for microwave detection. The ability to integrate the gain medium into a chip-scale resonator while actively tuning its coupling strength ($Q_L$) is highly relevant for building portable, high-performance quantum sensors that bridge fundamental physics with practical engineering.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a highly integrated, room-temperature solid-state maser operating at radio frequencies (RF) for applications like ultra-sensitive microwave magnetic-field detection.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated the first on-chip RF maser using pentacene, achieving stimulated emission at 106.62 MHz and reaching a magnetic-field sensitivity of $\sim 10 	ext{ fT}/\sqrt{	ext{Hz}}$.

**Method.** They utilized an optically pumped system within a compact on-chip LC resonant circuit, employing active feedback control to tune the resonator's quality factor ($Q_L$) between oscillator and sensing regimes.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is pentacene doped p-terphenyl exhibiting optically pumped triplet states ($T_1$). The maser operates by exploiting the X-Y sublevel transition ($\sim 106$ MHz) within an LC resonator structure integrated on a chip, replacing bulky metallic resonators.

**Key observables.** Maser output signal intensity (showing $\propto \sqrt{E_{	ext{pump}}}$ dependence), stable oscillation spectra locked to the X-Y frequency, and magnetic field response ($\partial S / \partial B_1$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating frequency of 106.62 MHz; sensitivity of $10 	ext{ fT}/\sqrt{	ext{Hz}}$; tunability via controlling $Q_L$ from intrinsic ($\approx 40$) to several thousand.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The threshold pump energy required for low-$Q_L$ operation far exceeds the pentacene damage threshold, suggesting improving the intrinsic $Q_L$ is necessary for further development.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the mechanism (pumping $ightarrow T_1 ightarrow X-Y$ inversion) and the two operating regimes. Time-domain data shows signal build-up, FFT confirms stable oscillation, and plots demonstrate intensity scaling with pump energy and threshold dependence on $Q_L$.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the physical mechanism of optical pumping in pentacene to achieve triplet state inversion, describes the integration into an LC resonator circuit, presents experimental results showing tunable operation between maser oscillation (high $Q_L$) and sensitive sensing (low $Q_L$), and concludes with performance metrics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Room-temperature solid-state masers offer exceptional frequency selectivity and ultra-low noise for weak-signal detection. However, their reliance on bulky metallic resonators has significantly hindered integration, miniaturization, and extension to lower frequencies. Here, we demonstrate the first on-chip radio-frequency maser operating at room temperature, exploiting optically pumped triplet states of pentacene. The device produces stimulated emission at 106.62 MHz and enables ultra-sensitive microwave magnetic-field detection with a sensitivity of ($\sim 10\,\rm{fT/\sqrt{Hz}}$), functioning simultaneously as a local oscillator and a sensor. By actively controlling microwave dissipation, we achieve efficient regulation of the maser output, revealing a key mechanism for tuning emission in open cavity-free systems. This work extends pentacene-based masers into the radio-frequency regime and establishes a highly integrated on-chip architecture for room-temperature masers, offering a new pathway toward portable quantum devices.

</details>

(a) Input–output curves of the driven lower polariton, where the vertical axis shows the lower–polariton population n and the horizontal axis displays the drive intensity |F1|2/ω2 x; the different values of g are indicated in the legend. At small light–matter...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically analyzes bistability in semiconductor microcavities operating under ultrastrong coupling. By retaining counter-rotating terms beyond the standard RWA, the authors show that while the qualitative physics remains similar to strong coupling, quantitative predictions for hysteresis loops are significantly shifted due to spectral renormalization.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes beyond the standard Rotating Wave Approximation (RWA) in cavity QED systems, showing how fundamental quantum corrections (counter-rotating terms) alter macroscopic nonlinear phenomena like optical switching.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the phenomenon of optical bistability in exciton-photon microcavities when operating within the ultrastrong-coupling regime.

**Main result.** While the bistability retains a standard Kerr-oscillator form, the inclusion of counter-rotating processes and diamagnetic terms renormalizes the polariton spectrum, causing measurable shifts in the turning points and hysteresis window compared to predictions from the strong-coupling (RWA) limit.

**Method.** The analysis uses a mean-field approximation on the effective single-mode Hamiltonian derived after diagonalizing the full Hopfield-Rabi model using Bogoliubov transformations.

**Model / system.** The system is an exciton-photon microcavity modeled by a Hamiltonian including Rabi coupling, Kerr nonlinearity, and external driving. The dynamics are treated via a Lindblad master equation for the lower polariton branch approximation.

**Key observables.** Bistability (S-shaped input-output curves), polariton frequencies ($\omega_{1,2}$), effective detuning ($	ilde{\Delta}_1$), and nonlinearity ($U_{\mathrm{LP}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ultrastrong-coupling regime ($\eta \gtrsim 0.1$), coupling strength ($g$), and the diamagnetic term $D$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a weakly nonlinear regime, allowing for an effective single-mode description (lower polariton approximation), and explicitly neglects phase-space filling effects.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates how the lower and upper polariton frequencies depend on the coupling strength $g$ and other parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the full Hamiltonian formulation, reduces it to an effective single-mode description via polariton diagonalization, derives the semiclassical equations of motion using a mean-field approach, and finally analyzes stability criteria to map out the bistability regions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate a coherently driven exciton--photon microcavity with Kerr nonlinearity in the ultrastrong-coupling regime. When the lower and upper polariton branches are well separated in energy, the full Hopfield--Rabi--Kerr model reduces to an effective single-mode description of the lower polariton. We analyze the stability of the lower-polariton steady states. We show that the resulting bistability is qualitatively similar to that in the strong-coupling regime. However, in the ultrastrong-coupling regime counter-rotating processes and the diamagnetic $A^{2}$ term renormalize the polariton spectrum and composition, changing the effective detuning $\tildeΔ_{1}$ and nonlinearity $U_{\mathrm{LP}}$ $g$-dependency beyond the strong-coupling (RWA) picture. As a result, although the semiclassical bistability criterion keeps its standard Kerr--oscillator form, the turning points and hysteresis window are shifted relative to the strong-coupling prediction.

</details>

**Summary.** This work models double-stranded semiflexible chains to understand how base-pairing and twisting energies dictate the overall helical structure. Using Monte Carlo simulations, the authors map out distinct morphological phases—including unwinding transitions—by tracking topological invariants like the Gauss linking number. The findings elucidate a delicate energetic balance governing structural stability in biopolymer systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on biophysics (DNA), the methodology—using statistical mechanics tools like phase transition analysis, energy minimization across competing structural states, and topological diagnostics ($	ext{Lk}$)—is highly relevant for understanding complex many-body systems in AMO physics or quantum materials where local interactions drive global order.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the global morphological transitions of double-stranded semiflexible chains, specifically determining the balance required between base-pairing interactions and twisting energy to maintain the double-helix structure without external constraints.

**Main result.** The model exhibits distinct phases (flat, random coil, double-helix, unwound) whose transitions are characterized by first-order changes in linking number. The system's stability is governed by minimizing total energy across these competing structural states.

**Method.** Monte Carlo simulations using the Metropolis algorithm were employed to sample configurations based on a Hamiltonian incorporating bending, pairing, and twisting energies.

**Model / system.** The physical system is modeled as two semiflexible chains interacting via base-pairing ($E_{	ext{pair}}$) and diagonal interactions ($E_{	ext{diag}}$), with handedness regulated by $E_{	ext{twist}}$. The total energy includes bending terms derived from the Kratky-Porod potential.

**Key observables.** Gauss linking number ($	ext{Lk}$), Order Parameter ($	ext{Nu/N}$), correlation functions ($\langle t_n \cdot t_0 angle$, $\langle c_n \cdot c_0 angle$), and average energy per segment ($\epsilon$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strengths ($D$ for base-stacking, $P$ for twist symmetry breaking) and the dimensionless temperature parameter ($\Lambda = T/T_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Bending flexibility is assumed to follow the Kratky-Porod potential. The analysis relies on fitting correlation functions to damped oscillatory forms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate distinct morphologies based on varying interaction parameters ($P$ and $D$), plot the linking number ($	ext{Lk}(n)$) across different phases, and show temperature dependence of persistence lengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the model Hamiltonian, uses Monte Carlo simulations to map out phase diagrams based on coupling constants ($D, P$), analyzes topological invariants like the linking number to pinpoint transitions, and finally examines thermal effects via correlation functions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The mechanical and structural properties of dsDNA have been successfully described by models with varying levels of complexity and coarse-graining schemes. Prior work has characterized local stacking/twist effects and force-torque phase diagrams under external constraints. However, the role of base-pairing and torsional elasticity in global morphological transitions remain poorly characterized in the absence of external constraints. Here we investigate the delicate balance required for the strength of base-pairing interactions and the twisting energy to preserve the double-helix structure in a model made up of two semiflexible chains. We found that the model exhibits several distinct morphological phases: flat, random coil, double-helix, and the unwound double-helix. We calculate the Gauss linking number to characterize transitions between these phases.

</details>

**Summary.** This work experimentally realizes and analyzes one-dimensional ballistic aggregation using a train of liquid droplets. The research confirms that the process follows predicted scaling laws governed by a single length scale, demonstrating that mean-field theories fail when directional memory is present in collisions.

**Why it may be interesting.** Although focused on classical fluid dynamics, the failure of mean-field descriptions due to directional memory echoes challenges in modeling non-equilibrium quantum systems where simple approximations break down.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates ballistic aggregation, a non-equilibrium process relevant across physics scales, by confirming theoretical scaling laws through experimental realization.

**Main result.** The global aggregation process is shown to be governed by a single length scale, and the measured mass growth exponent matches the theoretically predicted value of 2/3.

**Method.** The authors combine high-speed droplet tracking experiments with event-driven simulations to analyze particle mergers in one dimension.

**Model / system.** The system models droplets aggregating ballistically from a liquid jet breakup, which is treated as a non-equilibrium process where collisions carry directional memory. The analysis compares this physical system against theoretical frameworks like CPY scaling and Frachebourg's analytical solutions.

**Key observables.** Average particle mass growth ($\langle m angle$), number-weighted mass distribution function ($p(x)$), and correlation coefficients between neighboring particles (mass, velocity).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The characteristic length scale $\ell = 	au ar{v}^2/\sigma_v$, jet parameters ($D_{	ext{jet}}, v_{	ext{jet}}$), and the exponent $2/3$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Air drag is included as a perturbation, but the 1D ordering constraint protects the bulk scaling characteristics. Mean-field descriptions (like Smoluchowski) are shown to fail due to directional memory.

**Figures summary.** Figures show confirmation of the $2/3$ growth law via data collapse vs. rescaled coordinates, convergence of mass distributions to analytical solutions, and analysis of velocity correlations for simulations versus experiments.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical relevance of ballistic aggregation, presents experimental measurements on droplet trains, confirms scaling laws using a single length scale $\ell$, analyzes fine-grained properties like mass distribution tails, and discusses deviations caused by air drag.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ballistic aggregation is a canonical non-equilibrium process, relevant across scales from granular gases to planetary accretion. Collisions are driven by differences in velocities, building up persistent correlations between neighbors. Here, we provide the first experimental realization of one-dimensional ballistic aggregation in a train of droplets formed by the breakup of a liquid jet. Experiments and simulations confirm the analytically predicted scaling, with the global process shown to be governed by a single length scale. While air drag inverts the sign of neighbor velocity correlations, a 1D ordering constraint protects bulk characteristics of ballistic aggregation such as the scaling exponent and the shape of the large mass tail. More broadly, our results show that Smoluchowski-like mean-field descriptions fail when collisions carry directional memory -- as demonstrated here for jet-generated sprays.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a microscopic theory to analyze how the nonlocality of Landau levels affects electromagnetic interactions in 2DEG systems. By calculating observables like Purcell factors and Lamb shifts beyond local approximations, the authors demonstrate that spatial dispersion is crucial for accurately predicting light emission rates, especially for transitions forbidden under simple models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous quantum electrodynamics treatment of confined electron systems in strong magnetic fields, which is highly relevant for understanding light-matter interactions in nanoscale solid-state devices and plasmonics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to theoretically probe the nonlocality of Landau levels (LLs) within a two-dimensional electron gas (2DEG) when subjected to strong perpendicular magnetic fields.

**Main result.** Nonlocal effects significantly modify electromagnetic responses, leading to enhanced Purcell factors for dipole-forbidden transitions and revealing collective magnetoplasmon resonances absent in local approximations.

**Method.** The authors derived the nonlocal susceptibility tensor ($\chi$) using a Green's function formalism beyond standard approximations (Markov/RWA) and applied it to calculate observables like Purcell factors and Lamb shifts.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2DEG in GaAs quantum wells under a strong magnetic field, leading to quantized Landau levels. The theory models the response using a nonlocal susceptibility $\chi(q, \omega)$ derived from microscopic current density calculations.

**Key observables.** Purcell factor ($F_P$), environment-induced Lamb shift ($\Delta\omega$), and dipole emitted spectra $S(\omega)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field strength (a few Tesla), relevant length scales up to hundreds of nanometers, cyclotron frequency ($\omega_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Calculations are performed beyond the Markov and rotating-wave approximations; the theory relies on decomposing the susceptibility into longitudinal, transverse, and Hall components.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare local versus nonlocal predictions for Purcell factors and Lamb shifts across various frequencies ($\omega_c, 2\omega_c$), highlighting large deviations for forbidden transitions and showing spectral features like magnetoplasmon resonances.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by deriving the Green's function and the full nonlocal susceptibility tensor $\chi(q, \omega)$, then applies this formalism to calculate key optical responses (Purcell factors, Lamb shifts) for comparison against local approximations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a two-dimensional electron gas, a strong perpendicular magnetic field confines electrons to quantized cyclotron orbits, giving rise to Landau levels with discrete orbit radii. Even the smallest Landau orbit, set by the magnetic length, spans tens of nanometers for fields of a few Tesla, imposing an intrinsic nonlocal response to electromagnetic excitations. From a microscopic theory of the nonlocal susceptibility, we derive the Green's function, the central quantity governing all electromagnetic interactions, and evaluate Purcell factors, Lamb shifts, and emission spectra from a proximal dipole emitter beyond the Markov and rotating-wave approximations. Significant nonlocal effects resulting from spatial dispersion of the Landau level response modify the response for experimentally relevant situations up to distances of hundreds of nanometers and, in particular, brighten locally dipole-forbidden transitions due to near-field gradients at multiples of the cyclotron frequency. The relevant length scales are typical of state-of-the-art nanostructured terahertz architectures, and some of our nonlocal features are consistent with recent experiments using Landau level polaritons.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces the On-site dephased ensemble (DE) approximation to tackle strongly correlated Mott insulators. By averaging over local static solutions, DE efficiently reproduces key signatures of strong correlations seen in DMFT. This offers a powerful, computationally cheaper alternative for studying these challenging electronic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics, the concept of describing a complex quantum state as an ensemble average over multiple localized configurations shares conceptual links with open quantum system descriptions and environmental coupling in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is accurately solving the many-body electronic structure problem, especially for paramagnetic Mott insulators which defy standard methods like DFT.

**Main result.** The proposed On-site dephased ensemble (DE) approximation successfully recovers key features of strongly correlated Mott systems, matching DMFT results while offering a computationally efficient first-principles route.

**Method.** The DE approximates the local electronic problem by treating it as a thermal ensemble over all accessible local static solutions, bypassing expensive quantum Monte Carlo or large supercell calculations.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on paramagnetic Mott insulators, using models like NiO and FeO within a 5-orbital d-band framework governed by Hubbard interactions (U).

**Key observables.** Local electronic self-energy ($\Sigma(\omega)$), local spectral function ($A(\omega)$), and the persistence of local moments.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hubbard interaction strength (e.g., U = 6 eV) and temperature/inverse temperature ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The DE neglects inter-basin tunneling between static solutions, making it an approximation valid in the limit of strong Hubbard U.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the grand potential landscape of local states, the imaginary part of the self-energy, and the resulting spectral functions for both the ensemble average and individual symmetry-broken states.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the DE approximation by comparing it to DMFT and supercell methods. It then applies this method to paradigmatic Mott insulators (NiO, FeO), benchmarking its results against established DMFT calculations across various observables.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Addressing the many-body electronic-structure problem is a central goal of modern condensed-matter physics. Paramagnetic Mott insulators, in particular, have long represented a challenge for standard approaches, such as density-functional theory. Historically, these systems have been tackled either by considering many-body dynamics, as in the case of dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT), or, more recently, by invoking a polymorphous description consisting of large supercells populated with static symmetry-broken motifs whose spatial average restores the paramagnetic state. Inspired by these viewpoints, we introduce the on-site dephased ensemble (DE) approximation, in which the local electronic-structure problem is described by a thermal ensemble of all accessible local static solutions; this gives rise to a strong frequency dependence of the local electronic self-energy, as seen in DMFT or in the coherent-potential approximation of disordered alloys. We show that the DE successfully recovers defining hallmarks of strongly correlated Mott systems, both in terms of the self-energy as well as the persistence of local moments in time, in quantitative agreement with DMFT. By bypassing expensive quantum Monte Carlo solvers or large supercell calculations, this approach offers efficient routes to treating paramagnetic Mott systems in a first-principles setting, and highlights a deeper connection between the physics of strong correlations and that of disorder.

</details>

**Authors:** Ryan Thorngren  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.20605v1>

Veff defined by gauge averaging is local in the cone |V | &lt; CT (gray) and enjoys the local symmetry exactly. Here C is a geometric constant. If there is a local-symmetry-protected memory phase at V = 0 up to some Tc, then the memory persists to a fluctuation-stabilized phase in a wedge, as shown schematically in green. **Summary.** This work proves the perturbative stability of self-correcting memory in 3D Wegner gauge theory using a novel technique called 'gauge averaging.' By showing that fluctuations effectively restore broken symmetries, the authors demonstrate that the system's topological information is robust against small perturbations. This establishes a quantitative understanding of how thermal fluctuations stabilize complex quantum phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical techniques used—especially gauge averaging and bottleneck analysis—provide powerful tools for analyzing the stability of topological order in lattice models, which has parallels in understanding quantum error correction codes (like the toric code).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is proving that the self-correcting memory inherent in 3D Wegner gauge theory remains stable even when subjected to small, arbitrary perturbations of its Hamiltonian.

**Main result.** The authors prove that this self-correcting memory phase is robustly stable against weak perturbations, and furthermore, this robustness increases with temperature up to a critical point $T_c > 0$.

**Method.** The core technique is 'gauge averaging,' which effectively restores broken gauge symmetries by averaging the system's state over the symmetry group. Stability is rigorously established by bounding the bottleneck ratio of the Markov chain, leading to an exponentially long mixing time.

**Model / system.** The model is based on Wegner's 3D $\mathbb{Z}_2$ gauge theory, describing fluctuations on a lattice (like surfaces $S$). The system is analyzed using the Gibbs state derived from a perturbed Hamiltonian ($H_0 + V$), where $H_0$ relates to the boundary length of these fluctuating surfaces.

**Key observables.** Self-correcting memory (exponentially long mixing time), topological winding number, and the bottleneck ratio $\mu(A[C]) / \mu(\partial A[C])$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$ or $\beta$), perturbation strength ($|V|$), lattice size ($L$), and critical temperature ($T_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof assumes the symmetry group $G$ is finite abelian, that the perturbation $|V|$ is sufficiently weak relative to the temperature/parameters (e.g., $\beta|V|_{\eta} < C_{\text{gauge}}$), and relies on bounding sums over polymers using criteria like the KP criterion.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the phase diagram, showing that stable memory exists within a region defined by gauge averaging, bounded by regions where the mathematical method fails (the 'dragon').

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing bounds on the unperturbed system using Peierls arguments and bottleneck lemmas. It then introduces gauge averaging to handle perturbations, culminating in bounding the ratio of measures $\mu_G(\partial A[C]) / \mu_G(A[C])$ to prove exponential decay with system size $L$, thereby guaranteeing long mixing times.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that the self-correcting memory in 3d Wegner gauge theory is stable to arbitrary small enough perturbations of the Hamiltonian. Our proof relies on a new method we dub ``gauge averaging'', which gives conditions under which explicitly broken gauge symmetries are effectively restored by fluctuations. These conditions show that the self-correcting memory phase is fluctuation stabilized, with its robustness to perturbations increasing with increasing temperature, up to some $T_c > 0$.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes a geometric framework to unify spin relaxation theory by replacing separate $R_1$ and $R_2$ rates with a single covariant relaxation tensor ($\mathcal{R}_{\mu
u}$). By using noncommuting pulse sequences on hyperpolarized spins in diamond, the authors experimentally demonstrate that off-diagonal coupling terms exist, proving that relaxation is a directional process governed by this underlying tensor structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, coordinate-independent mathematical language for open quantum systems, unifying disparate descriptions (Bloch/Redfield/Lindblad) by treating relaxation as a tensor field on the state manifold. This has direct implications for understanding decoherence in solid-state qubits and quantum sensing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The conventional description of spin relaxation separates longitudinal ($R_1$) and transverse ($R_2$) rates phenomenologically, obscuring a deeper structural unity.

**Main result.** Relaxation is fundamentally governed by a single covariant tensor ($\mathcal{R}_{\mu
u}$), which can be experimentally accessed by using noncommuting pulse sequences that reveal off-diagonal coupling terms.

**Method.** The authors develop a geometric framework to describe dissipation in Liouville space, utilizing experimental measurements on spin systems subjected to noncommuting control pulses and measuring accumulated geometric phase (holonomy).

**Model / system.** The system is spin relaxation dynamics, modeled by the evolution of the density operator $\dot{ho} = -i/\hbar[\hat{H}(r), ho] + \mathcal{D}(ho)$. The framework unifies Bloch, Redfield, and Lindblad descriptions using a covariant relaxation tensor.

**Key observables.** Covariant relaxation tensor ($\mathcal{R}_{\mu
u}$), effective relaxation matrices ($R_{ij}^{	ext{eff}}$), geometric phase accumulation (holonomy).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noncommuting pulse sequences, transmitter frequency offset ($\Delta\omega$), hyperpolarized ${}^{13}	ext{C}$ spins in diamond with NV centers.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The conventional $R_1$/$R_2$ separation is an approximation that fails in driven or anisotropic regimes where the full tensorial nature must be considered.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how commuting generators yield smooth, single-axis contraction of spin trajectories, whereas noncommuting generators produce multi-lobed patterns due to ordering dependence (commutator terms).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the geometric framework by identifying the covariant relaxation tensor ($\mathcal{R}_{\mu
u}$), shows how this tensor is probed experimentally using noncommuting pulse sequences in diamond NV centers, and confirms that geometric phase accumulation provides an independent diagnostic of non-dissipative dynamics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin relaxation is conventionally described by two independent phenomenological rates - longitudinal ($R_1$) and transverse ($R_2$) - whose separation obscures a deeper structural unity. Here we develop a geometric framework in which dissipation is represented by a single covariant relaxation tensor acting in Liouville space, from which $R_1$ and $R_2$ emerge as complementary projections. This tensor structure is not merely formal but is experimentally accessible through pulse sequences that probe noncommuting directions in spin space. Using hyperpolarized $^{13}C$ spins in diamond with nitrogen-vacancy centers, we show that commuting pulse trains yield effective relaxation matrices that are approximately diagonal, while noncommuting sequences produce off-diagonal components that vary with transmitter frequency offset and pulse ordering, providing evidence that relaxation is a directional process governed by a tensor rather than a pair of scalar rates. Complementary measurements of geometric phase demonstrate that noncommuting dynamics introduce ordering-dependent effects that are separable from dissipation, consistent with the interpretation of relaxation as geometric transport on the state manifold. This framework unifies Bloch, Redfield, and Lindblad descriptions within a coordinate-independent formulation and provides a natural language for relaxation in driven, anisotropic, and non-equilibrium spin systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper resolves the third-particle paradox in Quantum Reference Frames by shifting focus from formal equality of reduced density operators to operational constraints on measurable probabilities. It argues that irrelevance must be defined relative to the set of actually accessible measurements, providing a robust method for defining subsystem structure across different quantum descriptions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to theoretical quantum physics dealing with open systems and subsystem decomposition, as it provides a rigorous framework for defining physical relevance when multiple reference frames or subsystems are involved.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper resolves the 'third-particle paradox' that arises when describing quantum systems across different Quantum Reference Frames (QRFs). The paradox concerns apparent inconsistencies in subsystem descriptions when a system deemed irrelevant in one frame appears relevant after transformation to another.

**Main result.** The paradox is resolved by arguing that physical claims of irrelevance must be interpreted operationally—based on the measurable probabilities of available observables—rather than through formal algebraic equalities of reduced density operators. The correct focus is on the restricted algebra generated by accessible measurements.

**Method.** The authors employ an operational approach, comparing predicted probabilities for a set of relevant observables ($\mathcal{M}_A$) across different QRF descriptions rather than equating the full reduced states directly.

**Model / system.** The theoretical framework involves quantum systems partitioned into multiple subsystems (e.g., A, B, C, D) undergoing transformations between Quantum Reference Frames (QRFs).

**Key observables.** Accessible observables ($\mathcal{M}_C$), reduced density operators ($ho(A)$), and the comparison of probabilities derived from these measurements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the accessible algebra $	ext{alg}(\dots)$, which dictates which degrees of freedom are physically measurable or relevant.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Physical claims regarding subsystem structure must be interpreted as statements about probabilities for a specified set of measurements, not formal equalities between density operators.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the paradox in QRFs by comparing two procedures for tracing out an irrelevant particle. It then develops the operational resolution by focusing on the restricted algebra generated by measurable observables, concluding that this operational constraint avoids the formal contradiction.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We give an operational resolution of the third-particle paradox, relevant in the theory of quantum reference frames. The apparent paradox is that a system which is irrelevant in one quantum-reference-frame description can seem to become relevant after changing to another quantum reference frame, because the reduced state obtained after transforming a larger system need not agree with the state obtained by first discarding the extra system and then transforming. We argue that this comparison is not operationally meaningful unless the observables are transformed together with the states, or equivalently, unless the subsystem that needs to be discarded is properly identified. If the third particle is irrelevant for all measurements actually available in the original frame, then the transformed measurements form a restricted algebra in the new frame for which the third particle remains irrelevant. The paradox therefore results from replacing an operational statement about probabilities by a stronger, representation-dependent statement about equality of reduced density operators. We close by relating the question of when degrees of freedom may be discarded to the observable-induced, operational approach to subsystem structure.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper calculates how quantum gravity effects, specifically graviton emission, cause which-path decoherence in matter-wave interferometry. By analyzing the vacuum and squeezed states of the gravitational field, the authors derive quantitative estimates for the decoherence exponent. The main conclusion is that this effect is far too small to be measured by current or foreseeable experimental setups.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly focused on AMO platforms like trapped ions or cold atoms, the methodology of calculating decoherence by tracing out environmental degrees of freedom (here, gravitons) is highly relevant to open quantum systems theory and quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates which-path decoherence experienced by matter waves passing through an interferometer due to coupling with quantum gravitational fields (gravitons). It quantifies this effect by tracing out the graviton degrees of freedom.

**Main result.** The calculated decoherence exponent ($\Gamma$) for both vacuum and inflationary squeezed states is found to be extremely small, concluding that graviton-induced decoherence is negligible in current matter-wave interferometers.

**Method.** The analysis uses quantum field theory techniques, deriving the reduced coherence factor by tracing out the gravitons from the system's evolution operator. This involves calculating vacuum expectation values and utilizing displacement operators for squeezed states.

**Model / system.** The model is a matter-wave interferometer (two semiclassical branches) coupled linearly to linearized quantum gravity fields (gravitons). The calculations are performed in Minkowski spacetime, considering both standard vacuum fluctuations and highly squeezed states relevant to inflation.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the decoherence exponent $\Gamma = -\ln |D|$, which quantifies the reduction in interference visibility. Other key observables include the coherence factor $D$ and the radiated graviton energy/number.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the gravitational constant ($G$), mass ($m$), separation distance ($d$), time scale ($	au$), and cosmological parameters related to inflation ($\Omega_{	ext{inf}}, H_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a conserved source stress tensor, restricts attention to propagating gravitons in Minkowski spacetime, and employs approximations derived from the low-energy effective field theory of gravity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic setup of the which-path configuration (Gaussian excursion) and show the dependence of decoherence on mass and kinematic scales. Tables provide order-of-magnitude estimates for $\Gamma$ across various experimental platforms.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from deriving the general formalism using displacement operators, calculating the vacuum case ($\Gamma_{	ext{vac}}$), extending the analysis to general squeezed states, and finally specializing to inflationary squeezing ($\Gamma_{	ext{inf}}$) to compare all contributions against experimental limits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive which-path decoherence for matter-wave interferometry that arises from tracing out the gravitons of linearized quantum gravity. Because the gravitons are driven linearly by the matter source, the branch-dependent evolution can be solved exactly, and the reduced coherence is given by the characteristic function of the initial graviton state, evaluated at the difference of the branch-induced field displacements. In vacuum, the decoherence exponent equals half the mean number of gravitons radiated by the difference source. For a smooth Gaussian trajectory, it reduces to $Γ_{\rm vac}=(8/15)\,Gm^2d^4/(\hbar c^5τ^4)$, ranging from $10^{-89}$ to $10^{-61}$ across representative matter-wave platforms. For a general squeezed graviton vacuum, we obtain an exact expression in which squeezing either suppresses or enhances the vacuum response, depending on the squeezing phase. For the strongly squeezed state produced by inflation, this expression reduces to $Γ_{\rm inf}=(π/20)\,Ω_{\rm inf}(md^2H_0/\hbar)^2$ and reaches at most $\sim10^{-27}$ for optimistic parameters. Hence, the which-path decoherence induced by radiative gravitons is therefore negligible in current matter-wave interferometers.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops provable classical and quantum algorithms to find the best approximation of a general fermionic state $ho$ using only a Slater determinant. By analyzing the optimization landscape, they identify a critical fidelity threshold at $2/3$. The results offer concrete computational tools for accurately characterizing correlated many-body systems beyond simple mean-field theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous algorithmic tools provided for approximating complex quantum states (like those from neural networks) are highly relevant for developing robust simulation techniques in AMO physics, especially when dealing with correlated electron systems where mean-field approximations fail.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is finding the Slater determinant that maximizes fidelity with a given, potentially complex, fermionic many-body state $ho$. This is motivated by improving upon mean-field approximations like Hartree-Fock.

**Main result.** The authors provide provable classical and quantum algorithms to find this closest Slater determinant within specified error bounds. They also establish that $F=2/3$ marks a critical transition point in the optimization landscape.

**Method.** The approach combines estimating the 1-RDM, restricting the search space to an 'active space' using natural orbitals, and then employing $\epsilon$-covering net techniques for exhaustive search over the restricted manifold of Slater determinants.

**Model / system.** The work is applied to fermionic many-body systems, specifically mentioning the Fermi-Hubbard model on lattices and the spinless t-V chain. The state $ho$ can originate from neural quantum states or other simulation methods.

**Key observables.** Fidelity $F = \langle S|ho|Sangle$, the 1-RDM ($\gamma_{ij}$), and the optimal fidelity $	ext{OPT}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Error tolerance $\epsilon$, number of fermions $n$, single-particle modes $m$, and the critical threshold value of $2/3$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The classical algorithm relies on restricting the search to an active space derived from the 1-RDM, which is assumed sufficient. The complexity proofs rely on standard complexity conjectures.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the algorithmic protocol (including RDM estimation and covering nets), show the non-convexity of the optimization landscape with a transition at $F=2/3$, and present results for the Fermi-Hubbard model.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the problem, then details the classical algorithm using 1-RDM estimation and active space reduction. It proves complexity bounds, analyzes the optimization landscape's non-convexity around $F=2/3$, and concludes with applications to models like Fermi-Hubbard.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Learning compact, interpretable descriptions of quantum many-body states is an important task in quantum science. We study the task of learning the Slater determinant with maximum fidelity to an arbitrary fermionic many-body state, with motivation from both Hartree-Fock methods and agnostic tomography. Given an $n$-fermion wavefunction built from $m$ fermionic modes, we provide classical and quantum algorithms returning a Slater determinant with fidelity within $\varepsilon$ of maximal in time $m^{\text{poly}(n,1/\varepsilon)}$. We prove matching hardness lower bounds, assuming standard complexity conjectures, along some parameter axes. Given access to quantum copies, we prove this can be accomplished with $\text{poly}(m,n,1/\varepsilon)$ copies of $ρ$. We also show that above a fidelity of $2/3$ any stationary point is the unique global maximum while below $2/3$ the optimization landscape can have spurious stationary points, and hence $2/3$ marks a transition point in the optimization landscape for this problem. We apply the algorithm to the Fermi-Hubbard model, extracting the closest Slater determinant from neural quantum state solutions. Together, our results provide algorithmic tools with provable guarantees in understanding fermionic many-body systems with classical or quantum simulation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides an exact analysis of a spin-pseudospin model mimicking cuprate chains, focusing on how geometric frustration leads to complex phase behavior. Using the transfer-matrix method, the authors identify pseudo-critical lines where thermodynamic anomalies occur due to competition between charge and spin degrees of freedom. The findings highlight that such geometry promotes short-range coherence and distinct quasi-phase transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on cuprates, the methodology of analyzing quasi-degeneracy in the transfer matrix spectrum to predict continuous but sharp thermodynamic anomalies (pseudo-transitions) is a powerful technique applicable to other low-dimensional quantum systems exhibiting competing interactions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates thermal pseudo-transitions occurring within a geometrically frustrated spin-pseudospin sawtooth chain, which models one-dimensional cuprates.

**Main result.** The system exhibits pronounced pseudo-transition behavior characterized by sharp but continuous peaks in specific heat and avoided-crossing scale at pseudo-critical temperatures. The geometry promotes frustration and short-range coherence.

**Method.** The analysis relies on the exact transfer-matrix method (TMM) to derive thermodynamic properties like free energy and entropy.

**Model / system.** The model is a spin–pseudospin X sawtooth chain incorporating three valence states of copper ions, capturing competition between magnetic exchange and electrostatic coupling in corner-sharing triangular units.

**Key observables.** Free energy, pseudo-critical line condition, specific heat, avoided-crossing scale, local correlation functions ($\langle S_i S_{i+1} angle$, $\langle \sigma_i \sigma_{i+1} angle$), and residual entropies of ground states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction ratios like $\Delta/J$, $V/J$, and $J_1/J$; temperature ($T$) relative to pseudo-critical temperatures ($T_p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Periodic boundary conditions are assumed for the chain length, and the transfer-matrix method is used for exact evaluation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spin-pseudospin sawtooth chain structure, the four possible ground states (AF, FR1, FR2, FR3), and temperature dependence of correlation functions across different parameter regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the model and Hamiltonian, then uses TMM to derive eigenvalues and the pseudo-critical condition. It analyzes the resulting ground state phases and finally examines finite-temperature behavior, showing how zero-temperature boundaries evolve into entropy ridges signaling pseudo-transitions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present an exact analysis of a spin--pseudospin X sawtooth chain that incorporates three distinct valence states of copper ions and serves as a minimal model for one-dimensional cuprates composed of corner-sharing copper triangles. The model includes magnetic exchange and electrostatic coupling constants both along the base sites and between base and apex sites capturing the competition between spin and charge degrees of freedom. Using the transfer-matrix method, we derive exact expressions for the free energy and obtain an analytical condition defining the pseudo-critical line associated with pronounced thermodynamic anomalies. The ground-state analysis reveals, besides an antiferromagnetic phase, three frustrated phases characterized by distinct residual entropies. At finite temperatures, these zero-temperature phase boundaries evolve into narrow entropy ridges signaling pseudo-transitions between the corresponding quasi-phases. The specific heat and the avoided-crossing scale exhibit sharp but continuous peaks at the pseudo-critical temperature, whereas the physical correlation length may be controlled by a different subleading eigenvalue. Local correlation functions uncover a cooperative rearrangement from charge-dominated to magnetically correlated regimes. Our results demonstrate that the sawtooth geometry promotes frustration and short-range coherence leading to pronounced pseudo-transition behavior.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports a high-precision, experimental determination of the reduced electric-dipole matrix element for $	ext{Ba}^+$ using trapped ions. By meticulously comparing scattering rates and Stark shifts, the authors significantly improve the calculated value for the static differential scalar polarizability. This advancement directly reduces systematic uncertainties in room-temperature optical clock error budgets.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes the boundaries of precision measurement in atomic physics. The techniques used—combining time-resolved population dynamics with Stark shifts—are central to quantum optics and developing next-generation atomic clocks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is a high-precision measurement of the reduced electric-dipole matrix element $\langle P_{1/2}\|r\|S_{1/2}angle$ in singly ionized barium ($	ext{Ba}^+$). This measurement aims to improve the accuracy of calculating the Blackbody Radiation (BBR) shift for optical clocks.

**Main result.** The authors determined the reduced dipole matrix element to be $3.3227(12)$ and extracted a static differential scalar polarizability $\Delta\alpha_0(0) = -73.09(12)$ a.u., significantly improving clock error budgets.

**Method.** The measurement combines off-resonant scattering rate measurements with dispersive Stark-shift measurements on the $S_{1/2} - P_{1/2}$ transition in $	ext{Ba}^+$. The ratio of these rates is used to extract the matrix element while suppressing systematic errors.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses trapped $	ext{Ba}^+$ ions, probing transitions between $S_{1/2}$, $P_{1/2}$, and other levels. The analysis relies on rate equations describing population dynamics under the Rotating Wave Approximation (RWA).

**Key observables.** Reduced dipole matrix element $\langle P_{1/2}\|r\|S_{1/2}angle$, scattering rates ($\gamma_s$), and Stark shifts ($\Delta s$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The measurement is highly sensitive to systematic effects like polarization imbalance, Zeeman shifts, and detuning imbalances; these are quantified and minimized.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the validity of rate equations derived under certain approximations (e.g., first order in small parameters) and carefully accounts for various systematic shifts by comparing measurements at different conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the level structure, timing sequences for measurements, and plots showing the measured matrix element versus detuning, confirming consistency across measurement settings.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the experimental setup using $	ext{Ba}^+$ ions, describes the methodology involving interleaved Stark-shift and scattering-rate measurements, presents the data analysis to extract the RME, and concludes by applying this result to improve BBR shift calculations for optical clocks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a high-precision measurement of the reduced electric-dipole matrix element $\langle P_{1/2}\|r\|S_{1/2}\rangle$ in $^{138}\mathrm{Ba}^+$. By comparing off-resonant scattering rates with dispersive Stark-shift measurements, we determine the matrix element to be $3.322\,7(12)$, corresponding to a $P_{1/2}$ excited-state radiative lifetime of $7.866\,3(56)$~ns. Combining our experimental results with a prior model for the differential scalar polarizability $Δα_0(ω)$, we extract the static value $Δα_0(0) = -73.09(12)$a.u. This determination directly improves the evaluation of the blackbody radiation (BBR) shift, minimizing a prominent systematic limitation in room-temperature $\mathrm{Ba}^+$ optical clock error budgets. These results provide a stringent benchmark for atomic-structure calculations and advance high-precision applications in quantum metrology and tests of fundamental physics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper extends the concept of 'link products'—a method for composing quantum processes—from finite-dimensional systems to continuous variables. It introduces a systematic framework using covariance matrices that allows researchers to model complex open quantum interactions like non-Markovian noise or adaptive measurements in CV platforms. This provides essential tools for designing and analyzing advanced quantum protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful mathematical toolset (the CV link product) that generalizes established concepts from discrete quantum information theory into the continuous variable domain, which is crucial for modern photonic quantum optics experiments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a systematic framework, analogous to link products, to analyze how quantum circuit fragments interact and compose specifically for continuous-variable (CV) systems.

**Main result.** This formalism extends existing tools to CVs, allowing the characterization of non-Markovian processes and adaptive interactions by efficiently calculating resulting covariance matrices in the Gaussian regime.

**Method.** The core method involves defining a Continuous-Variable Link Product ($\star_J$) applied to Choi states representing circuit fragments. In the Gaussian limit, this composition is calculated using iterative contractions on covariance matrices rather than full density operators.

**Model / system.** The system models quantum information tasks where the process is viewed as an 'open circuit fragment' (e.g., unknown noise source or subroutine). The mathematical tools are built around CV states characterized by quadrature expectation values and their associated covariance matrices ($\Gamma$).

**Key observables.** Covariance matrix ($\Gamma$), Choi state representation ($\Upsilon_C$), and the resulting total covariance matrix ($\Gamma_{	ext{tot}}$) after stitching.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Squeezing parameter ($r$), quadrature means ($\vec{d}$), and the limiting process variables ($s, r', s' 	o \infty$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The formalism relies on approximations in the limits of large separation distances ($r' 	o \infty$ and $r'' 	o \infty$) to simplify the complex link product calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the concept of circuit fragments vs. closed protocols; Figure 2 shows the Choi state representation for a fragment in finite dimensions, while later figures detail the matrix manipulations for stitching.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need for CV link products by drawing parallels to finite-dimensional systems. It then introduces the formalism using Gaussian states and covariance matrices, demonstrating its application to non-Markovianity and adaptive sensing through explicit calculation examples.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum circuits are often drawn as complete processes, with fixed inputs and outputs. In many quantum-information tasks, however, the natural object is only a fragment of such a circuit: an unknown source of non-Markovian noise to be probed, a subroutine to be inserted into a larger algorithm, or an agent implementing an adaptive strategy. In finite dimensions, the link product provides a systematic means to analyze how such circuit fragments interact and compose. Here, we develop the corresponding framework for continuous-variable systems. We introduce continuous-variable circuit fragments and associated link products that stitch such fragments together into larger processes. We show that the formalism simplifies substantially in the Gaussian regime, where link products can be evaluated efficiently using covariance-matrix representations. We use the formalism to construct non-Markovian processes and adaptive agent-environment interactions from modular components. This extends the quantum-comb toolkit to continuous variables, providing systematic methods for adaptive sensing, non-Markovian noise mitigation, and higher-order quantum circuit design.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces ARGON, a novel compilation framework designed to overcome the exponential complexity barrier in programming quantum circuits for neutral atom processors. By decoupling static spatial constraints from dynamic temporal scheduling using a GNN predictor, ARGON achieves massive speedups and significantly higher execution fidelity compared to current state-of-the-art methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a critical software tool that directly addresses the scaling limitations of physical quantum hardware platforms like neutral atoms by abstracting away intractable combinatorial search problems into manageable, predictive inference tasks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Compiling quantum circuits for neutral atom processors is bottlenecked by jointly optimizing dynamic atom transport scheduling and static spatial interference constraints, leading to an exponentially expanding search space.

**Main result.** ARGON achieves compilation in under 10 seconds, providing up to a $>10^4	imes$ speedup over state-of-the-art baselines while improving execution fidelity by up to $10^2	imes$.

**Method.** The framework uses a spatiotemporal decoupling paradigm: precomputing static layouts offline and employing a Graph Neural Network (GNN) to predict viable temporal routes, which are then synthesized via heuristic routing.

**Model / system.** The system is based on Neutral Atom (NA) quantum processors utilizing programmable optical tweezers for high qubit uniformity and flexible connectivity. Operations involve dynamic atom transport and entangling gates driven by the Rydberg blockade mechanism.

**Key observables.** Compilation Time, Physical Execution Fidelity, Average Speedup Factor.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit Number ($N$), Interaction Radius ($R_{int}$), Decoherence rates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption addressed is that the joint spatiotemporal coupling can be broken by separating static geometric conflict resolution from dynamic temporal routing.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares compilation time and fidelity degradation of existing compilers across varying qubit numbers; Figure 2 illustrates the complex spatiotemporal scheduling constraints involving gate conflict regions and AOD movement rules.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces ARGON by identifying the joint optimization bottleneck, proposing a three-stage solution (offline layout library $ightarrow$ GNN prediction $ightarrow$ heuristic routing), and validating its superior scalability and fidelity improvement over existing methods.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Neutral atom quantum systems offer a promising pathway to large-scale quantum computing due to high qubit uniformity and flexible connectivity. To exploit this architecture, compilers must coordinate dynamic atom transport alongside highly parallel entangling gates. As circuits scale, the interplay between these operations becomes a system bottleneck, introducing denser logical interactions and longer temporal dependencies. Compilers must simultaneously satisfy rigid spatial constraints and complex movement schedules. Existing joint spatiotemporal compilation methods face an exponentially expanding search space, incurring substantial overheads or compromising fidelity as circuit size grows.   In this work, we propose ARGON, a scalable compilation framework that introduces a spatiotemporal decoupling paradigm for neutral atom processors. Our key novelty is offloading static geometric conflict resolution to an offline phase, precomputing a library of hardware-certified, high-parallelism spatial layouts. To guide temporal routing, we deploy a Graph Neural Network (GNN) predictor to evaluate candidate layouts against deep temporal horizons, proactively evading downstream kinematic bottlenecks. Finally, a heuristic router translates the selected sequence into collision-free physical transport.   Evaluations show ARGON completes compilation in under 10 seconds, delivering up to a >10^4x and 600x average speedup over state-of-the-art baselines. ARGON also minimizes routing decoherence and reduces Rydberg stages, improving execution fidelity by up to 10^2x on dense circuits.

</details>

**Authors:** A. Sapkota, J. Schmidt, J. M. Wilde, L. -L. Wang, W. Tian, M. Matsuda, A. Kreyssig, S. L. Bud'ko, P. C. Canfield

**Summary.** This paper investigates the complex interplay between structural collapse and magnetism in $	ext{Sr}(	ext{Ni}_{1-x}	ext{Co}_x)_2	ext{P}_2$. Using neutron diffraction, the authors map out a magnetic phase diagram showing a transition from helical antiferromagnetism to ferromagnetism upon Co doping. The results highlight that the magnetic evolution requires theoretical frameworks beyond standard itinerant or local-moment models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides detailed experimental evidence on competing magnetic ground states in a strongly correlated material system, pushing beyond simple Heisenberg model predictions for itinerant magnetism.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to elucidate the detailed nature of magnetic ground states (Ferromagnetic vs. Helical Antiferromagnetic) and their evolution in doped $	ext{Sr}(	ext{Ni}_{1-x}	ext{Co}_x)_2	ext{P}_2$.

**Main result.** Neutron diffraction confirms that while doping $x=0.88$ and $x=0.94$ yield incommensurate helical AFM order, the composition $x=0.97$ exhibits a clear ferromagnetic ground state.

**Method.** The research combines single-crystal neutron diffraction measurements with Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations to probe magnetic structures.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the $	ext{A}(	ext{TM})_2	ext{X}_2$ compound series, specifically $	ext{Sr}(	ext{Ni}_{1-x}	ext{Co}_x)_2	ext{P}_2$, which features a unique 'one-third collapsed' structural phase. The magnetic behavior is analyzed across doping levels $x$.

**Key observables.** Magnetic ordering type (FM vs. AFM), incommensurate propagation vector $	au$, Néel temperatures ($T_N$), and Curie temperatures ($T_C$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping concentration $x$ (e.g., 0.88, 0.94, 0.97); temperature range; structural parameters like the $c/a$ ratio.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The evolution of magnetic states is subtle and does not appear to be fully captured by conventional itinerant or local-moment Heisenberg models.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the magnetic phase diagram, neutron diffraction data confirming incommensurate peaks for intermediate $x$, and comparisons between measured and calculated intensities using helical structure models.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the context of the unusual bonding motif, presents experimental results from neutron diffraction showing doping-dependent magnetic transitions (helical $ightarrow$ ferromagnetic), and concludes by discussing how these findings challenge existing theoretical models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

SrNi$_2$P$_2$ represents a unique case of a collapsed structural phase (one-third collapsed, where one out of every three P-P pairs forms a bond) in the A(TM)$_2$X$_2$ family of compounds (A = alkali metal, alkaline-earth metal, or rare earth; TM = transition metal; X = pnictogen). Furthermore, Co doping studies aimed at understanding the interrelationship between this unusual bonding motif and the resulting physical properties produced a magnetically rich phase diagram, specifically on the Co-rich side of the phase diagram. However, important questions remained regarding the detailed nature of the magnetic ground states. To address these issues, we performed single-crystal neutron diffraction measurements on Sr(Ni$_{1-x}$Co$_x$)$_2$P$_2$ with compositions $x = 0.88$, 0.94, and 0.97. For $x = 0.88$ and 0.94, the measurements reveal incommensurate helical magnetic order with a doping-dependent propagation vector $(0,0,τ)$, similar to that observed in Sr(Ni$_{1-x}$Co$_x$)$_2$As$_2$. In contrast, the $x = 0.97$ composition shows clear signatures of a ferromagnetically ordered ground state, resolving the earlier ambiguity regarding the nature of the low-temperature phase. Furthermore, our results highlight the subtle balance between these competing ground states, whose evolution does not appear to be fully captured by the conventional frameworks of either itinerant or local-moment Heisenberg models typically applied to related 122 systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel, SDP-free security framework for discrete-modulated Continuous-Variable Quantum Key Distribution. By grounding the analysis in the Heisenberg uncertainty principle and utilizing source-mode expansion, the authors successfully quantify key rates for high-order constellations. The work is validated both theoretically and experimentally on photonic platforms, demonstrating strong noise resilience.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses practical limitations in quantum communication protocols by providing computationally tractable security bounds for real-world hardware constraints (discrete modulation), which is crucial for realizing scalable quantum networks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a secure key rate analysis framework for discrete-modulated Continuous-Variable Quantum Key Distribution (CV-QKD) that avoids computationally prohibitive Semidefinite Programming (SDP).

**Main result.** The authors developed a security framework based on the Heisenberg uncertainty principle, enabling high-order constellation analysis and showing performance approaching ideal Gaussian limits with 256 points.

**Method.** The method involves developing an entanglement-based security bound by introducing ancillary modes and employing symmetrization techniques to simplify the covariance matrix constraints, thereby obviating SDP.

**Model / system.** The system is CV-QKD using discrete modulation, modeled via a multi-mode entangled source ($ho_{ACB}$) characterized by its covariance matrix. The analysis extends from theoretical bounds to experimental validation on both discrete and integrated photonic platforms.

**Key observables.** Secret Key Rate (SKR), Covariance matrices ($\gamma_{ACB}$), Normalized SKR ($	ext{SNorm}_{BE}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Constellation size (up to 256 points), excess noise ($	ext{SNU}$), channel transmittance ($T$), and the degree of modulation order.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The security analysis relies on exploiting the convexity of the Secret Key Rate (SKR) function, and experimental results assume successful implementation of advanced Digital Signal Processing (DSP).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the prepare-and-measure vs. entanglement-based schemes, show the three steps of covariance matrix symmetrization, compare SKR for different modulation orders (e.g., 256-QAM), and detail the experimental setup using chip-based photonics.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the SDP limitation in CV-QKD security proofs to proposing a Heisenberg uncertainty principle-based framework. This is followed by detailed mathematical derivations involving multi-mode state symmetrization, culminating in both theoretical analysis and experimental validation across various modulation formats.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Continuous-variable quantum key distribution is a compelling framework for scalable quantum networks due to its seamless integration with existing optical communication infrastructure. However, a fundamental gap persists between theoretical protocols requiring ideal Gaussian modulation and the constrained, discrete-modulated signals dictated by practical high-speed hardware. Current security proofs for discrete modulation rely on semidefinite programming, which suffers from prohibitive computational overhead for high-order constellations and lacks direct physical insight into non-Gaussian modulation.In this Letter, we overcome this limitation by developing a security framework that obviates semidefinite programming in favor of an approach grounded fundamentally in the Heisenberg uncertainty principle. By introducing a multi-mode entanglement-source model to characterize non-Gaussian state preparation, we establish an explicit mapping between constellation geometry and the secret key rate. This framework effectively quantifies the security implications of hardware-limited, finite state preparation, enabling both numerical and analytical security analysis under high-order constellations. We experimentally validate our method on both discrete-component and integrated photonic platforms, demonstrating that a quadrature amplitude modulation format with 256 constellation points can asymptotically approach the Gaussian capacity limit. Beyond quantum key distribution, the principle of tightening uncertainty-constrained bounds via source-mode expansion offers a paradigm for exploring the information-theoretic properties of complex non-Gaussian systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes a chiral spin chain undergoing a phase transition driven by a three-site interaction. The authors establish that the critical point is characterized by an unusual Lifshitz transition with z=3 scaling. They derive a complex boson field theory and confirm the coexistence of magnon and Luttinger liquid excitations in the resulting ordered chiral phase.

**Why it may be interesting.** The combination of Luttinger liquid physics with a distinct ferromagnetic magnon mode at criticality is highly relevant for understanding quantum phase transitions in low-dimensional magnetic materials, connecting concepts from condensed matter theory to integrable models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the zero-temperature properties and quantum critical behavior of a spin-1/2 Heisenberg chain perturbed by a three-site chiral term.

**Main result.** The transition point exhibits an unusual Lifshitz transition with a dynamical critical exponent z=3, and the resulting chiral phase features coexisting magnon modes alongside Luttinger liquid excitations.

**Method.** The authors combine analytical field theory (chiral bosonization) with advanced computational techniques like Matrix Product States and Thermodynamic Bethe Ansatz calculations.

**Model / system.** The system is a spin-1/2 Heisenberg chain Hamiltonian modified by a three-site chiral term, $\mathcal{H} = \sum_{n} [S_n \cdot S_{n+1} - g S_n \cdot S_{n+1} 	imes S_{n+2}]$. The physics is analyzed across regimes defined by the coupling constant $g$ relative to a critical value $g_c=2/\pi$.

**Key observables.** Dynamical structure factor $S(q, \omega)$, spontaneous magnetization ($m$), and vector spin chirality ($\chi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The chiral coupling strength $g$, the critical point $g_c = 2/\pi$, and the dynamical critical exponent $z=3$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximations include neglecting certain irrelevant interactions (like backscattering) and using finite truncations in continuum bosonization methods.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dynamical structure factor peak dispersion $\omega(q)$ at the critical point, and plots show the magnetization $m$ and chirality $\kappa_z$ exhibiting predicted asymptotic scaling near $g_c$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the model and identifying the phase transition to deriving a strongly interacting chiral boson field theory for the critical point. It then uses advanced numerical methods (MPS, Lanczos) and analytical tools (TBA) to calculate key observables like the structure factor and magnetization in both the ordered and critical regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the zero-temperature properties of a spin-$1/2$ Heisenberg chain with an additional three-site chiral term of strength $g$. The model is known to be integrable and previous work using Bethe ansatz has identified a phase transition from the usual critical state for $g< g_c = 2/π$ to a chiral phase for $g>g_c$. Here we combine analytical and computational approaches to demonstrate that the transition point comprises an unusual Lifshitz transition with dynamical critical exponent $z=3$, and the chiral phase is a partially spin-polarized phase which spontaneously breaks SU(2) symmetry. We derive a strongly interacting chiral boson field theory for the critical point, and show that even its small-momentum, low-energy response is non-trivial. We also determine the universal properties of the chiral phase, and specifically show that it has a simultaneous quadratically dispersing ferromagnetic "magnon" mode coexisting with the power-law singularities and linearly dispersing excitations typical of a Luttinger liquid. Our conclusions are validated using numerics based on matrix product state methods and exact diagonalization.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates how interlayer forces control the electronic state of Charge-Density Waves (CDWs) in $4	ext{H}_{\mathrm{b}}	ext{-TaS}_2$. By using STM, the authors demonstrated that the CDW wave vector elastically responds to the stacking registry between adjacent layers. This establishes electronic elasticity as a general mechanism by which weak interlayer interactions dictate correlated electronic properties in van der Waals heterostructures.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics, the concept of an order parameter's elasticity being controlled by weak coupling is analogous to how external fields or interactions can tune quantum phases in superconducting or correlated systems relevant to AMO research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The intrinsic variability of incommensurate Charge-Density Wave (CDW) wave vectors in layered quantum materials obscures their fundamental electronic properties.

**Main result.** Weak interlayer interactions can reshape the low-energy electronic structure by controlling the CDW ordering wave vector through the inherent electronic elasticity of the CDW state.

**Method.** Low-temperature Scanning Tunneling Microscopy (STM) combined with Fourier and quasiparticle-interference analysis was used to probe distinct elastic states in a model heterostructure.

**Model / system.** The study uses the $4	ext{H}_{\mathrm{b}}	ext{-TaS}_2$ heterostructure, modeling alternating commensurate CDWs on 1T layers and incommensurate CDWs on 1H layers to investigate interlayer coupling effects.

**Key observables.** CDW ordering wave vectors ($\mathbf{q}$), shifts in unoccupied flat bands (few-meV shifts), and moiré patterns observed via STM/FT analysis.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Compressive ($\approx -2.3\%$) and tensile ($\approx +3.2\%$) elastic strain states induced by interlayer registry.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The CDW wave vector is treated as an intrinsic elastic degree of freedom responsive to external/interlayer strain fields.

**Figures summary.** Figures show STM images, Fourier transforms revealing distinct CDW peaks based on termination (D1 vs D2), and QPI maps demonstrating energy shifts corresponding to different strain states.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares CDW patterns across different stacking registries (1H-term vs 1T-terminated regions) using STM/FT, linking the observed structural variations to measurable electronic structure changes via flat band shifts.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Incommensurate charge-density waves (CDWs) in layered quantum materials frequently exhibit widely varying ordering wave vectors, even among nominally identical samples, obscuring their intrinsic electronic properties. Here we identify an inherent origin of this variability through the electronic elasticity of an incommensurate CDW using the model heterostructure 4H$_{\mathrm{b}}$-TaS$_2$, composed of alternating commensurate CDW on 1T and incommensurate CDW on 1H layers. Low-temperature scanning tunneling microscopy, combined with Fourier and quasiparticle-interference analysis, exploits the lattice-pinned 1T CDW as an internal reference to resolve discrete compressive ($-2.3\%$) and tensile ($+3.2\%$) elastic states of the neighboring 1H CDW selected by the interlayer registry of the adjacent layers. Corresponding few-meV shifts of a flat band demonstrate that weak interlayer interactions reshape the low-energy electronic structure through the intrinsic elasticity of the incommensurate CDW. These findings establish electronic elasticity as a mechanism by which subtle interlayer interactions control correlated electronic states in van der Waals heterostructures.

</details>
