**Summary.** This work characterizes the Al-doped Ni2MnSn Heusler alloy, revealing strong coupling between its crystal structure and magnetic properties. Key findings include multiple phase transitions (paramagnetic-to-ferromagnetic and martensitic) and evidence of a complex reentrant cluster-glass state due to competing magnetic interactions. The research demonstrates potential for magnetocaloric applications based on these coupled transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of reentrant cluster-glass states and magnetostructural coupling in Heusler alloys provides a rich experimental platform for understanding how structural disorder influences complex magnetic ground states, relevant to frustrated magnetism models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the complex magnetic ground state, magnetocaloric effect, and multiple magnetic phases in Al-doped Ni2MnSn Heusler alloys.

**Main result.** The alloy exhibits strong magnetostructural coupling, evidence of competing ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic interactions leading to a reentrant cluster-glass state, and measurable magnetocaloric response near phase transitions.

**Method.** Measurements include temperature-dependent magnetization (ZFC/FC), Differential Scanning Calorimetry (DSC), X-ray Photoemission Spectroscopy (XPS), and calculation of magnetic entropy change ($\Delta S_M$).

**Model / system.** The physical system is the intermetallic Heusler alloy Ni2MnSn0.75Al0.25, which undergoes structural changes (martensitic transformation) coupled with complex magnetic ordering.

**Key observables.** Curie temperature ($T_C \approx 734	ext{ K}$), martensitic transition temperature ($\sim 263	ext{ K}$), magnetocaloric entropy change ($\Delta S_M$), and evidence of exchange bias.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Weiss temperature ($	heta_{	ext{CW}}=746.4	ext{ K}$), effective magnetic moment ($6.82\ \mu_B$), and field exponents ($n=0.784$ to $1.07$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the observed phenomena are governed by competing FM/AFM interactions modified by Al substitution and atomic disorder.

**Figures summary.** Figures show XRD patterns confirming crystal structure, SEM/EDX images verifying composition, and magnetization curves illustrating magnetic transitions and hysteresis loops.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically characterizes the material using structural (XRD, SEM), electronic (XPS), and thermodynamic/magnetic measurements (Magnetization, DSC, $\Delta S_M$ calculation) to map out multiple coupled phase transitions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Among Heusler compounds,Ni based alloys have been extensively investigated because they exhibit desirable properties such as high Curie temperatures, which are advantageous for advanced magnetic and spintronic devices.The effect of Al substitution on the magnetic ground state of Ni2MnSn was investigated using the Ni2MnSn0.75Al0.25 Heusler alloy.Temperature-dependent magnetisation measurements identify a second-order paramagnetic to ferromagnetic transition at TC is 734K,followed by a first-order martensitic transformation near 263K,demonstrating strong magnetostructural coupling.Curie Weiss analysis yields a positive Weiss temperature theta CW is 746.4K and an effective magnetic moment of 6.82muB,confirming the predominance of ferromagnetic exchange interactions. The bifurcation between the ZFC and FCW magnetization curves,together with non saturating hysteretic M vs H loops, indicates the coexistence of competing ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic interactions.Further magnetic investigations establish the formation of an interacting reentrant cluster glass state accompanied by an exchange-bias effect.The observed magnetic behavior is attributed to the modification of Mn Mn exchange interactions induced by Al substitution and the associated atomic disorder,resulting in a complex magnetic ground state.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops advanced theoretical protocols to self-test the three-qubit CCZ hypergraph state in a device-independent manner. It shows that standard methods fail due to non-Pauli stabilizers, requiring the use of specific correlator equalities and constructing novel Bell inequalities for verification. The results clarify the distinction between determining a state from correlation constraints versus finding it via maximal quantum violation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools (SOS proofs) to verify quantum states and measurement settings without trusting the physical device's perfect operation, which is crucial for scalable quantum technologies.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors aim to develop device-independent self-tests for the three-qubit CCZ hypergraph state, addressing limitations of standard graph-state testing methods.

**Main result.** They provide two explicit device-independent self-tests for the CCZ state, demonstrating that determining a state from correlator equalities is distinct from identifying it via maximal Bell violation.

**Method.** The work combines analyzing twenty specific correlators derived from five measurement contexts with constructing and proving bounds on an explicit Bell inequality using Sum-of-Squares (SOS) decomposition over algebraic number fields.

**Model / system.** The system is the three-qubit CCZ hypergraph state, which serves as a resource for quantum information processing. The analysis focuses on its non-Pauli stabilizer structure and its behavior under local measurements.

**Key observables.** Correlators (input–output probabilities), Bell inequality violations, and generalized stabilizers ($K_A$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The state is defined by the cubic phase polynomial; the test relies on comparing nonlocal correlations to local bounds (e.g., 11 vs 9).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Standard graph-state self-testing arguments fail because the stabilizers are not standard Pauli strings, necessitating alternative mathematical tools.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference figures showing branch cube propagation and equations defining correlations (e.g., Table 1 listing measurement contexts).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing that correlator equalities fix the state to showing why maximal Bell violation fails for canonical measurements, culminating in the construction of a successful Bell inequality test.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The three-qubit CCZ state is the smallest rank-three hypergraph state and an elementary entangled magic resource. Its cubic phase is governed by generalized stabilizers that are not Pauli strings, so standard graph-state self-testing arguments do not apply directly. We show that twenty correlators, all obtainable from five of the eight global input triples in the tripartite two-input, two-output scenario, determine this state and the action of the Pauli $X/Z$ measurements up to local isometries. The proof fixes eight equally weighted computational branches and propagates conditional $X$-flip relations across the branch cube, recovering the minus sign of the $111$ amplitude. These five-context correlations are nonlocal, but the canonical Pauli measurements cannot attain the largest quantum value of any Bell inequality that they violate: whenever they maximize a Bell expression, its local bound has the same value. Introducing an independent third measurement makes self-testing from maximal Bell violation possible. We construct an explicit Bell inequality whose maximal quantum violation self-tests the CCZ state and all three local measurements. An exact sum-of-squares decomposition proves the quantum bound, and its equality conditions yield an analytic SWAP extraction. Together, these results give two explicit device-independent self-tests of the CCZ state and demonstrate that determining a state and its measurements from several correlator equalities is distinct from identifying them through the maximal violation of a single Bell inequality.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper studies flavor current correlators in dense, strongly-coupled matter using holographic methods. It derives and verifies an extended hydrodynamic approximation that extends the known validity range of standard hydrodynamics. The findings are crucial for accurately modeling transport properties in cold, highly asymmetric quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work extends hydrodynamic descriptions into regimes previously thought inaccessible, providing theoretical tools for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in extreme astrophysical environments like neutron stars, which is a key area of modern statistical physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates flavor-current correlators in strongly-coupled, dense matter under conditions of finite quark chemical potential ($\mu_q$) and isospin asymmetry ($\mu_3$). The goal is to extend the applicability of hydrodynamic descriptions into non-standard regimes.

**Main result.** The authors show that the standard hydrodynamics validity extends to an extended regime ($T \ll \omega, k \ll \mu$), where correlators match non-Abelian hydrodynamic predictions. They propose a new approximation using the holographic product formula to capture both hydrodynamic and $	ext{AdS}_2$ pole effects.

**Method.** The research employs holographic methods (holographic duality) to compute two-point correlators of charged currents in the bulk, deriving non-Abelian hydrodynamics for the boundary theory. Results are verified through numerical analysis of exact correlators and quasi-normal mode spectra.

**Model / system.** The system is strongly-coupled dense matter (relevant for neutron stars), modeled holographically using a five-dimensional Einstein-Yang-Mills theory. The physics involves charged currents governed by non-Abelian dynamics due to the presence of an isospin chemical potential ($\mu_3$).

**Key observables.** Two-point correlators of charged currents, quasi-normal mode (QNMs) spectrum.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Quark chemical potential ($\mu_q$), isospin asymmetry ($\mu_3$), temperature ($T$), and wave vector/frequency ($\omega, k$). The analysis focuses on the near-extremal regime where $\omega, k, T \ll \mu$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumptions involve using holographic duality to model strongly coupled matter and assuming validity in specific low-energy regimes (near-extremal/extended hydrodynamic).

**Paper structure.** The paper first derives the non-Abelian hydrodynamic description for charged currents. It then computes the two-point correlators holographically at finite chemical potentials, demonstrating agreement with hydrodynamics in the near-extremal regime. Finally, it applies the holographic product formula to propose an extended approximation and verifies results numerically.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Flavor-current correlators are studied in strongly-coupled dense (holographic) matter, at finite quark chemical potential $μ_q$ and finite isospin asymmetry. The non-Abelian hydrodynamic description of the charged currents is derived in the presence of an isospin chemical potential $μ_3$. The two-point correlators of charged currents are then computed holographically at finite quark and isospin chemical potentials. In the near-extremal hydrodynamic regime, $ω, k, T, μ_3 \ll μ\equiv \sqrt{μ_q^2+μ_3^2}$, relevant for cold strongly coupled matter, the IR properties of the correlators are studied. It is shown that in this regime, the correlators agree with the non-Abelian hydrodynamic predictions. Therefore, the traditional regime of validity of standard hydrodynamics extends beyond $ω, k \ll T \ll μ$ to the so-called extended hydrodynamic regime $T\ll ω, k \ll μ$. The holographic product formula is applied to the present non-Abelian system, and is used to propose an extended hydrodynamic approximation capturing both hydrodynamic-like poles and the leading effect of AdS$_2$ poles, by resumming the low-$ω$ logarithms. The results are verified through a detailed numerical analysis of the exact correlators and quasi-normal mode spectrum.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces QST-Flow, a novel framework using normalizing flows for continuous-variable quantum state tomography. It overcomes the computational bottleneck associated with resolving high-dimensional, non-Gaussian phase-space structures by representing quasiprobability distributions as trainable neural densities. This allows for scalable and measurement-efficient reconstruction of complex bosonic states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical limitations of characterizing nonclassical quantum states in continuous variable systems, which is a major topic in quantum optics and quantum information science.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Continuous-variable quantum state tomography (QST) is computationally limited by resolving non-Gaussian structure in high-dimensional phase space.

**Main result.** The QST-Flow framework provides a scalable, measurement-efficient method to reconstruct CV quantum states by modeling their phase-space quasiprobability distributions using normalizing flows.

**Method.** It employs flow-based generative models (QST-Flow) to represent the Husimi Q function (QST-QFlow) or the Wigner function (QST-WFlow) as trainable neural densities, avoiding fixed grid limitations.

**Model / system.** The focus is on continuous-variable bosonic systems interrogated via phase-space quasiprobability distributions. The method handles both positive functions (Husimi Q) and sign-changing functions (Wigner).

**Key observables.** Husimi Q function, Wigner function, reconstructed quantum state density matrix.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality of the phase space ($2M$ for $M$ modes), which dictates computational scaling.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that experimental measurements can provide sufficient samples to train the flow model, and it uses specific mathematical decompositions (e.g., for Wigner function) to maintain normalization.

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate single-mode reconstructions of various non-Gaussian states (Cat, Fock, etc.) using both Q and W representations, comparing performance against prior ML methods on noisy data.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitations of traditional tomography, proposes the unified flow-based framework (QST-Flow), details the specific models for Q and W functions, derives the necessary loss functions (KL divergence/L1 loss), and validates the method across multiple state types and modes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Continuous-variable quantum state tomography is limited by the cost of resolving non-Gaussian structure in high-dimensional phase space. We introduce QST-Flow, a quantum state tomography framework via flow-based generative modeling that represents experimentally accessible phase-space quasiprobability distributions with normalized, samplable neural densities rather than a truncated density matrix. The framework has two variants: QST-QFlow models the positive Husimi-$Q$ function with a single normalizing flow, while QST-WFlow models sign-changing Wigner functions as a trainable difference of two normalized flows. This construction preserves quasiprobability normalization and enables exact density evaluation, direct sampling, and importance-sampled learning from finite phase-space measurements without a fixed grid. Benchmarks on non-Gaussian cat, binomial, Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill, number, and Fock states show accurate single-mode reconstructions, extension to multimode states, robustness on noisy Wigner data, and improved reconstruction error compared with prior machine-learning tomography methods. QST-Flow opens a promising route toward scalable, measurement-efficient phase-space tomography of nonclassical bosonic systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the scalability bottleneck in large quantum data centers where long communication paths degrade entanglement fidelity. The authors propose the Hop-Independent Quantum Data Center (HI-QDC), an isometric modular architecture. By leveraging path diversity and end-to-end purification, HI-QDC ensures that network performance remains at the quality level of a single elementary link, regardless of how many modules are chained together.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on network topology rather than fundamental AMO physics, the concept of using topological redundancy (path diversity) as a resource input for quantum error correction/purification is highly relevant to building scalable quantum hardware platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving 'hop-independent fidelity' in large quantum data centers, where increasing physical path length degrades end-to-end entanglement quality.

**Main result.** The proposed HI-QDC architecture allows modular units to convert their internal path diversity into an effective link-level resource, enabling scalable networks whose performance is independent of the module's internal hop count.

**Method.** The authors analyze end-to-end entanglement purification protocols (comparing 2-to-1 vs. nested r-to-1) and apply these requirements to a probabilistic BCube network model.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a server-centric quantum data center network utilizing the BCube topology for interconnecting Quantum Processing Units (QPUs). Entanglement quality is quantified using the Werner state parameter.

**Key observables.** Entanglement fidelity, end-to-end entanglement distribution rate, and the minimum number of raw Werner-state copies required for purification.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Path length ($\ell$), target elementary-link Werner parameter ($w^*$), and the resource cost (number of input copies $n_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes probabilistic generation of elementary links, deterministic entanglement swapping, and that fidelity degradation is solely due to link imperfections rather than memory decoherence.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the modular HI-QDC architecture, showing how path diversity supplies raw copies for purification. A key figure compares the exponentially increasing required input copies ($n_0$) versus path length ($\ell$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the fidelity bottleneck in scaling quantum data centers using a black-box model. It then analyzes resource requirements via end-to-end purification protocols, leading to the proposal of the HI-QDC module which resolves the scalability issue by converting path multiplicity into fidelity recovery.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Server-centric quantum data-center architectures offer scalability by distributing communication tasks across QPUs rather than concentrating complexity in a centralized switching core. However, scaling such architectures increases the path length, the number of Bell-state measurements, and the loss of end-to-end fidelity. We ask whether the path diversity of a server-centric topology can be converted into a mechanism for preserving not only rate, but also fidelity. We study this through end-to-end purification as a fidelity-restoration mechanism. First, in a black-box model, we determine the minimum number of raw end-to-end Werner-state copies, each carrying the degraded Werner parameter of a distance-ell path, that purification must consume to recover a single copy matching an elementary link, comparing recursive 2-to-1 and optimized nested r-to-1 purification. Second, we instantiate these requirements in a probabilistic BCube architecture, whose edge-disjoint path diversity supplies the raw copies. Because purification imposes a lower bound on input fidelity, no path redundancy can raise the output below this threshold, which limits scaling. To address it we present the Hop-Independent Quantum Data Center (HI-QDC), an isometric, modular, scalable architecture in which purification transforms end-to-end entanglement across a module into an effective link-level resource for the inter-module topology. Our results identify the regimes in which a BCube module, under end-to-end purification, preserves both the fidelity and the yield of an elementary link. The module then hides its internal hop count and acts as an effective elementary link for a higher-level network. Thus topology supplies the path multiplicity purification requires, while purification converts it into fidelity recovery, enabling recursively scalable quantum data-center networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates pseudogap formation in a layered attractive Hubbard model using the Fluctuating Local Field (FLF) method. The authors demonstrate that this non-perturbative approach captures a persistent pseudogap structure in the density of states, which remains visible even when mean-field theory predicts the superconducting gap has closed due to thermal fluctuations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The non-perturbative treatment of superconducting fluctuations via the FLF method provides advanced theoretical insight into spectral weight renormalization and gap physics, which is highly relevant for understanding strongly correlated materials like cuprates.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the formation of a pseudogap phase in a layered attractive Hubbard model, aiming to understand its origin relative to superconducting fluctuations.

**Main result.** The Fluctuating Local Field (FLF) method successfully demonstrates the emergence of a persistent pseudogap structure in the density of states, which persists even when mean-field theory predicts the gap should close above $T_c$.

**Method.** The primary theoretical tool is the Fluctuating Local Field (FLF) method, applied within a cluster scheme to calculate Green's functions and self-energies non-perturbatively.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is the layered attractive Hubbard model at quarter filling. The Hamiltonian includes hopping terms ($t$ intralayer, $t_2$ interlayer) and an interaction term, analyzed using a cluster approach extended to the infinite lattice via a static symmetry-breaking field $\Delta$.

**Key observables.** Green's function (GF), self-energy ($\Sigma$), Density of States (DOS), and the superconducting gap structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Moderate correlation strength ($|U|/t \approx 1$ to $2$) and weak interlayer coupling ($t_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the FLF method, which uses a static approximation for the fluctuating field ($\Delta$), and in some contexts, assumes $\Sigma' \approx 0$ when treating zero modes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the layered hopping structure; DOS plots compare mean-field vs. FLF results showing pseudogap persistence above $T_c$; Self-energy plots show agreement below $T_c$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the model and applying the FLF method to finite clusters, then extrapolating these results using a static $\Delta$ approximation to describe the infinite layered system, culminating in comparisons of DOS and self-energies across different temperature regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider the layered attractive Hubbard model with a moderate interaction strength at quarter filling. The Green's function, self-energy, and density of states are calculated for relatively large clusters using the fluctuating local field method within the approximation of vanishing effective interaction. The emergence of a pseudogap is demonstrated for the cluster system, and it is shown that integration over the fluctuating field is equivalent to the summation of zero-mode ladder diagrams above critical temperature. For the large layered system, a pseudogap in the density of states is obtained within a cluster scheme that treats the coupling between layers as a static $U(1)$ symmetry-breaking field.

</details>

**Authors:** Allen Zang, Bikun Li, Xinan Chen, Eric Chitambar, Liang Jiang, Martin Suchara, Tian Zhong  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21555v1>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an 'accumulating and shuffling' strategy that enhances entanglement purification protocols when multiple noisy entangled sources are available but their identities are unknown. By accumulating states in buffer memories and using shared randomness to mix them before purification, the protocol significantly boosts fidelity and success probability compared to standard methods. This offers a practical theoretical tool for building robust quantum networks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly impacts quantum network theory by providing a resource-efficient technique (using only classical randomness and memory) to boost entanglement quality, which is crucial for scaling up distributed quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research addresses how to improve entanglement fidelity for fault-tolerant quantum computation when input entangled pairs are noisy, heterogeneous, and the source labels are unknown at the purification stage.

**Main result.** The proposed 'accumulating and shuffling' strategy significantly enhances both the expected success probability and output Bell fidelity compared to baseline methods, with improvements increasing monotonically with the number of accumulation rounds.

**Method.** The authors develop a theoretical framework comparing the performance of standard entanglement purification protocols against those utilizing shared classical randomness to shuffle accumulated entangled states before inputting them into the protocol.

**Model / system.** The model involves quantum networks distributing noisy bipartite Werner states ($ho_i$) from multiple sources ($n$). The core mechanism utilizes buffer quantum memories and relies on the availability of classical shared randomness.

**Key observables.** Expected success probability ($p_{succ}$) and success-weighted output Bell fidelity ($F_{succ}$ or $	ilde{F}_{succ}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of sources ($n$), number of accumulation rounds ($m$), decoherence rate parameter ($r$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol requires classical shared randomness and the ability to store states in buffer quantum memories. Simplifications are made assuming mutual independence of random source outputs for certain derivations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic comparisons between non-AS and AS scenarios, and later figures compare performance metrics ($F_{succ}$) across different numbers of accumulated rounds ($m=2$ vs $m=3$) relative to decoherence rates ($r$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general problem setup (heterogeneous sources) to introducing the AS strategy. It provides rigorous mathematical proofs comparing performance metrics using tools like elementary symmetric polynomials and analyzing asymptotic limits, concluding with specific analyses involving decoherence channels.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement purification protocols (EPPs) are essential for improving entanglement fidelity to support fault-tolerant distributed quantum information processing. Practical entanglement sources are often heterogeneous and source labels may be unavailable at the EPP layer. We show that classical shared randomness, together with buffer memories, can enhance entanglement purification when source labels are unavailable, without state characterization or EPP circuit optimization. The strategy is to accumulate multiple entanglement distribution rounds and then use shared randomness to shuffle all the stored entangled states before packaging them as inputs to the EPP. For any $n$ Werner sources and any fixed $n$-to-1 bilocal Clifford EPP, we prove that accumulating and shuffling improves the expected success probability and the success-weighted output Bell fidelity over the baseline without accumulating and shuffling, for every $n$, for every finite number of accumulation rounds and in the asymptotic limit, and the improvement increases monotonically with the number of accumulation rounds.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a method to build high-dimensional controlled-NOT (CNOT) quantum gates using generalized parity modules. The construction is heralded and deterministic, overcoming limitations of previous schemes by identifying only two usable module types. This work advances the feasibility of qudit-based quantum computation in optical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a robust theoretical framework for building universal quantum gates in high-dimensional Hilbert spaces using measurement-based techniques, which is crucial for scaling up photonic quantum computation beyond simple qubit systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to develop deterministic, heralded, and dimensionality-insensitive methods for constructing high-dimensional module-based quantum computation gates.

**Main result.** The authors successfully constructed a generalized controlled-NOT (CNOT) gate using only two specific types of parity modules out of all possible possibilities. This scheme requires no postselection and generalizes to arbitrary qudit dimensions ($d$).

**Method.** They propose constructing the CNOT gate via sequential operations involving generalized parity modules, Fourier transformations, and ancillary qubits/qudits.

**Model / system.** The system involves high-dimensional quantum states (qudits) where computation is built upon 'generalized parity modules,' which are implemented optically using Orbital Angular Momentum (OAM) degrees of freedom in single-photon systems.

**Key observables.** The success of the parity module operation is heralded by specific detection outcomes from photon-number-resolving and single-photon detectors.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality $d$ of the qudit basis, and the structure of the two usable parity modules: $\mathcal{P}=(j\ominus i)mod d$ and $\mathcal{P}=(j \oplus i)mod d$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The scheme assumes the ability to implement quantum nondemolition measurements for the parity module and relies on specific detection events (e.g., no firing of certain detectors) to confirm gate success.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the construction of the generalized CNOT gate using blocks like $P_1$, $P_2$, and Fourier transformations ($F_d$), showing the optical components involved in parity sorting.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the concept and constructs the CNOT gate for qutrits, then generalizes this construction mathematically to arbitrary qudit dimensions ($d \ge 4$) using linear algebra and state evolution equations. Finally, it details an optical implementation scheme utilizing OAM encoding.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Parity measurements have been explored as building blocks for preparing and discriminating entangled states, as well as for implementing quantum computation. We first develop two alternative high-dimensional generalized parity modules, and then propose a procedure for constructing high-dimensional generalized module-based controlled-NOT gate. The construction of module-based quantum computing introduced here is deterministic, heralded, insensitive to the dimensionality of the computing basis, and postselection technique is not required. The result shows that out of $(d-1)!$ generalized parity modules, only modules $ \mathcal{P}=(j\ominus i)\bmod d$ and $ \mathcal{P}=(j \oplus i)\bmod d$ can be used as building blocks for high-dimensional quantum computing. Furthermore, we proposed an optical nondestructive scheme for implementing generalized parity module through quantum nondemolition measurements, and the success of the parity module is heralded by photon-number-resolving detectors and single-photon detectors.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes and experimentally validates a method for space-division multiplexing Quantum Key Distribution (QKD) using few-mode fibers. By employing Multi-Plane Light Conversion (MPLC), they successfully separate entangled photons transmitted through distinct guided modes, enabling parallel quantum channels. This demonstrates a pathway toward building scalable, multi-user quantum internet architectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses scalability in quantum networking by integrating spatial degrees of freedom into established QKD protocols, which is a major topic at the intersection of quantum optics and communication theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge is achieving high key rates and distributing independent secret keys for Quantum Key Distribution (QKD) in practical quantum communication networks.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated a space-division multiplexed BBM92 QKD scheme using few-mode fibers, achieving measurable secure key rates across multiple parallel channels.

**Method.** They employed Multi-Plane Light Conversion (MPLC) to demultiplex entangled photons transmitted through a Few-Mode Fiber (FMF), exploiting distinguishable guided modes as independent quantum channels.

**Model / system.** The system uses an FMF supporting multiple spatial modes ($	ext{LP}_{01}$ and $	ext{LP}_{11b}$) to carry entangled photon pairs generated via SPDC. The MPLC acts as the receiver component for separating these parallel channels.

**Key observables.** Quantum Bit Error Rates (QBER) ($1.9 \pm 0.4\%$ and $6.8 \pm 0.8\%$), secure key rates (bits/s), and visibility ($V_H$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** FMF core diameter ($15 \mu	ext{m}$), NA $= 0.1$, operating wavelengths ($\lambda_s = 1530 	ext{ nm}$, $\lambda_i = 1570 	ext{ nm}$), and the QKD security threshold of $11\%$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that exploiting distinguishable guided modes preserves the necessary quantum correlations for secure QKD, unlike high-dimensional schemes based on coherent superpositions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the concept using FMF and MPLC for parallel channels. Figure 2 details the experimental setup components. Figure 3 compares QBER and key rate vs. rounds for multiplexed versus non-multiplexed cases, and Figure 4 shows coincidence counts per round.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the need for scalable QKD networks, presents the theoretical concept of using FMFs with MPLC for space-division multiplexing, details the experimental setup and measurements (BBM92 protocol), reports the measured QBER and key rates, and discusses limitations regarding coupling efficiency.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

As quantum key distribution (QKD) progresses from laboratory demonstrations toward practical deployment, quantum communi- cation networks increasingly require higher key rates and the ability to distribute independent secret keys among multiple users and network nodes. In this paper we present a promsing approach with multi-plane light conversion (MPLC) for demultiplexing entangled photons, transmitted through a few-mode fiber (FMF). We experimentally demonstrated a spatially multiplexed BBM92 QKD scheme. The modes are selectively excited through separate single-mode-fibers and subsequently separated by MPLC into distinct output ports. Unlike high-dimensional QKD based on coherent modal superpositions, our approach exploits distinguishable guided modes as parallel channels while preserving the entanglement required for QKD. For the multiplexed links, we obtain quantum bit error rates of $1.9 \pm 0.4\%$ for the channel 1 and $6.8 \pm 0.8\%$ for the channel 2. These results are relevant for scalable quantum-secured networks, space-division-multiplexed QKD systems, multi-user entanglement distribution, and future quantum internet architectures, where parallel quantum channels must be implemented without compromising the quantum correlations required for security.

</details>

(a) Polaron dispersion in the spiral phase (t2/t1 = −0.20) versus the center-of-mass momentum K along the lab- frame path −Q∗→Γ→+Q∗(|Q∗| = 1.2), for magnon trunca- tions C+1m, . . . ,C+5m (viridis). Marker size ∝Z(K); dot- ted: bare dispersion ε0. A sharp quasiparticle (Z ≃0.92) at the Lifshitz-shifted momentum K = −Q∗evolves into a multi-magnon continuum beyond the critical momentum Kc, where the spiral becomes locally unstable (see SM [23]). **Summary.** This work precisely locates the quantum critical point where Nagaoka ferromagnetism destabilizes into an incommensurate spiral state on a triangular lattice. By combining analytical theory with advanced numerical methods, the authors establish $t_{2,c}/t_1 = -0.182$ as the transition boundary. This provides crucial theoretical insight into magnetic fluctuations and pairing mechanisms in doped correlated electron systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous determination of a quantum phase transition boundary in a highly correlated model like the Hubbard model is fundamental to understanding magnetic ordering mechanisms relevant to high-temperature superconductivity.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study determines the exact quantum instability point where Nagaoka ferromagnetism breaks down into a long-wavelength coplanar spiral state in a doped triangular lattice.

**Main result.** The transition occurs at a critical hopping ratio of $t_{2,c}/t_1 = -0.182$, establishing the first quantum instability of this ferromagnetic phase and providing a baseline for magnon-mediated pairing.

**Method.** The problem is solved by reducing it to an effective two-body hole–magnon scattering problem, utilizing analytical calculations combined with rigorous numerical techniques like Exact Diagonalization (ED) and spectral flow analysis.

**Model / system.** The system is the single-hole infinite-$U$ Hubbard model on a triangular lattice, incorporating nearest-neighbor ($t_1$) and next-nearest-neighbor ($t_2$) hopping terms. The physics is governed by kinetic energy minimization in the presence of strong correlations.

**Key observables.** Spiral stiffness coefficient ($c_2$), quasiparticle weight ($Z \simeq 0.92$), ground-state energy difference $E(\Phi) - E_{FM}$ vs. magnetic flux $\Phi$, and the optimal spiral wavevector $Q^*$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical hopping ratio $t_{2,c}/t_1 = -0.182$ is central; the model operates in the infinite-$U$ limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis treats the hard-core hole–magnon constraint exactly and relies on small-$Q$ expansions near criticality to characterize the continuous nature of the transition.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the vanishing spiral stiffness at $t_{2,c}/t_1 = -0.182$, show the formation of an energy minimum (spiral well) in spectral flow plots as $|t_2|$ increases, and map out polaron dispersion curves in the resulting spiral phase.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the instability by first deriving effective Hamiltonians, then using analytical methods to pinpoint the critical point ($t_{2,c}/t_1 = -0.182$), confirming this result with ED and spectral flow analysis across multiple diagnostics (e.g., energy vs. flux, quasiparticle weight).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the single-hole infinite-$U$ Hubbard model on the triangular lattice with nearest- and next-nearest-neighbor hoppings $t_1$ and $t_2$. A frustrating $t_2$ destabilizes Nagaoka ferromagnetism through a Lifshitz transition to a long-wavelength coplanar spiral at $t_{2,c}/t_1=-0.182$. We determine the critical point analytically by reducing the many-body problem to an effective two-body hole--magnon scattering problem in which the hard-core hole--magnon constraint is treated exactly. Numerical calculations confirm the spiral ground state and reveal a coherent spin polaron with quasiparticle weight $Z\simeq0.92$ at the Lifshitz-shifted momentum $\mathbf{K}=-\mathbf{Q}^*$. These results establish the first quantum instability of the triangular-lattice Nagaoka ferromagnet and provide the magnetic baseline for magnon-mediated pairing at finite doping.

</details>

### [Shiba duality and $η$-altermagnetism: Pairing and charge orders in bipartite attractive Hubbard models](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21430v1)

**Authors:** Yu-Ping Lin

**Summary.** This work develops the concept of $\eta$-altermagnetism by applying Shiba duality to map spin-based Altermagnetism principles onto charge and pairing orders. Using BdG theory on attractive Hubbard models, the authors predict specific $\eta$-pseudospin splitting patterns that arise from symmetry breaking due to lattice hopping anisotropies.

**Why it may be interesting.** It extends concepts of magnetic ordering (ALM) into superconducting/charge sectors using a novel pseudospin degree of freedom ($\eta$), which is relevant for understanding exotic pairing symmetries in strongly correlated superconductors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates $\eta$-altermagnetism ($\eta$-ALM) as a generalization of Altermagnetism (ALM) to pairing and charge orders in attractive Hubbard models.

**Main result.** Shiba duality establishes an exact correspondence between repulsion-driven Antiferromagnetism (AFM) and attraction-driven $\eta$-AFM, leading to observable $\eta$-pseudospin splitting structures like $p$-, $d$-, or $f$-wave patterns.

**Method.** The study employs mean-field Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) theory combined with Shiba duality mapping techniques.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by the pure Hubbard Hamiltonian on bipartite lattices, analyzed at half-filling using both spin and $\eta$-pseudospin order parameters.

**Key observables.** Onsite charge order ($s_{\mu}^i$), onsite spin order ($\sigma_{\mu}^2$), onsite pairing order ($p_{\mu}^i$), and the resulting $\eta$-pseudospin splitting energies in band structures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Half-filling ($n_f=1$), attractive interaction strength (related to $U_0 < 0$), and hopping anisotropies (e.g., second-neighbor hopping).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mean-field approximations within the BdG formalism, assuming intrinsic particle-hole symmetry for certain analyses.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $\eta$-ALMs on checkerboard and honeycomb lattices, showing ground-state orders (CDM and USP), resulting band splitting patterns, and BZ mappings of these energies.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the connection via Shiba duality between spin ALM and $\eta$-ALM. It then details the BdG formalism for calculating charge/pairing orders and analyzes how anisotropic hopping breaks symmetry to generate measurable $\eta$-pseudospin splitting in momentum space.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that Shiba duality maps the altermagnetic principle of momentum-dependent band splitting from spin to $η$-pseudospin, defining $η$-altermagnetism ($η$-ALM) as a Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) counterpart of ALM in pairing and charge orders. In half-filled pure Hubbard models on bipartite lattices, the duality relates repulsion-driven antiferromagnetism (AFM) to attraction-driven $η$-AFM with uniform singlet pairing and staggered charge-density modulation. In the BdG bands, the Shiba-dual parity-time-reversal $\mathcal{\tilde{P}}\mathcal{\tilde{T}}$ symmetry protects a Kramers degeneracy of $η$-pseudospin. Anisotropic second-neighbor hopping breaks this degeneracy and generates $η$-ALM. Odd-parity $η$-ALM shows $η$-pseudospin splitting, whereas even-parity $η$-ALM has spin-$η$-locked splitting. Hartree-Fock-Bogoliubov computations on checkerboard and honeycomb lattices find $η$-ALMs with $p$-, $d$-, and $f$-wave splitting structures. Possible generalizations and experimental probes are discussed.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors theoretically show that the orientation of an $m{H}_2^+$ molecule acts as a structural switch to engineer optical Schrödinger cat states using high-harmonic generation. By analyzing two different physical mechanisms—resonance selection and destructive interference—they demonstrate tunable crossovers between 'kitten' and 'cat' quantum regimes, offering unprecedented control over nonclassical light sources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work demonstrates using molecular structure ($	heta$) as a novel, intrinsic control knob in quantum optics to manipulate complex nonclassical states like Schrödinger cats, which is highly relevant for advanced quantum light sources and quantum simulation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to engineer nonclassical optical Schrödinger cat states by controlling the high-harmonic generation (HHG) process in a molecular system.

**Main result.** Molecular orientation ($	heta$) provides a structural control parameter, enabling tunable crossovers between 'kitten' and 'cat' quantum states via two distinct physical mechanisms: resonance enhancement and two-center destructive interference.

**Method.** The study couples Time-Dependent Schrödinger Equation (TDSE) simulations with the fully quantized HHG framework to calculate post-selected harmonic-mode states.

**Model / system.** The system involves an $m{H}_2^+$ molecule interacting with a strong, linearly polarized 800 nm laser pulse. The state is characterized by its Wigner function $W(x, p; 	heta)$.

**Key observables.** Wigner function negativity (diagnosing nonclassicality), the amplitude $|eta_q(	heta)|$ of the post-selected state, and the characterization as 'kitten', 'cat', or classical.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The molecular orientation angle ($	heta$), the harmonic order ($q$), and the laser intensity/focal geometry (which are shown to be decoupled from the primary control).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The coherence factor $c$ is assumed essentially orientation-independent, and the analysis relies on post-selection via XUV photon-number resolution.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate how varying $	heta$ causes a crossover between 'kitten' and 'cat' states. Specific cuts show the evolution of $|eta_{55}(	heta)|$, demonstrating reentrant behavior around an interference minimum $	heta^*$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by coupling TDSE to quantized HHG, then analyzes two distinct control regimes: resonance-enhanced low-order harmonics (showing opposite crossovers for different transitions) and plateau harmonics exploiting two-center destructive interference (showing a reentrant cat $	o$ kitten $	o$ cat sequence).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We theoretically demonstrate that the molecular orientation angle $θ$ provides a structurally intrinsic, continuously tunable control parameter for engineering optical Schrödinger cat states via high-harmonic generation (HHG) in H$_2^+$. Coupling time-dependent Schrödinger equation simulations to the fully quantized HHG framework, we evaluate the Wigner functions of the post-selected harmonic-mode states under two complementary conditioning strategies. Conditioning on resonance-enhanced low-order harmonics exploits the complementary dipole selection rules of the $1σ_g\to1σ_u$ and $1σ_g\to1π_u$ transitions, driving a kitten-cat crossover whose direction is opposite in the two channels as $θ$ is varied. Conditioning on plateau harmonics instead exploits two-center destructive interference, producing a reentrant cat$\to$kitten$\to$cat transition controlled by the order-dependent interference angle $θ^*(q)$. In both cases the crossover is decoupled from the laser intensity, focal geometry, and molecular density, offering a degree of control with no counterpart in atomic targets.

</details>

**Summary.** This review comprehensively surveys semi-Dirac fermions—quasiparticles with unique directional dispersion—which emerge at topological Lifshitz transition points. It details the theoretical differences between Type-I and Type-II phases by analyzing their distinct signatures in measurable quantities like DOS, Landau levels, and optical response. The work highlights that these exotic states are crucial for understanding modern quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of topological phase transitions and exotic band structures provides rich ground for understanding emergent collective behavior in strongly interacting electron systems, which is central to condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The article reviews semi-Dirac fermions, which are two-dimensional quasiparticles exhibiting unique dispersion relations (quadratic or linear) depending on the direction of motion.

**Main result.** The existence and properties of these fermions are intrinsically linked to topological Lifshitz transitions that govern changes in Fermi surface connectivity. Type-I and Type-II phases exhibit distinct signatures in their electronic structure.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis involving calculating topological invariants like the Berry curvature, solving for Density of States (DOS) and Landau Levels (LLs), and analyzing transport properties using semiclassical quantization.

**Model / system.** The physics is modeled using effective Hamiltonians (e.g., Type-I and Type-II forms) that describe low-energy excitations in quantum materials, often arising from topological phase boundaries.

**Key observables.** Density of States ($ho(\epsilon)$), Landau Level energy spectra ($\epsilon_n$), optical conductivity ($\sigma(\omega)$), and the Berry phase (topological invariant).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The transition parameter $\Delta$ controls the type of semi-Dirac fermion; magnetic field $B$ is crucial for LL quantization.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Calculations often assume the clean limit for transport properties, and the analysis relies on semiclassical approximations for Landau levels.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of DOS across the Lifshitz transition for both Type-I ($ho \propto \sqrt{\epsilon}$) and Type-II ($ho \propto \epsilon^{1/3}$), and show the characteristic energy scaling of LLs for different fermion types.

**Paper structure.** The review progresses by introducing the concept, detailing specific Hamiltonian models (Type-I vs. Type-II), analyzing key diagnostics like DOS and LLs, discussing transport properties, and concluding with experimental evidence from synthetic lattices and quantum materials.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This article reviews the recent progress on the subject of semi-Dirac fermions, two dimensional quasiparticles that disperse quadratically, as Galilean invariant particles, or linearly, as massless relativistic particles, depending on their direction of motion. These particles exist at a phase boundary set by the continuous change in the connectivity of Fermi surfaces, known as topological Lifshitz transitions. The basic properties and the current experimental evidence of the existence of these particles in both synthetic lattices and quantum materials are presented. The many-body problem of semi-Dirac fermions is discussed from a theoretical perspective with an eye to physical observables of relevance to experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes that deep mathematical symmetries, specifically duality constraints derived from statistical mechanics, dictate the optimal error correction threshold for many quantum codes. By mapping QEC to a critical point problem and employing Renormalization Group techniques, the authors show that these universal constraints unify the understanding of performance across different code architectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful mathematical framework (duality/RG) from condensed matter physics to analyze fundamental limits in quantum computation, connecting abstract symmetries to concrete physical performance metrics like error thresholds.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how duality constraints determine the optimal error correction threshold for various families of quantum error-correcting codes.

**Main result.** Duality constrains all em-symmetric CSS codes to share a single universal optimal code capacity threshold, and this self-duality is preserved under code concatenation.

**Method.** The authors employ statistical mechanical mappings, generalized Kramers-Wannier duality, and Renormalization Group (RG) flow analysis on the associated disordered Ising model.

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is Quantum Error Correction (QEC), specifically focusing on CSS codes. The problem is mapped to a critical point of a statistical mechanical model described by an Ising-like Hamiltonian involving Pauli stabilizers.

**Key observables.** Optimal error correction thresholds ($p_c$), logical failure rate ($	ext{P}_{	ext{fail}}$), and the free energy cost associated with logical operators.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Error probability ($p$), code block size ($n$ or $L$), coupling constant ($K_p$), and the replica limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping QEC to a statistical mechanical model, assuming self-duality holds for zero-rate families, and using approximations like full postselection in RG flow calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams comparing different code types (topological vs. concatenated) relative to the Nishimori line, and show data collapse plots demonstrating the fit of $	ext{P}_{	ext{fail}}$ to scaling laws derived from RG analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the connection between QEC thresholds and critical points via statistical mechanics. It then applies generalized duality arguments to constrain these thresholds for specific code classes, culminating in an analysis using RG flow to model concatenation effects on the failure probability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Error correction thresholds are often treated as the primary figure of merit for comparing quantum error-correcting code families. We show that the optimal error correction threshold for many commonly considered codes is constrained to a single universal value at leading order in a replica limit. Through a statistical mechanical mapping, we demonstrate that duality constrains all zero-rate em-symmetric CSS codes to have the same optimal code capacity threshold. Here, em symmetry means that the X- and Z-type parity-check matrices are equivalent up to row and column permutations. Under this statistical mechanical mapping, em-symmetric CSS codes are self-dual under a generalized Kramers-Wannier duality up to a mixing of logical sectors. For zero-rate code families, this mixing contributes only subextensive corrections, so the thermodynamic bulk free energy is self-dual in the trivial logical sector. This self-duality fixes the clean critical point and constrains the disordered phase boundary. We also show that self-duality is preserved under code concatenation, and that optimal decoding of concatenated codes can be reformulated as a renormalization group flow on a hierarchical lattice. Our results provide a common framework for analyzing topological, concatenated, and more general quantum low-density parity-check code families, including both their optimal code capacity thresholds and their sub-threshold logical error suppression.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an exceptional point geometry approach to unify the description of weak topological boundary states in 2D lattices. Using the PCL model, it shows that edge, corner, and compact localized modes can all be understood as manifestations of singularities (exceptional points/curves) within the complex momentum space of the system's Hamiltonian. This offers a powerful, unified theoretical tool for characterizing various forms of topological localization.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful generalization of topological concepts (like bulk-boundary correspondence) into the language of complex geometry (exceptional points), which is highly relevant for designing novel quantum materials or photonic structures with controlled boundary physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a unified geometric framework for understanding weak topological boundary states, encompassing edge, corner, and compact localized modes.

**Main result.** Weak topology can be described by exceptional singularities in the complex momentum space of an analytically continued Bloch Hamiltonian. This provides a single theoretical structure unifying different types of boundary localization phenomena.

**Method.** The analysis involves analytically continuing the lattice Hamiltonian into complex momentum space and identifying zero modes by solving equations derived from this continuation.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the Plaquette Chiral Lattice (PCL) in two dimensions, which serves as a minimal realization of dual weak topology. The system's behavior is governed by its Bloch Hamiltonian, $h(\mathbf{k})$.

**Key observables.** Momentum-resolved winding numbers ($
u_x, 
u_y$), the location of exceptional points (EPs) and curves in complex momentum space, and the boundary localization length ($\ell$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters include the hopping coefficients ($v, \gamma, w, \xi$) and the complex momenta variables ($z_x, z_y$), which determine the nature of the localized state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on analytically continuing the Bloch Hamiltonian to complex momentum space for applicability beyond the specific PCL model. A transition occurs when reciprocal exceptional singularities reach the real Brillouin zone.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the lattice structure, show the fraction of conserved momenta supporting zero modes in ribbons, and depict the construction involving edge EP branches and corner mode localization within the bidisk.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the PCL model, derives the Bloch Hamiltonian, extends the analysis to complex momentum space by analytically continuing the Hamiltonian, and then uses the resulting exceptional point geometry to characterize edge, corner, and compact localized states sequentially.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this article, we demonstrate that weak topology can be formulated geometrically in terms of exceptional singularities of an analytically continued Bloch Hamiltonian. A general plaquette chiral model in two dimensions serves as a minimal realization of dual weak topology, possessing two independent families of weak topological invariants, one for each spatial direction. The weak-topological edge states correspond to exceptional points in complex momentum space, while corner zero modes emerge from exceptional curves obtained by complexifying both momenta. Compact localized states arise when the exceptional roots collapse to the origin. This framework provides a unified complex-momentum description of edge, corner, and compact localization.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes a sophisticated geometric perturbation theory for nonlinear electronic transport in Bloch bands. It extends standard descriptions by calculating higher-rank tensors that capture complex multiband geometry. The key achievement is the derivation of intrinsic third-order conductivity channels using rigorous diagrammatic and recursive methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state transport, the rigorous geometric framework—using higher-order invariants beyond curvature—provides powerful mathematical machinery applicable to any system where geometry dictates response, such as quantum optics or open quantum systems dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a geometric perturbation theory to calculate nonlinear transport coefficients, specifically charge conductivity, in electronic systems described by Bloch bands.

**Main result.** It derives the full set of intrinsic third-order conductivity channels ($\sigma^L$ and $\sigma^W$) that are purely geometric, showing how higher-rank tensors encode physics beyond simple quantum metrics.

**Method.** The methodology involves constructing a hierarchy of geometric tensors ($Q^{(N)}$) using the Bargmann trace and employing diagrammatic techniques to separate vertex-order (dressed) corrections from strict-order (bare gap) corrections.

**Model / system.** The system is electronic matter described by Bloch wavefunctions in periodic potentials, analyzed via perturbation theory applied to interband couplings induced by an external electric field.

**Key observables.** Third-order charge conductivity components ($\sigma_{abc;d}$), including intrinsic channels like the gradient of a three-band energy loop ($\sigma^L$) and curvature corrections ($\sigma^W$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electric field strength ($E$), perturbation order ($N=3$ for conductivity), and relaxation time ($	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of equilibrium distribution functions for intrinsic channels, leading to fully geometric results independent of scattering rates in the $	au 	o 0$ limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Bargmann expansion up to fourth order, diagrammatic rules for connected/disconnected tensors ($Q^{(N)}$), and specific diagrams showing strict-order corrections needed for second- and third-order conductivity calculations.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from developing the general geometric perturbation theory via the Bargmann trace hierarchy ($\Lambda(N)$) to deriving algebraic recursion relations, then applying these tools to calculate energy corrections (BW formalism), and finally collecting terms into specific, physically interpretable nonlinear transport channels for conductivity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a geometric perturbation theory of Bloch states for perturbations coupling via the interband Berry connection. The gauge-invariant Bargmann trace builds the dressed dispersion and connection order by order as a hierarchy \(Q^{(N)}\), starting with the quantum geometric tensor. Higher members encode multiband geometry beyond the quantum metric and Berry curvature. Connected amplitudes control vertex-order corrections, while strict-order corrections reduce to disconnected products. For a uniform electric field we find the fully coherent, purely geometric sector of the third-order response.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper tackles the incompatibility between current quantum device modeling standards (designed for superconducting qubits) and the unique capabilities of neutral atom systems. By proposing an enhanced, spatially aware device model that accounts for dynamic shuttling and zoned operations, the authors demonstrate a massive improvement in compilation fidelity when integrating these advanced platforms into universal quantum software ecosystems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the software abstraction layer bottleneck for next-generation quantum hardware, providing a crucial theoretical blueprint for integrating dynamic physical platforms like neutral atoms into standardized quantum computing toolkits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Existing universal quantum ecosystem device models are limited by superconducting hardware assumptions, failing to capture the dynamic capabilities of emerging neutral atom platforms.

**Main result.** The proposed redesign of device models enables seamless integration of neutral atoms into existing ecosystems, leading to a routing overhead fidelity improvement up to a factor of 100,000.

**Method.** The authors propose targeted extensions to current device model specifications (using JSON) to incorporate spatial coordinates and dynamic operations like shuttling and zoning.

**Model / system.** The system involves Neutral Atom quantum processors utilizing optical tweezers/lattices for qubits. The model must abstract features like dynamic qubit rearrangement, zoned operations, and precise site locations in 2D/3D space.

**Key observables.** Routing overhead fidelity (the primary metric), which measures the accumulated error across a circuit's gates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit count (e.g., 16 qubits) and gate count (e.g., 600 gates); spatial parameters like interaction/blocking radii, basis vectors for lattice definition.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Current universal quantum ecosystems rely on device models that assume static qubit positions and fixed coupling maps, which is the core limitation being addressed.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate NA operations including shuttling and zoned gates; Figure 4 shows how lattices are constructed via basis vectors; Figure 5 details the workflow from JSON specification to a shared library accessed via QDMI.

**Paper structure.** The paper identifies the technology lock caused by SC-centric device models, proposes model extensions to capture NA dynamics (shuttling/zoning), demonstrates this using the QDMI framework, and quantifies the fidelity improvement over static models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum computing is transitioning from an academic idea to a practical technology, driven by recent hardware advancements and clear paths toward real-world applications. Universal quantum ecosystems (e.g., Qiskit, Cirq, PennyLane) facilitate this transition by providing a consistent interface to diverse quantum devices, abstracting hardware-specific details through a device model that captures each device's computational capabilities. However, these device models have historically been shaped by superconducting hardware, assuming static qubit positions and fixed coupling maps. This prevents them from representing the unique computational capabilities of emerging technologies such as neutral atoms, which feature dynamic qubit rearrangement and zoned operations. As a result, although numerous specialized compilers for neutral atom devices already exist, they cannot retrieve the hardware information they need through these ecosystems - creating a technology lock that hinders or even prevents the integration of neutral atom devices. In this work, we demonstrate how this limitation leads to suboptimal compilation results and can exclude certain devices entirely. Motivated by this, we propose rethinking current device models to faithfully represent neutral atom devices, enabling their seamless integration into universal quantum ecosystems. Evaluations conducted within the Quantum Device Management Interface (QDMI) demonstrate that the proposed device model unlocks a routing overhead fidelity improvement by a factor of up to 100,000 on a circuit with 16 qubits and 600 gates.

</details>

### [Gaplessness indicator by topologically trivial twisting operators](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20944v1)

**Authors:** Yuan Yao, Linhao Li, Feng-Feng Song  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.20944v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.20944_figures/2607.20944_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. QMC simulations of (1 −⟨UF⟩) with Fcos and Fsawtooth: (a) the gapped AKLT model; (b) the MG model; (c) XXZ model with ∆= 2 in the gapped regime; (d) the gapless spin-1/2 HAF model.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work establishes a general criterion to distinguish between gapped and gapless quantum many-body systems in 1D with U(1) symmetry. It achieves this by analyzing the finite-size scaling of expectation values involving 'topologically trivial twisting operators.' This provides a robust diagnostic tool, which can also predict measurable decay rates for static structure factors.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of gaplessness indicators based on local, topologically trivial observables provides a powerful, potentially experimentally accessible diagnostic tool that circumvents the need for complex topological invariants previously required.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors aim to develop general, necessary conditions for determining if a one-dimensional quantum many-body system with U(1) symmetry is gapped.

**Main result.** They show that the ground-state expectation value of specific 'topologically trivial twisting operators' must approach unity in the thermodynamic limit for a gapped system. Violation of this scaling indicates gaplessness.

**Method.** The method involves analyzing the behavior of correlation functions and specialized lattice operators (twisting operators) in the ground state sector using techniques involving Fourier decomposition and finite-size scaling arguments.

**Model / system.** The theory applies to general one-dimensional quantum many-body systems possessing U(1) symmetry and lattice translation symmetry, including models like spin chains or fermion chains.

**Key observables.** The primary diagnostic is the ground-state expectation value $\langle 	ext{gs}|U_F|	ext{gs}angle$, which must scale as $1 + O(1/L)$ for gapped systems. Additionally, static structure factors are analyzed.

**Important parameters / regimes.** One dimension (1D), U(1) symmetry, thermodynamic limit ($L 	o \infty$), and the zero average value of the function defining the operator ($ar{F}=0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the existence of lattice translation symmetry and assumes certain conditions on the structure factor decay rates for gapped states.

**Figures summary.** Numerical results confirm that gapped models exhibit $\langle U_F angle = 1 + O(1/L)$, while gapless models violate this scaling. The Fermi sea calculation serves as a concrete example of violation.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops from proposing initial, restrictive conditions (Theorem 3) to generalizing the diagnostic tool using 'topologically trivial' operators (Theorem 4). It then proves key lemmas regarding operator behavior in gapped systems and derives a general scaling law for static structure factors (Theorem 9).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose several general necessary conditions for quantum many-body system in one dimension respecting U(1) symmetry to be gapped. We show that the ground-state expectation value of topologically trivial twisting operators must approach unity in the thermodynamic limit with a certain finite-size scaling. Equivalently, its violation can indicate gaplessness of U(1)-symmetric Hamiltonians. The topological triviality of such a twisting operator enables us to derive infinitely many other gaplessness indicators by static structure factor to any order in real experiments, which are impossible to obtain by earlier topologically nontrivial twisting operators. We also apply analytic and numerical calculations to test the efficiency and consistency of our results.

</details>

**Authors:** Alexander Makarovskiy, Adam Taylor, Zhenghao Li, Michael Hanks, Aubrey Clark, M. S. Kim, Ian Walmsley, William Clements

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the feasibility of using photonic circuits in Variational Quantum Algorithms by quantifying 'trainability' through variance ratios. It establishes a critical distinction: low-order polynomial observables remain trainable with polynomial resource scaling, whereas higher-order ones fail exponentially. This work validates photonic VQC as a promising near-term platform under specific structural constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous, resource-based analysis showing that certain structured observables (low-order PNPs) maintain favorable scaling properties necessary for practical near-term quantum computation on photonic hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the 'trainability' of photonic quantum circuits for Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQAs), which determines if loss function gradients can be resolved efficiently.

**Main result.** Fixed-order photon-number polynomials maintain trainability, requiring only polynomially many samples as system size grows, unlike high-order observables which require exponential resources.

**Method.** The authors introduce a diagnostic framework based on the ratio of sample variance to circuit variance to quantify the required number of quantum measurements for gradient estimation.

**Model / system.** The platform is passive linear optical networks (PLONs) acting on photonic qubits, restricted to fixed total photon number input states. The dynamics are governed by unitary matrices U(M).

**Key observables.** Photon-number observables (PNMs), which form the basis for constructing general polynomial observables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of sample variance to circuit variance; system size ($M$) and photon number ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on passive linear optics (PLONs) and assumes a fixed total photon number input state for the initial analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how the ratio between sample and circuit variance measures the required samples for gradient estimation, showing scaling differences based on observable complexity.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a theoretical framework using moment calculations (first and second moments) to analyze the variances associated with estimating expectation values derived from photon number polynomials in PLONs. It compares polynomial vs. exponential scaling of these required samples.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Variational quantum algorithms are a leading approach to near-term quantum computing, but their scalability can be limited by barren plateaus and the sampling cost of resolving small changes in the loss landscape. Here, we study the trainability of passive linear-optical quantum circuits and introduce a framework based on the ratio of sample variance to circuit variance. This ratio determines the number of circuit samples required to resolve local loss differences and gradients to proportional accuracy. We apply this framework to photon-number observables and identify both trainable and non-trainable regimes. Supported by analytic results and a numerically observed polynomial decay of the circuit variance, we find that fixed-order photon-number polynomials require only polynomially many samples as the system size grows, whereas high-order polynomials and observables based on output probabilities generally require exponentially many samples. Within the trainable regime, we further identify classes of observables in which quantum estimation achieves a polynomial speed-up over multiple classical methods. Within this family, neural network observables provide one practical construction that allow measurement outcomes to be efficiently processed into the desired polynomial. These results establish photonic variational quantum computing as a promising platform for near-term applications.

</details>
