**Summary.** This paper advances Quantum Monte Carlo simulations for strongly correlated fermions by introducing gauge augmentation into the AFQMC framework. It reinterprets stochastic process non-uniqueness as a tunable 'gauge' parameter, allowing researchers to dynamically improve sampling efficiency and accuracy. The results demonstrate that these gauge-enhanced methods provide robust tools for tackling complex many-body Hamiltonians.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on nuclear/condensed matter Hamiltonians, the development of advanced stochastic sampling techniques applicable to fermionic dynamics is highly relevant for simulating complex quantum many-body systems in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Calculating the ground state properties of interacting many-fermion systems is computationally challenging due to strong correlations and basis set limitations.

**Main result.** The proposed gauge-augmented AFQMC methods successfully enhance sampling efficiency and reduce statistical fluctuations compared to conventional approaches when applied to models like LMG.

**Method.** The core method involves extending Phaseless Auxiliary-Field Quantum Monte Carlo (AFQMC) by incorporating stochastic gauge freedom, treating the force bias as a drift gauge or utilizing Fermi gauges derived from natural orbitals.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on general interacting many-fermion systems, using the Lipkin-Meshkov-Glick model as a proof of concept. The underlying physics is described by a second-quantized Hamiltonian with strong correlations.

**Key observables.** Ground state energy expectation values and systematic bias/statistical fluctuations relative to exact results (FCI).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($\lambda$), imaginary time evolution parameter ($	au$), and the choice of gauge parameters ($g_s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The AFQMC approach relies on approximations, notably the phaseless constraint; the novelty lies in systematically exploiting the non-uniqueness of the stochastic process via gauge transformations.

**Figures summary.** Comparison of QMC estimators against exact FCI results demonstrating reduced bias and fluctuations when using symmetry-projected trial states (PHF+SP) versus standard methods (DHF).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the challenge, details the theoretical framework by proposing gauge augmentation (drift/Fermi gauges), discusses implementation via natural orbitals and symmetry projection, and validates these techniques on a simple shell model Hamiltonian.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose novel Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) methods for interacting many-fermion systems by leveraging the stochastic gauge freedom, originally developed in Gaussian phase-space QMC, within the phaseless auxiliary-field QMC (AFQMC) framework. In particular, we reinterpret the conventional force bias in phaseless AFQMC as a drift gauge and explore Fermi gauges based on natural orbitals of a reduced one-body density matrix defined via a mixed estimator, yielding stochastic, time-dependent Hartree-Fock-like dynamics. We propose a symmetry-projection sampling scheme to enhance the sampling efficiency. As a proof of concept, we apply these gauge-augmented AFQMC methods to a simple shell-model Hamiltonian: the Lipkin-Meshkov-Glick model. Numerical results illustrate the potential of stochastic gauges to enhance accuracy and reduce fluctuations, underscoring the promise for advancing these new techniques toward more realistic shell-model applications.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces standardized benchmarks (QAlg-Bench and QIT-Bench) to rigorously test how well large language models can formally prove theorems in quantum information science. By comparing performance with and without access to verified domain libraries (LAD), the authors quantify model strengths, showing that structured knowledge significantly improves proof generation capabilities.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the frontier of formal verification in quantum theory, providing a concrete, quantifiable measure of AI's current mathematical reasoning capabilities within the domain of quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to measure the current capability of large AI agents (LLMs) to construct rigorous, machine-checkable proofs for theorems in quantum algorithms and quantum information theory.

**Main result.** Library-Augmented Deduction (LAD) significantly boosts performance across all tested models compared to baseline attempts, while revealing recurring weaknesses in areas like quantum simulation and entanglement theory.

**Method.** The authors developed two formal benchmarks, Lean-QuantumAlg-Bench and Lean-QIT-Bench, consisting of theorem-completion tasks evaluated within the Lean 4 proof assistant environment.

**Model / system.** The work evaluates four state-of-the-art LLMs (GPT-5.5, Kimi K3, DeepSeek V4-Pro, MiniMax M3) attempting to prove theorems concerning quantum algorithms and information theory concepts.

**Key observables.** Difficulty-weighted scores (e.g., 60.4/100 on QAlg-Bench), completion rates, economic cost ($	ext{USD}/	ext{score point}$), and time cost ($	ext{s}/	ext{score point}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The evaluation compares a task-only baseline against the Library-Augmented Deduction (LAD) setting, which provides access to verified domain libraries.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The benchmarks assume that performance can be quantified by deterministic proof checking within a fixed Lean 4 environment, establishing a reproducible baseline for future AI development.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the benchmark construction workflow. Figure 2 and 4 show score leaderboards comparing model performance (score/completion) across QAlg-Bench and QIT-Bench in both baseline and LAD settings. Table 8 provides detailed difficulty-normalized field scores.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the construction of the two benchmarks (QAlg-Bench and QIT-Bench), describes the evaluation methodology (baseline vs. LAD), presents quantitative results comparing multiple LLMs across various metrics, and concludes by discussing implications for AI science.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Formal verification is becoming increasingly practical for quantum computing, yet the ability of AI agents to construct machine-checkable proofs in this domain remains unmeasured. We introduce Lean-QuantumAlg-Bench and Lean-QIT-Bench, two Lean 4 benchmarks containing 36 and 40 theorem-completion tasks for quantum algorithms and quantum information theory, respectively. Every task compiles in a fixed environment and is evaluated by deterministic proof checking and targeted semantic review, with difficulty weights assigned before model execution. We evaluate four models-GPT-5.5, Kimi K3, DeepSeek V4-Pro, and MiniMax M3-within a common theorem-proving framework under two settings: a task-only baseline and library-augmented deduction (LAD), which additionally provides access to a verified domain library. The highest difficulty-weighted scores are 60.4 out of 100 on the quantum-algorithm benchmark and 59.6 out of 100 on the quantum-information benchmark. LAD improves both score and completion rate in all eight model-benchmark comparisons, with gains of up to 15.9 points, providing evidence that verified libraries can strengthen domain-specific proof agents. The results reveal recurring weaknesses of agentic proving in areas such as quantum simulation, quantum learning, quantum information measures, and entanglement theory. Monetary and wall-clock costs per score point also vary substantially across models, highlighting important capability-efficiency trade-offs. We expect these benchmarks to establish a reproducible baseline for developing more capable and reliable proof agents, and to pave the way toward self-evolving AI scientists for advancing quantum information science.

</details>

**Authors:** Azhar Iqbal, Derek Abbott  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21103v1>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes an EWL quantum game to determine how entanglement influences evolutionary stability. It finds that while entanglement stabilizes the equilibrium within a restricted strategy subspace, this stabilizing effect vanishes when the allowed strategies are expanded to the full SU(2) group. This demonstrates a strong dependence of dynamical stability on the assumed strategic domain.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work explores how the mathematical structure imposed by quantum constraints (like entanglement) can stabilize dynamical equilibria that are unstable classically, highlighting the critical role of the underlying parameter space in determining emergent stability properties.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how entanglement, introduced via EWL quantization, affects the evolutionary stability (ESS) of an interior mixed Nash equilibrium in a quantum game.

**Main result.** Entanglement successfully stabilizes the equilibrium as an ESS only within a restricted two-parameter strategy space; this stabilization fails when the strategy space is enlarged to the full SU(2) manifold.

**Method.** The authors use concepts from evolutionary game theory (ESS criteria) applied to quantum games, comparing payoff gaps calculated under restricted versus full parameterizations of quantum strategies.

**Model / system.** The model is a symmetric $2 	imes 2$ game analyzed using the Eisert–Wilkens–Lewenstein (EWL) quantization scheme. Strategies are parameterized either by a restricted two-parameter set or the full three-parameter SU(2) group.

**Key observables.** Payoff gaps ($P(s^*, s^*) - P(s, s^*)$), strictness of Nash equilibrium, and satisfaction of the second ESS condition.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The entanglement parameter $\gamma$, which controls the degree of quantum correlation in the game dynamics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is confined to a specific $2 	imes 2$ game structure and relies on comparing stability across restricted vs. full strategic domains.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the normalized Nash payoff gap confirming strictness in the restricted space for $\gamma > 0$. Figure 3 summarizes the loss of ESS status when moving from the restricted EWL domain to the full pure SU(2) space.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes that the classical game ($\gamma=0$) fails the ESS condition. It then proves stabilization in the restricted EWL space for $\gamma > 0$. Finally, it demonstrates the failure of this stability when extending the strategy set to the full SU(2) family.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We examine how Eisert--Wilkens--Lewenstein (EWL) quantization affects evolutionary stability in a symmetric $2\times2$ game whose interior mixed Nash equilibrium is not evolutionarily stable. In the restricted two-parameter EWL strategy space, the quantum equilibrium $s^*=(π/2,π/4)$ becomes a strict symmetric Nash equilibrium, and hence an evolutionarily stable strategy, for every $γ>0$. Extending the admissible pure strategies to the full three-parameter $SU(2)$ family preserves the Nash equilibrium but introduces a continuum of payoff-neutral mutants, causing strictness and the second ESS condition to fail. Thus, entanglement stabilizes the equilibrium only within the restricted EWL strategy space; the effect does not survive enlargement to full $SU(2)$.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21379v1>

**Summary.** This work uses an exact 1D hard-rod model within statistical mechanics to explain the anomalous behavior of direct correlation functions in crystalline solids. It shows that these divergences are not arbitrary but arise directly from the geometry and scarcity of local free volume within the crystal lattice. The findings provide a minimal, physically grounded explanation for both the magnitude and structure of these key thermodynamic quantities.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical hard-body systems, the rigorous connection drawn between singular thermodynamic quantities (like $c^{(2)}$) and geometric constraints (like minimal free volume) provides a powerful theoretical template applicable to understanding correlations in quantum many-body systems near phase boundaries or confinement.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to explain the unusually large amplitudes and specific spatial structure of second-order direct correlation functions in hard-sphere crystals compared to their liquid counterparts.

**Main result.** The paper establishes that both the magnitude (scaling as $1/n_{vac}$) and the lattice-specific form of these correlations are fundamentally controlled by the minimum local insertion free volume, $A_{\min}$.

**Method.** The authors employ an exact one-dimensional model for hard rods within a classical Density Functional Theory framework to derive theoretical scaling laws.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a crystal of hard spheres (approximated by hard rods in 1D) characterized by a periodic, crystal-like density profile. The analysis focuses on the local insertion free volume $A(x)$ as the key thermodynamic quantity.

**Key observables.** Second direct correlation function ($c^{(2)}$), first direct correlation function ($c^{(1)}$), and the local insertion free volume ($A(x)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Vacancy concentration ($n_{vac}$), residual free volume ($\Delta\alpha_a$), and the minimum local free volume ($A_{\min}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on using an exactly solvable 1D model and imposing a periodic density profile, rather than modeling the full crystallization transition.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate density profiles showing how $A$ changes between broad and sharply localized peaks. Other figures compare $c^{(2)}$ for sharp versus broad peaks, demonstrating geometric selectivity related to free volume.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the relationship between direct correlations and local free volume in 1D (using the Ornstein-Zernike equation). It then analyzes how this mechanism dictates both the magnitude ($c^{(2)} \sim -1/A_{\min}$) and the spatial structure of the correlation functions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Second order direct correlation functions in three-dimensional hard-sphere crystals have much larger amplitudes (of the order of $1/n_{\mathrm{vac}}$ where $n_{\mathrm{vac}}$ is the vacancy concentration) and a more strongly structured spatial form of apparent shorter range than their liquid-state counterparts. We separate the two underlying questions---why the correlations are large and why they have the form they do---using the exact one-dimensional Percus functional for hard rods. A periodic crystal-like density is represented by Gaussian peaks with occupation probability $q=1-n_{\mathrm{vac}}$. The relevant thermodynamic quantity is the local insertion free volume $A(x)$. Its minimum can be written as $A_{\mathrm{min}} =Δ_{αa}+(1-Δ_{αa})n_{\mathrm{vac}}$, where $Δ_{αa}$ is the residual free volume at full occupation caused by finite localization (characterized by a width parameter $α$) and lattice spacing $a$. The first and second direct correlations therefore contain the singular dependences $c^{(1)}\sim\ln A_{\mathrm{min}} $ and $c^{(2)} \sim-1/A_{\mathrm{min}} $. The observed $1/n_{\mathrm{vac}}$ dependence is the vacancy-dominated limit $n_{\mathrm{vac}}\ggΔ_{αa}$, rather than the most general result. The spatial form of $c^{(2)}$ has a separate geometrical origin: the hard-rod weight functions select configurations in which exclusion intervals and their boundaries intersect regions of small free volume. This produces plateaus, edges, and localized ridges tied to the underlying periodic density. A numerical solution of the inhomogeneous Ornstein--Zernike equation shows how these singular direct correlations are redistributed in the total and pair correlations. The model provides a minimal free-volume explanation for both the magnitude and the lattice-specific form of crystalline direct correlation functions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the phonon thermal Hall effect across various insulating materials, using $	ext{La}_{5/3}	ext{Sr}_{1/3}	ext{NiO}_4$ as a primary example. By measuring the thermal Hall resistivity ($\omega_{xy}$), the authors establish that its characteristic temperature and field dependencies provide a unified description for this phenomenon in different Mott insulators.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on thermal transport rather than quantum dynamics, the study probes collective excitations (phonons) in strongly correlated Mott insulators, which is a key area where lattice vibrations interact with electronic correlations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a unifying description for the phonon thermal Hall effect (THE) observed across various insulating materials.

**Main result.** The thermal Hall resistivity ($\omega_{xy}$) exhibits characteristic temperature and magnetic field dependencies ($T^{-1}$ divergence, linear $H$ dependence) that serve as a unifying signature for phonon THE in diverse insulators.

**Method.** Experimental measurements using dedicated one-heater-three-thermometer setups to measure the full thermal resistivity tensor ($\mathbf{w}$) and calculate conductivities ($oldsymbol{\kappa}$).

**Model / system.** The study focuses on doped Mott insulators, specifically $	ext{La}_{5/3}	ext{Sr}_{1/3}	ext{NiO}_4$, and compares results across other insulating systems like $	ext{SrTiO}_3$ and cuprates.

**Key observables.** Thermal Hall resistivity ($\omega_{xy}$), Thermal Hall angle ($	heta_{	ext{TH}}$), Longitudinal thermal conductivity ($\kappa_{xx}$), and specific heat ($C(T)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures (down to $\sim 2$ K), magnetic fields near $H=0$, and doping levels in Mott insulators.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes an isotropic and field-antisymmetric thermal resistivity tensor ($\mathbf{w}$).

**Figures summary.** Figures compare measured $\kappa_{xy}$ vs. $\omega_{xy}$, showing characteristic temperature divergences ($T^{-1}, T^{-2}$) for different materials, and illustrate the linear $H$ dependence of $\omega_{xy}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents measurements on $	ext{LSNO-1/3}$, analyzes its unique signatures ($\omega_{xy}(T)$, $\omega_{xy}(H)$), compares these findings to established data from other insulators (STO, LCO, Si), and concludes by proposing the unifying role of $\omega_{xy}$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A considerable phonon thermal Hall effect was recently discovered across a diverse collection of materials. To clarify this enigmatic thermal Hall response in various insulators, we investigate the doped Mott insulator La$_{5/3}$Sr$_{1/3}$NiO$_4$ as an example material system and reveal a characteristic phonon-dominated thermal Hall effect. As a sensitive probe of the transverse thermal response, the thermal Hall resistivity $w_{xy}$ exhibits an insulating-like temperature dependence $w_{xy}(T)$, a linear magnetic-field dependence $w_{xy}(H)$ near $H=0$, and a $T$-linear thermal Hall angle at low temperatures. The presence of similar phenomena across a series of insulators suggests that $w_{xy}$ serves as a unifying description of phonon thermal Hall effect, corroborated by an apparent correlation between the insulating-like $w_{xy}(T)$ and material's localized electronic state.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an unconditional construction for a one-time private-key unclonable encryption scheme for single bits. The authors rigorously prove its security by bounding the adversary's advantage using advanced techniques involving operator norms and spectral analysis within quantum information theory. It advances the field by providing tight, information-theoretic security guarantees for this cryptographic primitive.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, information-theoretic security proof for a quantum primitive (UE) that is highly relevant to quantum communication protocols, building upon foundational concepts in quantum cryptography.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is constructing an unconditionally secure, information-theoretically secure one-time private-key unclonable encryption (UE) scheme for single-bit messages.

**Main result.** The authors provide a construction achieving an advantage bound of $	ext{Pr}[	ext{Win}] \le 1/2 + 2^{-(n+1)/2}$, demonstrating the security of the proposed quantum encryption protocol.

**Method.** The proof involves advanced techniques from quantum information theory, utilizing Choi–Jamiołkowski representations and bounding operator norms ($\lVert G_+ Vert_\infty$) derived from analyzing the winning probability in an unclonable indistinguishability game.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum cryptographic scheme operating on one-bit messages using $n$-qubit ciphertexts and classical keys. Security relies fundamentally on the no-cloning principle applied to quantum states.

**Key observables.** The success probability of an adversary ($	ext{Pr}[	ext{Win}]$) in the unclonable indistinguishability experiment, and related operator norms like $\lVert E_{BC} Vert_\infty$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key length $n$ (determining the security parameter), and derived bounds such as $\gamma = d/L$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof relies on spectral decomposition, bounding quadratic forms using filter operators ($F$), and assuming specific algebraic properties of Pauli operators acting on tensor product Hilbert spaces.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the scheme, formulating the security game in terms of operator traces (Choi states), deriving bounds on correlation operators using lemmas (like Lemma 3.3), and finally applying spectral analysis with filter operators to achieve the tight final bound.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We give an unconditional construction of information-theoretically secure one-time private-key unclonable encryption scheme for one-bit messages, with efficient encryption and decryption and exponentially small unclonable-indistinguishability advantage.

</details>

### [Unified theory of classical and quantum semiparametric efficiency](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21524v1)

**Authors:** Mankei Tsang

**Summary.** This paper introduces a unified geometric theory to calculate statistical efficiency bounds for estimating parameters when the underlying model is high-dimensional (semiparametric). By generalizing classical and quantum concepts like the Cramér-Rao and Helstrom bounds, it offers a powerful tool to determine fundamental limits on measurement precision in fields ranging from general sensing to advanced optical imaging.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful mathematical toolkit for quantifying ultimate measurement precision limits in scenarios where many parameters are unknown, which is crucial for advanced quantum sensing and metrology beyond simple parameter estimation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a unified statistical theory for efficiency bounds applicable to both classical and quantum semiparametric models.

**Main result.** It provides a general framework that unifies the Cramér-Rao and Helstrom bounds, allowing computation of classical and quantum limits for complex estimation tasks like optical imaging.

**Method.** The approach is highly abstract and geometric, defining concepts like the parameter tangent space and information semimetric to generalize existing statistical bounds beyond finite dimensions.

**Model / system.** The theory is applied broadly, covering general classical and quantum models (Gaussian, Poisson fields) with specific applications in coherent and incoherent optical imaging problems.

**Key observables.** Statistical efficiency bounds, the output efficient influence ($
abla_{	ext{out}}eta$), and classical/quantum limits to imaging resolution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** High-dimensional unknown parameters ($	heta$) summarized by a finite-dimensional parameter of interest ($eta(	heta)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is the need for minimal assumptions about high-dimensional parameters, and mathematically, it relies on differentiability and the existence/properties of certain operators like $F^{	ext{push}}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from fundamental abstract concepts (parameter tangent space, information semimetric) to specific applications in classical statistics (e.g., Gaussian fields), quantum estimation theory (Helstrom bounds), and finally applies the unified formalism to complex physical systems like optical imaging.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In classical and quantum statistics, high-dimensional unknown parameters are abundant and it is often prudent to make minimal assumptions about them using so-called semiparametric models. To attack a wide range of semiparametric problems in one broad stroke, we present a unified treatment of statistical efficiency for classical and quantum semiparametric models, generalizing the Cramér-Rao and Helstrom bounds beyond finite-dimensional parameters. We introduce the fundamental concepts in abstract and geometric terms before applying them to many examples, covering general classical and quantum models as well as the paradigmatic special cases of Gaussian and Poisson fields. We give an in-depth treatment of channels in the semiparametric efficiency theory and advocate the use of the singular value decomposition to elucidate the statistical effects of channels. To demonstrate the utility of the formalism, we apply it to coherent and incoherent optical imaging problems, assuming an arbitrary field or intensity on the object plane without parametric assumptions. Our formalism enables us to compute classical and quantum limits to coherent and incoherent imaging resolution in statistical terms. For subdiffraction incoherent imaging, we demonstrate that spatial-mode demultiplexing can be far superior to direct imaging in estimating generalized Fourier coefficients and come closer to the quantum limits. We envision our theory becoming an essential tool for both classical and quantum statistics with useful applications to sensing and imaging, whenever minimal assumptions about a high-dimensional parameter should be made.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the concept of 'ideal bands' by examining when their associated topological functional saturates a theoretical lower bound. The authors provide an explicit construction for such bands under conditions of exponentially decaying hopping but prove that these ideal states are impossible to realize in models with only finite-range interactions. This establishes fundamental limitations on the topology achievable in lattice Hamiltonians.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mathematical constraints derived from topological invariants like the Chern number place severe restrictions on the realizability of specific band structures in physical models, which is fundamental knowledge for designing topological materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates 'ideal bands,' specifically Chern-ideal and Wilson-loop-ideal bands, defined by the saturation of a topological lower bound on their Dirichlet functional.

**Main result.** The authors prove that isolated Chern-ideal bands with non-zero Chern number cannot exist in finite-band tight-binding models utilizing only finite-range hopping. This conclusion extends to critical and Wilson-loop-ideal bands under similar conditions.

**Method.** The proof heavily relies on complex analysis, specifically using the identity theorem applied to functions derived from Bloch Hamiltonians defined over the Brillouin zone.

**Model / system.** The system is a finite-band tight-binding model defined on a 2D Bravais lattice with conventional translation symmetries. The Hamiltonian structure is analyzed via its momentum-space representation, $h(\mathbf{k})$.

**Key observables.** Chern number (Ch), Quantum metric ($\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{k}}$), Berry curvature ($F_{\mathbf{k}}$), and the Dirichlet functional.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The nature of hopping decay (exponentially decaying vs. finite-range) and the relative positions of embedded orbitals ($\Delta\xi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Conventional lattice translation symmetry is assumed; the proof for non-existence relies on assuming $	ext{Ch} > 0$ or that the Berry curvature does not diverge.

**Paper structure.** The paper first provides an explicit construction of Chern-ideal bands with Ch=1 using exponentially decaying hopping. It then develops a rigorous proof, utilizing complex analysis techniques (like extending variables to $\mathbb{C}^2$), demonstrating that the requirement for non-zero Chern number leads to contradictions when restricted to finite-range hoppings.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A band is called ideal when its Dirichlet functional saturates the topological lower bound. We study ideal bands in finite-band tight-binding models with conventional two-dimensional lattice translation symmetries, allowing the bands to have non-flat dispersion. We first provide an analytic construction of isolated Chern-ideal bands with Chern number $|\mathrm{Ch}|=1$ in finite-band models with exponentially decaying hopping. This construction applies only when at least two orbitals have different embedded positions (modulo lattice vectors), complementing the previously known construction for $|\mathrm{Ch}|>1$. We then show that isolated Chern-ideal bands with any nonzero Chern number cannot exist in finite-band models with finite-range hopping, regardless of the embedded orbital positions. The conclusion holds even if there are isolated band touching points, as long as the Berry curvature does not diverge anywhere in the Brillouin zone. We finally generalize the conclusions to Wilson-loop-ideal bands with zero total Chern number, such as Kane-Mele $\mathbb{Z}_2$-ideal bands.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically connects momentum-resolved EELS data with optical conductivity in strange metals by analyzing the underlying charge density response function. It proves that this bulk response must obey a universal $q^2$ scaling law as momentum transfer approaches zero. This constraint defines a crossover surface, offering a fundamental test for theories describing charge dynamics in high-temperature superconductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical constraint derived from fundamental symmetries (Ward identity) provides a powerful tool to test theoretical models of strongly correlated electron systems against disparate experimental measurements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to reconcile discrepancies between charge dynamics measured by momentum-resolved EELS (finite momentum) and those from optical spectroscopy (local limit) in strange metals.

**Main result.** It proves that the proper bulk density response function must exhibit a universal $q^2$ asymptotic suppression at fixed frequency, which constrains how finite-$q$ measurements relate to the local optical conductivity.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Ward identity connecting the charge-density polarization ($\chi''_{hoho}$) to the longitudinal conductivity ($\sigma_L$), and then proves this $q^2$ scaling holds for any non-negative, self-similar momentum-resolution kernel.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on strange-metal cuprates like Bi-2212, treating the charge dynamics via the proper homogeneous bulk charge-density polarization function ($\chi''_{hoho}$).

**Key observables.** Proper longitudinal charge-density polarization $\chi''_{hoho}(q, \omega)$, local conductivity $\sigma_L(\omega)$, and the resulting crossover surface $q^*(\omega, T)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Momentum transfer ($q$), frequency ($\omega$), temperature ($T$), and the ratio of momentum-resolution kernel width to nominal momentum.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system is a homogeneous, U(1)-conserving metal, and that the measured quantity can be related back to the proper bulk response via electrodynamic conversion.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes; the focus is on deriving functional relationships (Eq. 6, Eq. 8) and proving asymptotic theorems.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the Ward identity linking $\chi''_{hoho}$ to $\sigma_L$, derives the required $q^2$ asymptotic behavior for the bulk response, proves this scaling is robust against realistic momentum-resolution kernels, and concludes by defining the crossover surface connecting different measurement regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Momentum-resolved electron energy-loss spectroscopy (EELS) on strange-metal Bi$_2$Sr$_2$CaCu$_2$O$_{8+x}$ (Bi-2212) observes a broad charge continuum that is nearly momentum independent over an extended finite-momentum regime, whereas optical spectroscopy determines a metallic local conductivity. We analyze the long-wavelength response function that connects these regimes: the proper homogeneous bulk charge-density polarization. For any homogeneous $U(1)$-conserving metal whose longitudinal matter conductivity remains finite in the $q\to0$ limit at fixed nonzero frequency, the Ward identity gives $χ''_{ρρ}({\bf q},ω)=q^2\operatorname{Re}σ_L({\bf q},ω)/ω$. We then prove that any nonnegative normalized momentum-resolution kernel with a self-similar $q$-scaled profile, uniformly bounded second moment, and tight second-moment tails preserves this $q^2$ asymptotic behavior, changing only the prefactor. Consequently, a nearly local finite-$q$ continuum in Bi-2212, if continuously connected to a regular metallic optical limit of the same proper bulk response, is naturally described by a crossover surface $q_\ast(ω,T)$. The theorem is a constraint on the proper bulk density response, or on a positive $q$-scaled convolution of it; screened loss functions and surface EELS observables require the corresponding electrodynamic conversion before the constraint is applied.

</details>

### [QSTAR: Quantum Selective Transfer with Adaptive Routing](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21411v1)

**Authors:** Saim Rehman, Nouhaila Innan, Muhammad Shafique  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21411v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

<img src="2607.21411_figures/2607.21411_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Fig. 1: Overview of the proposed QSTAR methodology.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QSTAR, a selective framework for Quantum Transfer Learning that routes samples to a quantum circuit head only if the initial classical prediction is low-confidence. The results demonstrate that this targeted approach significantly boosts accuracy by leveraging quantum computation where it is most needed, suggesting quantum heads are best used as specialized fallback tools rather than general replacements.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on machine learning, the concept of selectively engaging complex physical models (like quantum circuits) only when classical methods fail or are uncertain mirrors concepts in open quantum systems where specific environmental coupling or measurement protocols are only applied under certain conditions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The evaluation of Quantum Transfer Learning (QTL) often incorrectly assumes the quantum head replaces the classical classifier, obscuring when the quantum component is genuinely useful.

**Main result.** Selective routing, which only passes low-confidence samples to a quantum fallback branch, significantly improves performance over standard QTL methods and outperforms adaptive classical baselines.

**Method.** The authors propose QSTAR, a selective framework that uses sample confidence scores ($	au$) to route inputs: high-confidence data stays with the classical prediction, while uncertain data is passed to a parameterized quantum circuit head.

**Model / system.** The model utilizes a frozen ResNet18 backbone for feature extraction on Fashion-MNIST. The classification involves comparing linear classical heads, MLP fallbacks, and various architecture-searched quantum circuits (KetGPT-designed heads).

**Key observables.** Accuracy ($	ext{Acc.}$), F1-score, and the performance improvement of the quantum branch specifically on low-confidence samples.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The confidence threshold $	au$ used for adaptive routing; circuit parameters (e.g., 10 parameters, 9 gates) in ablation studies.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the utility of quantum heads is conditional on input uncertainty rather than being a universal replacement for classical methods.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the QSTAR methodology. Table I compares fixed-head transfer learning results, and Table II shows adaptive routing performance across different confidence thresholds ($	au$).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitation of standard QTL evaluation, proposes the selective QSTAR framework using adaptive routing based on sample confidence, presents quantitative comparisons against various baselines (classical/quantum heads), and concludes by arguing for the role of quantum circuits as targeted fallback mechanisms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum transfer learning (QTL) is often evaluated by replacing a classical classifier with a fixed variational quantum head, but this hides a key question: when is the quantum branch actually useful? We propose QSTAR: Quantum Selective Transfer with Adaptive Routing, a selective QTL framework that keeps high-confidence classical predictions and routes only low-confidence samples to a fallback branch. Using a frozen ResNet18 backbone on Fashion-MNIST, we compare manually designed QTL heads, KetGPT-designed quantum heads, and parameter-matched classical baselines under a common data split and optimization schedule. Standard QTL heads reach at most 57.0% accuracy, while the strongest KetGPT head in the main filtered sweep reaches 78.5% accuracy and 0.785 F1-score. Although the strongest fixed classical head remains higher at 81.6%, selective routing gives the quantum branch a clearer role. On low-confidence samples, KetGPT #180 improves accuracy over a parameter-matched MLP fallback by 6.82, 4.31, and 3.03 percentage points at thresholds of 0.70, 0.80, and 0.90. At the full-system level, Adaptive KetGPT-QTL reaches 80.9% accuracy and 0.807 F1-score, outperforming the adaptive classical baseline. A separate compact-circuit ablation identifies KetGPT #160 as a stronger fixed-head candidate, reaching 81.9% accuracy with only 10 quantum parameters and 9 gates. These results suggest that architecture-searched quantum heads are most useful as targeted fallback branches for uncertain inputs rather than uniform replacements for classical classifiers.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel, gauge-invariant path integral formulation for quantum gravity by introducing Quantum Reference Frames (QRFs). By formulating physics using 'relational observables,' it achieves manifest gauge invariance without explicit gauge fixing. The key finding is that physical measurements become frame-dependent—localizing an event in one reference frame makes it appear 'fuzzy' when viewed from another.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum gravity, the core concept of describing physical quantities relative to internal, dynamical reference frames (QRFs) shares conceptual parallels with developing gauge-invariant descriptions in open quantum systems or time evolution when the background geometry itself is fluctuating or ill-defined.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is formulating a manifestly gauge-invariant path integral for quantum gravity that resolves issues arising from diffeomorphism invariance.

**Main result.** The authors propose a relational path integral formulation using Quantum Reference Frames (QRFs) which yields an anomaly-free, gauge-invariant description of gravity.

**Method.** They develop the formalism by constructing the path integral in terms of 'relational' or frame-dressed observables, bypassing explicit gauge fixing procedures.

**Model / system.** The system is Quantum Gravity, specifically addressing the gravitational path integral. The approach centers on defining physics relative to internal, dynamical reference frames (QRFs) rather than fixed background coordinates.

**Key observables.** Relational observables ($\hat{O}^A_R$), the relational effective action ($\Gamma_R$), and frame-change kernels ($K_{R 	o R'}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The formulation is characterized by its covariance under arbitrary QRF changes, leading to 'fuzziness' in local correlators when changing perspectives.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction proceeds at a non-perturbative level of quantum gravity, and while gauge invariance is achieved, the relationship between dynamics across different frames requires specialized kernels.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 conceptually illustrates the difference between sharply localized events in one foliation versus 'fuzzy' (field-dependent) events when viewed from another QRF perspective.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of gauge invariance in gravity, proposes the relational bundle-geometric formulation using QRFs and relational observables, derives the path integral structure, discusses the implications for vacuum states and correlators (fuzziness), and concludes by defining frame-dependent effective actions for renormalization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a relational bundle-geometric formulation of the gravitational path integral by invoking the new tool of quantum reference frames (QRFs), which in gravity are gauge-covariant coordinate systems constructed from the available field content. Formulated in terms of relational (frame-dressed) observables, this yields a manifestly gauge-invariant path integral without ghosts and anomalies, and in which observables and their correlators are local to a frame. While eliminating the need for gauge fixing, it is equivalent to Faddeev-Popov versions in which the QRF is gauge-fixed, recovering certain previous proposals. A key feature is its covariance under QRF changes: it is a perspective-neutral path integral which encodes all internal QRF perspectives and the transformations between them. This leads to several qualitative predictions: local correlators and time evolution of relational observables in one QRF perspective become fuzzy in another, and a new spectrum of relational vacua arises. Comprised of frame-dependent no-boundary and asymptotic ground states, a vacuum from one perspective appears generally excited in another. Finally, we construct gauge-invariant, yet frame-dependent effective actions by coupling sources exclusively to relational observables, setting the stage for a relational definition of renormalization.

</details>

### [Representation of the causal logic for the Dirac system and the electron](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21266v1)

**Authors:** Domenico P. L. Castrigiano, Carmine De Rosa, Valter Moretti  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21266v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<sub>Figure 1: The achronal region ∆⊂Σ and its region of influence ∆′ = Σ′ ∩(J+(∆) ∪ J−(∆)) on the causal base Σ′. Here J±(∆) := {x+z : x ∈∆, z nonspacelike , ±z0 ≥0}.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a mathematically rigorous framework to define particle localization for the Dirac electron that strictly adheres to principles of relativistic causality. It constructs a covariant causal-logic representation by analyzing conserved probability currents and establishing connections between different mathematical definitions of localization. The work is significant for providing a consistent, projection-valued observable in relativistic quantum mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly technical, the rigorous treatment of relativistic causality constraints on position measurement is fundamental to understanding quantum field theory observables beyond standard QFT formalism.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is constructing a covariant, causality-respecting localization observable for relativistic quantum particles like the Dirac electron.

**Main result.** The authors construct a Representation of the Causal Logic (RCL) that provides a projection-valued measure satisfying microscopic causality and correctly reproduces known results in specific limits.

**Method.** The methodology involves defining achronal localization via conserved probability currents, utilizing non-stationary phase arguments, and establishing mathematical correspondences between different types of localization operators (AL $\iff$ RCL).

**Model / system.** The primary system is the Dirac equation describing relativistic fermions (the electron), analyzed in both full spacetime regions and restricted to the positive-energy subspace. The underlying mathematics involves Lorentzian geometry and causal set theory.

**Key observables.** Covariant achronal localization operators ($T(\Delta)$ or $F(\Delta\wedge)$), the position operator, and the conserved Dirac probability current.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The high-boost limit (Lorentz contraction) and the structure of achronal regions ($\Lambda$) defined by Lipschitz continuity are key parameters analyzed.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction relies on extending measures from local intersections to full $\sigma$-additive extensions, assuming certain decay estimates hold for the probability current.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds logically by first defining the mathematical framework of achronal sets and their associated projection operators (using geometric measure theory), then applying this machinery to construct the covariant localization observable for the Dirac system, and finally analyzing its properties in various limits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct a covariant representation of the causal logic for the Dirac system and the electron, based on the conserved Dirac probability current and a general current-to-localization procedure for achronal regions. A polynomial decay estimate, derived by a non-stationary phase argument, verifies the conditions required to define covariant achronal localization for the full Dirac system. Extending this localization to complete spacetime regions yields the corresponding causal-logic representation; restriction to the positive-energy invariant subspace gives the analogous construction for the electron. We establish several structural properties. On Euclidean space, Dirac localization agrees with canonical projection-valued localization, while its position operator differs from the Newton--Wigner operator by an explicitly determined bounded self-adjoint correction. Full Dirac achronal localization is projection valued and represents achronal separateness by orthogonality, thus satisfying microscopic causality. After compression to the electron subspace, it becomes positive-operator valued while preserving causality. We further extend known results from spatial to general achronal regions, prove separation and norm-one criteria, and analyze the high-boost limit, obtaining Lorentz contraction in a precise probabilistic sense. Finally, we discuss the electron--positron decomposition and the state transformation induced by position measurements, showing that measurement-induced positron production is universal and independent of the specific measuring device.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Uni-XAS, a unified machine learning framework designed to solve the challenging bidirectional problem in X-ray Absorption Spectroscopy (XAS). It overcomes limitations of prior methods by treating spectral and structural inference as a single cross-modal alignment task. The resulting framework achieves state-of-the-art performance across retrieval, forward prediction, and inverse structure generation on a massive dataset.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on materials science spectroscopy, the methodology heavily relies on advanced deep learning techniques—such as generative flow matching, cross-modal alignment, and manifold optimization—which are highly relevant to developing ML models for complex physical systems beyond standard quantum computing paradigms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing a unified machine learning framework for X-ray Absorption Spectroscopy (XAS) that can consistently bridge the gap between heterogeneous 1D spectral data and 3D local atomic structures.

**Main result.** Uni-XAS establishes a scalable, reproducible benchmark demonstrating strong performance in cross-modal retrieval, accurate absolute-spectrum prediction, and composition-conditional 3D structure generation.

**Method.** The authors propose reframing the bidirectional problem as a cross-modal alignment and conditional generation task using specialized components like XASLip for alignment and Permutation-Rectified Flow Matching (PR-Flow) for inverse inference.

**Model / system.** The system is X-ray Absorption Spectroscopy (XAS), which probes local atomic environments by mapping between 3D local coordination geometries ($\mathbf{G}$) and continuous spectra ($\mathbf{Y}$).

**Key observables.** XAS spectra (1D continuous sequences) and explicit 3D coordinates defining local coordination geometry.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The framework utilizes a large-scale benchmark of 328,839 structure-spectrum pairs; key metrics include MAE in physical eV for energy anchors and OT-RMSD for structural fidelity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Existing methods are limited by decoupling forward/inverse tasks or failing to resolve permutation ambiguity among identical atoms. The framework assumes a shared latent space can unify these modalities.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between conventional decoupled pipelines and Uni-XAS's unified approach, showing how the shared latent interface supports retrieval, RAG forward prediction, and conditional 3D local-structure generation.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces Uni-XAS as a framework, detailing XASLip for alignment, followed by formulations for anchored absolute-spectrum generation (forward) and Permutation-Rectified Flow Matching (inverse). It concludes with performance evaluation on the large benchmark.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

X-ray absorption spectroscopy (XAS) is a key technique for probing local atomic environments, yet learning based modeling must bridge two heterogeneous modalities: 1D continuous spectra and 3D atomic structures. Existing approaches typically decouple forward spectrum prediction and inverse structure inference into separate regression tasks, hindering shared representation learning. Moreover, severe permutation ambiguity among identical atoms often limits inverse modeling to coarse structure descriptors rather than explicit 3D structure generation. In this work, we present Uni-XAS, a unified benchmark and learning framework that reframes bidirectional XAS modeling as a cross-modal alignment and conditional generation problem. We first propose XASLip, an alignment recipe coupling a physics-aware spectral encoder with an absorberaware manifold optimization strategy to resolve fine-grained intra-element coordination variations. Building upon this shared latent space, we formulate forward prediction as anchored absolute-spectrum generation via retrieval-augmented decoding, effectively preventing physical scale collapse and energy drift. For the inherently ill-posed inverse problem, we introduce Permutation-Rectified Flow Matching, which integrates type-wise optimal transport into a continuous generative flow to provide a principled solution to ligand permutation ambiguity without relying on heavy high-order equivariant architectures. Evaluated on a largescale standardized benchmark of 328,839 structure-spectrum pairs, Uni-XAS demonstrates strong performance in cross-modal retrieval, accurate absolute-spectrum prediction, and composition-conditional 3D structure generation, establishing a scalable, reproducible, and protocol-consistent foundation for multimodal learning and standardized evaluation in scientific spectroscopy.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a novel deep reinforcement learning framework utilizing Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) to solve the challenging problem of Approximate Quantum State Preparation (QSP). By treating circuit construction as an sequential decision-making process, the agent learns to append gates optimally to approximate target states with high fidelity. This demonstrates a powerful ML approach for quantum circuit design across multiple qubit counts.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum circuit synthesis, a crucial component of near-term quantum algorithm development. The application of advanced ML techniques like PPO to navigate exponentially large physical search spaces is highly relevant for designing complex quantum protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is Approximate Quantum State Preparation (QSP), which requires finding a quantum circuit to approximate a target state, a problem whose search space grows exponentially with the number of qubits.

**Main result.** The proposed framework successfully achieved high-fidelity approximations ($10^{-14}$) for various predefined and random states up to 5 qubits by optimizing circuit construction.

**Method.** Deep Reinforcement Learning (RL) using Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) is employed. An agent iteratively appends gates to a Parameterized Quantum Circuit (PQC), guided by a custom logarithmic reward function that balances fidelity improvement and gate count minimization.

**Model / system.** The system involves $n$-qubit quantum circuits initialized at $|0angle^{\otimes n}$. The action space includes single-qubit rotations ($R_x, R_y, R_z$) and CNOT gates. Performance is tested on 2 to 5 qubits.

**Key observables.** Fidelity $F(\psi, \phi)$ between the target state $\psi$ and the current state $\phi$, and the total number of gates used in the resulting circuit.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required approximation error threshold is set to $10^{-14}$. Training involves hyperparameters like penalty factor $p=10$ and scaling coefficient $\lambda=10$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation noted is that performance degrades as the number of qubits increases due to the exponential search space growth, and the agent's action space might be incomplete (e.g., missing Hadamard gate).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the training evolution (gates vs. distance) for Bell states and random 2-qubit states, alongside results comparing the number of gates required for GHZ and W states.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces QSP as a hard problem, proposes using PPO/RL to solve it by sequentially building PQCs. It details the specific RL setup (reward function, action space), presents experimental results across 2-5 qubits for various target states, and concludes with performance analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, a quantum architecture search framework for approximate quantum state preparation (QSP) is proposed. QSP is a challenging task, since the search space grows exponentially with the number of qubits, making the identification of the optimal circuit non-trivial. To address this problem, deep reinforcement learning is employed through an agent based on proximal policy optimization. The objective of the agent is to identify the best possible approximation of the target state while simultaneously minimizing the number of gates used. At each step, the agent appends a new gate to the circuit and recomputes the fidelity between the approximated state and the target states. Various experiments have been performed from 2 to 5 qubits. Both predefined states, such as Bell, GHZ, W, and Dicke states, and completely random states are considered. The proposed framework is able to achieve approximation errors of $10^{-14}$.

</details>

### [Cautious optimism for deep parameterized quantum circuits](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21409v1)

**Authors:** Marie Kempkes, Elies Gil-Fuster, Carlos Bravo-Prieto, Aroosa Ijaz, Alissa Wilms, Jens Eisert, Evert van Nieuwenburg, Vedran Dunjko  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21409v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.21409_figures/2607.21409_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1. Test loss as a function of the number of parameters p for (a) MNIST-1D and (b) Fashion MNIST classification, and (c) a multi- dimensional synthetic regression task, for different training set sizes N. Vertical dashed lines indicate the predicted interpolation thresholds p = NK. In all three tasks, the test loss peaks close to interpolation and decreases again in the overparameterized regime, displaying a characteristic double descent profile. The shaded areas correspond to the standard deviation for ten independent experiment repetitions, each using independently sampled training data.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the generalization capabilities of parameterized quantum circuits by analyzing their scaling behavior with model size. Using advanced random matrix theory and spectral analysis, the authors prove that these circuits can exhibit a double descent phenomenon. This suggests that increasing circuit depth/parameters does not automatically lead to performance degradation in practice.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects concepts from classical overparameterized neural networks (double descent) to quantum machine learning models, providing theoretical grounding for the utility of very large, complex quantum circuits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is understanding how the generalization performance of parameterized quantum circuits (PQCs) scales with an increasing number of trainable parameters.

**Main result.** The authors show that gradient-based PQCs can exhibit a double descent phenomenon, meaning their test error does not necessarily degrade as model size increases past the interpolation threshold.

**Method.** The analysis combines rigorous theoretical derivations using add-one-in perturbation techniques and spectral properties of random matrices to bound the expected risk. These bounds are then compared against numerical results.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on data re-uploading PQCs, where the quantum state $ho(x; \vartheta)$ depends on input data $x$ and trainable parameters $\vartheta$. The performance is measured by minimizing an expected risk function.

**Key observables.** Expected risk ($L(\vartheta)$) vs. model size ($p$), test loss, and eigenvalues of sample covariance/Hessian matrices.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of trainable parameters ($p$), training set size ($N$), the ratio $p/N$, and the interpolation threshold ($p=NK$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on asymptotic limits ($N, p 	o \infty$) and several technical assumptions regarding spectral properties (e.g., Assumptions 1-7) and local minima existence.

**Figures summary.** Numerical experiments consistently observe the predicted double descent behavior across multiple datasets, showing test loss peaking near the interpolation threshold ($p=NK$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops theoretical lower and upper bounds on the expected risk using spectral analysis. It then demonstrates that these derived bounds exhibit a maximum at $p=N$ (or $p=NK$), confirming double descent behavior both theoretically and numerically.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A central challenge in quantum machine learning is understanding the scaling behavior of parameterized quantum circuits (PQCs). In particular, it remains unclear how their performance on unseen data changes as the number of trainable parameters increases. Prior works have derived formal generalization guarantees for quantum models, but it is well-known that many such results do not fully characterize generalization behavior in practice. In this work, we show that gradient-based PQCs can exhibit improved performance on unseen data as model size increases, displaying the phenomenon of double descent. This contrasts with the traditional view that larger models lead to degraded generalization. We provide analytical results rigorously underpinning this behavior by leveraging add-one-in perturbation techniques and spectral properties of random matrices. We support these results with numerical experiments on re-uploading PQCs across several data sets and training set sizes, consistently observing the predicted double descent behavior. While other obstacles on the path toward practical quantum machine learning remain, our finding that deeper parameterized quantum circuits do not necessarily exhibit degraded performance provides reasons for cautious optimism.

</details>

**Summary.** This work compares hybrid quantum-inspired CNNs (HQiCNN) against standard CNNs for medical image classification using emulated quantum circuits. The study finds that performance depends heavily on data availability and hyperparameters, demonstrating that both models can achieve high accuracy while providing comparable, anatomically plausible explanations of their predictions.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous benchmark for quantum-inspired machine learning models by comparing them against classical baselines under controlled conditions, addressing the question of genuine quantum advantage in practical AI tasks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether small, classically-emulated quantum circuit components can meaningfully enhance complex deep learning models for medical image classification compared to purely classical architectures.

**Main result.** No single architecture consistently dominates; HQiCNN shows gains in intermediate data regimes, while CNN excels with large datasets. Both methods prove capable of attending to anatomically plausible regions.

**Method.** A systematic comparison was conducted between a Hybrid Quantum-inspired CNN (HQiCNN) and a classical CNN by varying hyperparameters like dataset size, entanglement, and observable sets.

**Model / system.** The model uses an emulated quantum circuit component (EQC) with four qubits, parameterized by rotations ($R_X, R_Y, R_Z$) and CNOT gates, which replaces a dense layer in the CNN backbone. This approach avoids physical hardware limitations.

**Key observables.** $|SHAP|$IoU and $EMD_{pos}$ metrics are used to quantify the spatial overlap and similarity of model attention maps across different architectures and data regimes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Training set size (1000 vs. 30,000 samples), learning rate ($10^{-2}$ to $10^{-4}$), circuit entanglement status, and observable sets ($	ext{O}1, 	ext{O}2, 	ext{O}3$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quantum component is simulated using native PyTorch functions; the analysis relies on comparing performance under controlled hyperparameter variations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 6 compares explainability plots ($|SHAP|$IoU) for OCT data across different training sizes, showing consistent attention regions. Figure 6b relates image count to $EMD_{pos}$, demonstrating convergence of explanations with more data.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the HQiCNN vs. CNN comparison, details the model architecture and hyperparameter search space (Table 1), presents results on performance across varying datasets/hyperparameters, and concludes with an explainability analysis using SHAP metrics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Numerous studies have analyzed the use of hybrid quantum-classical convolutional neural networks as a promising alternative to classical deep learning. However, network components on quantum hardware impose fundamental limitations, while the scalability of quantum circuits leads to trainability issues. In this work, we investigate whether small, classically-emulated quantum circuit components can play a meaningful role within complex models, offering an alternative to purely classical convolutional architectures. To this end, we present a systematic study of the effectiveness of a Hybrid Quantum-inspired Convolutional Neural Network (HQiCNN) compared with a parameter-matched classical Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) that differs only in an intermediate dense neural layer. Both models are evaluated on two real-world medical datasets while systematically varying the different hyperparameters, ensuring a fair model comparison that is both dataset and hyperparameter independent. The results show that no architecture consistently dominates the other: the HQiCNN achieves its largest gains in intermediate-data regimes, whereas the CNN reaches the highest accuracies for the largest training sets in both datasets. Furthermore, removing entanglement produces comparable performance while enabling substantially better scalability of quantum simulations, and richer observable sets become beneficial only when sufficient training data are available. Finally, we propose two SHAP-based explainability tools for comparing the predictions between both models, $|SHAP|$IoU and $EMD_{pos}$ metric, to demonstrate that both architectures consistently attend to anatomically plausible regions. Thus, we provide a comprehensive benchmark showing that, under certain conditions, hybrid quantum-inspired models are an alternative that can offer benefits in practical tasks such as medical image classification.

</details>

**Authors:** Hrishikesh Belagali, Thomas Van Camp, R. Pradeep, Sourin Das, Namit Anand, Ryan LaRose

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel polynomial-time classical algorithm for calculating the ground state energy of unitary cluster Jastrow (UCJ) circuits. By efficiently propagating fermionic integrals through unitary transformations, it achieves results comparable to or better than current large-scale quantum hardware experiments. This provides a crucial theoretical benchmark for simulating strongly correlated molecular systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful classical benchmark tool that bypasses hardware limitations by offering an exact, scalable method to evaluate complex quantum chemistry circuits used in AMO physics simulations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing an efficient, polynomial-time classical algorithm to compute the energy of single-layer unitary cluster Jastrow (UCJ) circuits.

**Main result.** The authors presented a method that can simulate large-scale UCJ experiments in minutes on standard hardware and achieved lower ground state energies than previously reported quantum simulations.

**Method.** The algorithm uses Heisenberg evolution of the second-quantized Hamiltonian, propagating one-body and two-body integrals through unitary transformations using techniques derived from Löwdin's formula.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on simulating electronic structure in chemistry using the Unitary Cluster Jastrow (UCJ) ansatz, which is a quantum circuit inspired by coupled cluster methods for strongly correlated molecules.

**Key observables.** Ground state energy ($E_{	ext{UCJ}}$), compared against Hartree-Fock and CCSD results.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of orbitals ($N$), number of qubits (up to 160 in testing).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The algorithm computes only the expectation value (weak simulation) and is limited to single layers ($L=1$) due to complexity issues with higher layers.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 plots runtime scaling for Hydrogen chains; Figure 3 compares cumulative energy from different propagation methods against approximation levels.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops mathematical machinery to exactly update one- and two-body integrals under unitary evolution, culminating in a polynomial-time algorithm for the single-layer UCJ energy calculation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent experiments on quantum computers have challenged the limits of classical computation in chemistry, simulating ground states of strongly correlated molecules. Many of these experiments have utilized the unitary cluster Jastrow ansatz, a quantum circuit inspired by the unitary coupled cluster ansatz that can be tailored to current quantum hardware. Notably, the largest experiment in Sci. Adv. 11, 25 (2025) executed a quantum circuit with 77 qubits and 10,570 gates on an IBM quantum computer and performed classical post-processing with up to 6400 nodes on Fugaku to compute ground state energies better than Hartree-Fock. In this work, we present a polynomial time classical algorithm to compute the energy of any single-layer unitary cluster Jastrow circuit, independent of locality constraints for quantum hardware. Our algorithm can reproduce the largest experiment from Sci. Adv. 11, 25 (2025) in less than a minute on a laptop, and through circuit optimization enabled by fast simulation we achieve a lower ground state energy than the experiment.

</details>

### [Fault-tolerant quantum algorithms for simulating atomic nuclei](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21563v1)

**Authors:** James Benstead, Michael Garn, Neil Gaspar, Sean Greenaway, Angus Kan, Lloyd La Ronde, Chandan Sarma, Paul Stevenson  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21563v1>

The variation in Toffoli...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops and analyzes fault-tolerant quantum algorithms designed to simulate the complex Hamiltonians governing atomic nuclei. By providing resource estimates for both effective shell-model and no-core models, it establishes a crucial benchmark for applying quantum computing to nuclear physics. The research highlights the computational challenges specific to nuclear interactions compared to electronic structure problems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the feasibility frontier of applying quantum computation to fundamental physics problems outside chemistry, offering concrete benchmarks that guide future hardware development for nuclear structure theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop concrete applications for fault-tolerant quantum computers by simulating the complex structure of atomic nuclei.

**Main result.** The authors provide resource estimates for simulating nuclear Hamiltonians using both effective shell-model and no-core-shell-model approaches, noting that while some benchmarks are comparable to chemistry simulations, the full no-core model requires significantly higher resources.

**Method.** They construct quantum algorithms based on Quantum Phase Estimation (QPE) tailored for fermionic systems, utilizing techniques like Jordan-Wigner transformations and circuit optimization to estimate resource costs in terms of Toffoli gates and qubits.

**Model / system.** The system is the atomic nucleus, modeled by Hamiltonians derived from chiral effective field theory. These Hamiltonians include one-, two-, and three-body interactions, which are more complex than those typically used for electronic structure problems.

**Key observables.** Ground state energy estimation, quantum resource estimates (Toffoli gate count, qubit counts), and nuclear structure properties.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity scales with the number of particles and orbitals; specific parameters include $e_{\max} = 8$, $e_{2\max} = 16$, and $e_{3\max} = 8$ for EFT interactions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** They assume that quantum resource estimation is the primary goal, and they compare results across different Hamiltonian formulations (phenomenological vs. no-core).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares resource estimates for simulating the full Hamiltonian versus partitioning it by symmetry sectors ($T_z$ or $M$), showing that sector-wise simulation leads to redundancy and increased cost for no-core models.

**Paper structure.** The work progresses from establishing the need for new applications beyond chemistry, detailing the construction of quantum algorithms (QPE) for nuclear Hamiltonians, providing resource estimates for specific nuclei ($^{32}	ext{Mg}$, $^{219}	ext{At}$), and finally analyzing the efficiency of Hamiltonian decomposition strategies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

To maximize the value of fault-tolerant quantum computers, it is essential to develop concrete applications beyond well-established domains such as chemistry and condensed-matter physics. Here we construct and compile quantum algorithms to simulate the structure of atomic nuclei -- a topic that has received relatively little attention from the quantum computing community despite its similarities to the electronic structure problem in chemistry -- via effective shell-model Hamiltonians and no-core-shell-model Hamiltonians with three-body interactions derived from chiral effective field theory. Furthermore, we provide quantum resource estimates, in terms of Toffoli gate and qubit counts, for these algorithms, which, to our knowledge, are the first such estimates for fault-tolerant quantum simulation of atomic nuclei. Notably, the estimates for $^{32}$Mg and $^{219}$At shell-model Hamiltonians are comparable to recent estimates of Femoco simulations, a standard benchmark in chemistry. For no-core-shell-model Hamiltonians suitable for light nuclei (up to $^{40}$Ca or so), we find that resource requirements are significantly higher, suggesting that more bespoke strategies are required to make such simulations practicable. Throughout this work, we draw upon the similarities between nuclear and electronic structure problems, while also highlighting challenges that are specific to the former. We hope this work will spur long-term collaborations between the nuclear and quantum computing community with the ultimate goal of realizing useful nuclear simulations on quantum computers.

</details>
