**Summary.** This paper provides a detailed theoretical analysis of Quantum Key Distribution (QKD) performance when transmitting keys underwater. It simulates the finite key regime, accounting for realistic noise sources like ambient light and water attenuation. The study demonstrates that post-processing techniques like Classical Advantage Distillation can significantly boost achievable distances in noisy underwater environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum communication in complex, noisy media (underwater), which is a major open problem in quantum networking and requires advanced understanding of channel capacity estimation beyond simple loss models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper evaluates Quantum Key Distribution (QKD) performance over realistic underwater channels, moving beyond asymptotic bounds to consider the finite key regime.

**Main result.** Classical Advantage Distillation (CAD) can significantly improve the maximal supported distance for QKD underwater when ambient light noise is high, although protocol choice and detector efficiency are critical factors.

**Method.** The authors simulate two decoy state protocols in a finite key setting, incorporating detailed models for signal attenuation loss and background noise specific to underwater environments.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is quantum communication through an underwater channel using weak coherent sources. The model incorporates Poisson photon statistics, detector imperfections (dark counts, efficiency), and environmental factors like water type and ambient light.

**Key observables.** Secret key establishment rate ($\ell$ / total signals sent) and the maximal supported distance under various noise conditions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Attenuation coefficient ($\alpha$), background irradiance ($E_0$), detector efficiency ($\eta$), and the CAD block size ($C$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a weak coherent source, relies on specific mathematical models for attenuation (e.g., exponential decay), and treats post-processing steps like error correction and privacy amplification.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare key rates using decoy state BB84 protocols with and without CAD under varying environmental conditions; Tables summarize the maximal asymptotic distance based on detector efficiency, light level, and CAD block settings for different water types.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first detailing the physical models for signal attenuation loss over distance, then modeling background noise sources. It culminates in evaluating the protocol performance using finite key derivations, specifically analyzing the benefit of Classical Advantage Distillation (CAD) under varying environmental parameters.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum key distribution (QKD) allows for the establishment of a secret key between two parties, secure against computationally unbounded adversaries. Most experimental and theoretical research in this area, investigates the performance of QKD over fiber or free space channels. A growing body of work, however, has begun to investigate its performance in underwater scenarios. Here, we consider the realistic finite key scenario, and evaluate the simulated performance of two different decoy state protocols. We show that there are certain underwater channels where "simpler" QKD protocols outperform more complex ones. Finally, we also investigate methods to improve QKD performance underwater, specifically looking at classical advantage distillation, which we show can greatly improve the maximal distance supported by QKD underwater.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously establishes a complexity-theoretic threshold for Random Circuit Sampling under realistic depolarizing noise. By combining polynomial extrapolation with a monotonicity argument, the authors show that RCS remains classically hard to simulate up to a specific noise strength $\gamma = \Theta(\log n/(nd))$. This identifies this scale as the asymptotic boundary between quantum advantage and classical simulation feasibility.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical limitations of quantum advantage claims by quantifying how noise degrades computational hardness. For AMO/Open Quantum Systems researchers, understanding this boundary is crucial for designing fault-tolerant protocols or interpreting experimental results from noisy hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining the noise-strength boundary that separates regimes where Random Circuit Sampling (RCS) remains classically hard to simulate from those where it becomes efficiently simulable.

**Main result.** The authors establish that noisy RCS remains classically hard to simulate within an inverse-polynomial total variation distance for noise strengths $\gamma = O(\log n/(nd))$, unless the polynomial hierarchy collapses.

**Method.** The proof combines a low-degree polynomial extrapolation technique with a monotonicity reduction, transferring known ideal hardness results to the noisy setting without extra assumptions.

**Model / system.** The model is Noisy Random Circuit Sampling (noisy RCS) applied to $n$-qubit circuits subjected to standard local depolarizing noise of strength $\gamma$. The analysis relies on comparing the noisy output probability to its ideal counterpart.

**Key observables.** Inverse-polynomial total variation distance (TVD) between the true and simulated sampling distributions, and the resulting hardness/simulability boundary scale.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise strength $\gamma$, number of qubits $n$, and circuit depth $d$. The critical asymptotic scale is identified as $\gamma = \Theta(\log n/(nd))$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The result assumes the standard average-case \#P-hardness conjecture for ideal RCS. It also relies on combining its hardness bound with existing simulability results for layered, regularly connected architectures.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a complexity phase diagram showing the established hard region (red) relative to known efficient simulation regimes under i.i.d. depolarizing noise.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the problem of noisy RCS hardness and then develops the mathematical machinery using polynomial extrapolation and monotonicity reduction. It applies these tools to transfer the ideal hardness conjecture to a specific, non-trivial noise strength $\gamma^*$. Finally, it combines this result with known simulability bounds to pinpoint the transition scale.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Random circuit sampling (RCS) is a leading candidate for demonstrating quantum advantage, supported by strong complexity-theoretic evidence of hardness in the ideal setting and by rapid experimental progress to date. In practice, however, noise is unavoidable, and a central problem is to identify the noise-strength boundary between classically simulable and classically hard regimes. In this work, we establish an architecture-general hardness bound for this boundary for the standard local depolarizing noise of strength $γ$. Assuming the standard average-case #P-hardness conjecture for ideal RCS, we show that, for any circuit architecture satisfying this conjecture, noisy RCS on the same architecture remains hard to simulate classically within any inverse-polynomial total variation distance whenever $γ=O(\log n/(nd))$ for $n$-qubit circuits of depth $d$, unless the polynomial hierarchy collapses. Crucially, noisy-RCS hardness follows without any additional conjectural or architecture-specific assumption beyond those already entering the ideal-RCS hardness framework. Our proof combines a low-degree polynomial extrapolation with a monotonicity reduction showing that efficient classical simulation at one depolarizing noise strength implies efficient simulation at every larger strength. Together, these ingredients transfer the standard ideal-RCS hardness conjecture to sampling hardness at a prespecified noise strength. Finally, combining the convergence-to-uniformity result of Dalzell et al. [Commun. Math. Phys. 405, 78 (2024)] with our monotonicity reduction yields efficient classical simulation for $γ=ω(\log n/(nd))$ on layered, regularly connected architectures. Thus, wherever the two architectural settings overlap, this identifies $γ=Θ(\log n/(nd))$ as the asymptotic complexity-transition scale.

</details>

### [QuantumChain: Blockchain-Backed Quantum Federated Learning for Financial Fraud Detection](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21449v1)

**Authors:** Epameinondas Douros, Konstantinos Dalampekis, Nouhaila Innan, Ioannis Theodonis, Muhammad Shafique  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21449v1>

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2607.21449_figures/2607.21449_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QuantumChain, a novel framework that applies Quantum Federated Learning to solve financial fraud detection under strict privacy constraints. It combines hybrid quantum-classical neural networks with advanced security measures like homomorphic encryption and blockchain auditing. The results demonstrate that this secure, quantum-enhanced approach improves detection recall over purely classical methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** While applied to finance, the core mechanism involves embedding variational quantum circuits into a deep learning structure (HQNN) for computation, which is relevant to NISQ device applications in quantum machine learning theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses financial fraud detection, which is complicated by decentralized data, class imbalance, and strict privacy requirements.

**Main result.** The proposed QuantumChain framework successfully integrates hybrid quantum models into a secure federated learning pipeline, achieving improved fraud-class recall over classical baselines while maintaining stable global convergence.

**Method.** It employs a multi-layered security protocol combining Hybrid Quantum Neural Networks (HQNNs), homomorphic encryption, threshold secret sharing, and blockchain auditing for privacy and integrity.

**Model / system.** The core model is the QuantumChain framework utilizing HQNNs, which embed variational quantum circuits between classical neural layers. Security relies on QKD-derived keys and a permissioned blockchain ledger.

**Key observables.** Fraud-class recall (primary metric), global accuracy, and the Quantum Bit Error Rate (QBER) for security checks.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Federated rounds (e.g., 5 rounds), client count (10 heterogeneous clients), circuit depth (Shallow vs. Deep QLayer).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The blockchain layer is simulated using a local Ethereum/Ganache environment, and QKD security relies on pre-shared credentials or digital signatures for authentication.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overall QuantumChain process flow (local training to aggregation), compare Shallow vs. Deep QLayer architectures, and show performance results across different settings.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the architecture of QuantumChain, outlines the multi-step protocol involving local quantum training, secure update transmission via encryption/QKD, blockchain commitment, and final server-side aggregation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Financial fraud detection is challenged by decentralized data, severe class imbalance, and privacy constraints. This paper presents QuantumChain, a secure Quantum Federated Learning (QFL) framework that combines hybrid quantum-classical neural networks, encrypted federated aggregation, blockchain-based auditability, and quantum-secure communication. Each client trains a local hybrid model in which a variational quantum circuit is embedded between classical neural layers, while model updates are protected through homomorphic encryption, threshold secret sharing, and QKD-based keying. A permissioned blockchain records aggregation events and supports reputation-weighted trust among participants. We evaluate QuantumChain on financial transaction data using a compact, size-matched classical baseline to isolate the effect of the quantum layer. Results show that the HQNN achieves comparable accuracy while improving fraud-class recall in most settings, reaching 94.6% recall compared with 93.2% for the classical model. The Deep QLayer improves performance in full-data settings, suggesting that added circuit depth helps recover representational capacity when the shallow circuit becomes limited. Mixed-state simulations further show that the recall trend persists under non-ideal quantum evolution. In federated deployment with 10 heterogeneous clients, global accuracy increases from 97.7% to 98.8% over five rounds before stabilizing. These results show that QuantumChain can integrate depth-aware hybrid quantum models into a secure federated fraud-detection pipeline while maintaining stable global convergence.

</details>

### [Sp(4,Z) actions on 3d U(1)^2 symmetric theories: Order-five duality and bilayer quantum Hall hierarchies](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20622v1)

**Authors:** Yasin F. Alam, Andreas Karch, Da-Chuan Lu, Ryan C. Spieler  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.20622v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

The horizontal coordinate is the total charge response νc, and the vertical coordinate is the layer-imbalance response νd = νu −νℓ. </details>

<img src="2607.20622_figures/2607.20622_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 2. Layer-imbalanced plot in the (νc, νd) plane for (33n) + r(220) + (221) + p[2]u + q[2]ℓ, with n, r = 0, 1, 2 and p, q = 0, 1. The colors label the parent (330), (331), or (332). Within each fixed r slice, solid lines fix the upper scalar level p, dashed lines fix the lower scalar level q. Singular entries for which νd is not defined are omitted.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work extends electromagnetic duality symmetries from single-$U(1)$ theories to $U(1)^2$ symmetric quantum field theories, governed by $\mathrm{Sp}(4,\mathbb{Z})$. By identifying an order-five duality element, the authors successfully generalize the Haldane–Halperin construction. This framework predicts specific Abelian states in bilayer fractional quantum Hall systems at even-denominator fillings.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between high-rank symplectic dualities ($\mathrm{Sp}(4,\mathbb{Z})$) and concrete physical predictions for correlated electron states (FQH liquids) provides a powerful mathematical toolset for classifying topological phases in condensed matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work generalizes the electromagnetic duality structure from single-$U(1)$ symmetric theories to those with $U(1)^2$ symmetry, governed by the $\mathrm{Sp}(4,\mathbb{Z})$ group.

**Main result.** They identify an intrinsically two-component element of order five in the boundary action and apply this framework to predict candidate Abelian states in bilayer fractional quantum Hall systems at specific even-denominator fillings.

**Method.** The authors use the K-matrix formalism to organize theory-generating webs, recasting the Haldane–Halperin hierarchy construction as a sequence of generalized $\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})$ operations.

**Model / system.** The focus is on 3D quantum field theories with $U(1)^2$ global symmetry, specifically applied to bilayer fractional quantum Hall (FQH) systems and Abelian Chern-Simons theories.

**Key observables.** Candidate FQH filling fractions ($
u=3/8+3/8$, $
u=5/12+5/12$), duality group structure ($\mathrm{Sp}(4,\mathbb{Z})$), and the order-five duality element.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of $U(1)$ symmetries ($n=2$) and the specific filling fractions characterizing the correlated states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that integral changes of anyon basis establish equivalence between the derived theories and their composite-fermion descriptions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first generalizing the $\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})$ duality to $\mathrm{Sp}(4,\mathbb{Z})$, deriving boundary operations, identifying the order-five element, and finally applying this structure to construct and analyze bilayer FQH hierarchies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The $\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})$ electromagnetic duality of 4d Maxwell theory induces theory-generating operations on 3d theories with $U(1)$ global symmetry. For theories with $U(1)^n$ symmetry, this structure generalizes to $\mathrm{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{Z})$. Focusing on the $U(1)^2$ case, we formulate the bulk $\mathrm{Sp}(4,\mathbb{Z})$ action and derive the corresponding boundary operations. We identify an intrinsically two-component element of order five, which generalizes the order-three $ST$ element of the single-$U(1)$ theory. Although its fifth power acts trivially in the bulk, the corresponding boundary operation closes only up to a decoupled $U(1)_1$ invertible phase, suggesting a mixed duality-gravitational anomaly. We realize the resulting theory-generating web for Abelian Chern-Simons theories within the $K$-matrix formalism and apply it to bilayer fractional quantum Hall systems. Recasting the Haldane--Halperin hierarchy construction as a sequence of $\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})$ operations, we generalize it to systems with charge $U(1)_c$ and pseudospin $U(1)_s$ symmetries. The resulting bilayer hierarchies contain branches terminating in an interlayer-correlated bosonic $(221)$ daughter sector, yielding candidate Abelian states at the even-denominator equal-layer fillings $3/8+3/8$ and $5/12+5/12$. Integral changes of anyon basis establish the equivalence of these states to their corresponding Abelian composite-fermion descriptions. We further discuss the spin-charge constraints that arise when the electromagnetic background field is treated as a spin-$c$ connection.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents 'balanced fusion,' a novel technique designed to significantly reduce error rates when implementing analog logical rotation gates crucial for fault-tolerant quantum computing. By restructuring how resource states are prepared via randomized unitaries simulation, the authors improve the error scaling from $O(p\phi)$ to $O(p\phi^{1.5})$. This advancement enhances the viability of these rotations as a practical alternative to other synthesis methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete algorithmic improvement in resource estimation for fault-tolerant quantum computation, directly impacting the feasibility of running complex algorithms on near-term devices by reducing gate errors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to suppress errors when implementing analog logical rotation gates ($R_z(\phi)$) required for fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Main result.** The authors introduce 'balanced fusion,' a technique that improves the error scaling of these rotations from $O(p\phi)$ (baseline) to a superior $O(p\phi^{1.5})$.

**Method.** They analyze the accumulation of coherent errors in randomized unitaries simulation (RUS) processes, specifically by fusing resource states in a binary tree structure rather than sequential preparation.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on logical qubits implementing $R_z(\phi)$ gates using resource states $|M(2i-1\phi)angle$ and the Randomized Unitary Simulation (RUS) process. The analysis compares this to standard synthesis methods.

**Key observables.** Error scaling with respect to physical error rate ($p$) and rotation angle ($\phi$), specifically achieving $O(p\phi^{1.5})$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Physical error rate ($p$), target rotation angle ($\phi$), number of trials ($k$), and the structure of resource state preparation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on an improved understanding of coherent error accumulation, assumes access to a black-box protocol for preparing magic states $|M(\phi)angle$, and compares results against baseline RUS methods.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the balanced fusion process using a binary tree structure for resource state preparation. Figure 2 compares expected error accumulation between Balanced Fusion and Baseline approaches over iterations.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first identifying the limitations of existing $O(p\phi)$ scaling methods, then introducing and analyzing 'balanced fusion' to achieve $O(p\phi^{1.5})$, providing detailed mathematical bounds for this improved error suppression.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

There have been a number of recent proposals to use analog logical rotations in early fault-tolerant quantum algorithms. Existing proposals implement a logical rotation by angle $φ$, with error $O(pφ)$, where $p$ is the physical error rate. While this is not fault-tolerant, if $φ$ is sufficiently small the logical error rate can be suppressed. In this work, we introduce and analyze the `balanced fusion' technique for improving the error scaling of analog logical rotations to $O(p φ^{1.5})$, without having to resort to fallback synthesis. Balanced fusion is enabled by an improved analysis of the accumulation of coherent error terms in analog logical rotations. Our techniques improve the viability of analog logical rotations for small rotation angles as an alternative to cultivation-powered rotation synthesis.

</details>

<a id="paper-2607.21581"></a>
### [Symmetry and Quantum Geometry in Bloch Bands](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21581v1)

**Authors:** Ziwei Wang, Charlie Raca, Steven H. Simon  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21581v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.21581_figures/2607.21581_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1: The honeycomb lattice (a) and a decorated honeycomb lattice (b), where each A site is replaced by a trimer. C3 symmetry requires the trimer to be arranged symmetrically around the center of C3-rotation, but does not uniquely specify an embedding.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2607.21581_figures/2607.21581_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2: Two honeycomb lattices with an offset and bonds in-between. C3-symmetry for the entire lattice is only restored if the offset is zero.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates quantum geometric quantities derived from Bloch bands, which typically depend on both model parameters and the specific real-space arrangement of orbitals. The authors show that if the system possesses underlying spatial symmetries, the geometry that minimizes key measures like Berry curvature variance must itself obey those symmetries. This provides a powerful symmetry constraint for understanding quantum geometry in crystalline solids.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection drawn between fundamental physical symmetries (like spatial or magnetic translation symmetries) and the minimization of geometric invariants is a powerful tool for constraining theoretical models in condensed matter physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors aim to find an explicit, geometry-independent measure for quantum geometric quantities (like Berry curvature variance or integrated quantum metric) in tight-binding models.

**Main result.** They prove that if the underlying tight-binding model possesses certain spatial symmetries, then the real-space geometry that minimizes these quantum geometric measures must necessarily respect those same spatial symmetries.

**Method.** The work uses minimization arguments over the space of possible real-space embeddings (orbital positions) for a given lattice structure to constrain the optimal geometry.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Bloch bands described by tight-binding models, initially in two dimensions and generalized to include magnetic translation symmetries. The analysis involves optimizing quantities related to the Berry curvature and quantum metric tensor.

**Key observables.** Berry curvature ($\Omega$), Quantum metric tensor ($g_{ij}$), Integrated quantum metric ($I$), and Variance of Berry curvature ($\sigma^2_\Omega$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spatial symmetries, magnetic flux parameters (related to the Hofstadter model), and lattice geometry/orbital embedding $\{r_{a,i}\}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that a global minimum for the geometric quantities exists in the space of embeddings, and it extends symmetry considerations to include gauge transformations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the honeycomb lattice structure used as an example system.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of geometry dependence. It then details the mathematical setup using quantum geometric tensors ($Q_{ij}, g_{ij}, \Omega$). The core argument demonstrates that symmetry dictates the minimal geometry, extending this principle to magnetic and composite symmetries, concluding with implications for models like the Hofstadter model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum geometric quantities have featured heavily in the discussion of the properties of quantum systems in recent years. Among quantities most commonly discussed is the variance of Berry curvature and the integral of the trace of the quantum metric tensor. Despite their usefulness, it is known that they suffer from one significant complication: for a tight-binding model, the quantum geometric quantities depend not only on the parameters of the tight-binding model itself, but also the real space geometry of the tight-binding model, the so-called "orbital embedding". One explicitly geometry-independent quantity is therefore the minimal value of the quantum geometric quantity out of all possible real space geometries. In this work, we demonstrate that, if the tight-binding model is compatible with certain spatial symmetries, then the real space geometry that minimizes the variance of the Berry curvature or the integral of the trace of the quantum metric tensor must obey all those spatial symmetries. We further show that the statement is applicable to systems with magnetic translation symmetries and other composite symmetries, with implications for the quantum geometry of the Hofstadter model.

</details>

**Authors:** Valerii Chuiko, Paul W. Ayers, Eduard Matito  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21399v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper improves Reduced Density Matrix Functional Theory (RDMFT) by systematically correcting natural orbital functionals using 4-index cumulant contributions. The method enforces strict physical constraints derived from the asymptotic behavior of fragment spin and delocalization indices upon bond breaking. This leads to highly accurate energy predictions that match high-level CASSCF calculations in the dissociation limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses fundamental limitations in describing static correlation and bond dissociation within quantum chemistry methods, which is crucial for understanding molecular electronic structure dynamics relevant to AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing more accurate natural orbital functional approximations for Reduced Density Matrix Functional Theory (RDMFT) to correctly model bond breaking.

**Main result.** The updated functional, incorporating 4-index cumulant contributions and enforcing physical constraints, reproduces CASSCF energies exactly in the dissociation regime for benchmark molecules like N2 and O2.

**Method.** The authors correct the PNOF5 functional by minimizing the distance to a target cumulant while imposing linear constraints derived from known asymptotic behaviors of local spin and delocalization indices.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on electronic structure calculations using RDMFT, specifically analyzing the dissociation process of diatomic molecules (e.g., N2, O2) into two fragments.

**Key observables.** Bond dissociation energies, fragment population covariance (delocalization index), local spin angular momentum ($\langle \hat{S}^2 angle$), and cumulant elements ($\Gamma_{ij;kl}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dissociation limit ($|R_{AB}|	o\infty$) is the critical regime analyzed for benchmarking.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that enforcing physical constraints on the cumulants can systematically improve functional accuracy, although limitations exist when active electrons are small or far from dissociation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the iterative procedure flowchart and show how constraint violations (P, Q, G conditions) decrease towards convergence during molecular dissociation for various species.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by identifying the failure of existing functionals at bond breaking, proposing a constrained optimization method to correct cumulant contributions, demonstrating convergence through iterative projection onto N-representability, and finally benchmarking against CASSCF results across several molecules.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Accurate modeling of bond breaking remains a central challenge for reduced density matrix functional theory (RDMFT). Although some modern functionals can yield reasonably accurate dissociation energies, they often fail to reproduce key properties of the dissociated fragments, such as a vanishing fragment population covariance (also known as the delocalization index) and the correct total spin angular momentum of each fragment (local spin). In this work, we revisit the construction of natural orbital functionals by correcting the cumulant contribution produced by the PNOF5 functional. Our method enforces known contributions of the cumulant to local spin fragments and the delocalization index at the dissociation limit. We obtain the closest cumulant consistent with these physically motivated constraints and subsequently purify the corresponding one- and two-electron reduced density matrices by imposing the standard $P, Q, \text{and } G$ $N$-representability conditions. The resulting functional yields improved behavior in strongly correlated regimes. Benchmarking on the dissociation of the singlet states of \ce{N2}, \ce{NO+}, \ce{O2}, \ce{S2}, and \ce{CO} shows that in the dissociation regime the energies computed from the updated cumulant exactly reproduce the complete active space self-consistent field (CASSCF) energies. We further analyze the limitations of the approach and identify scenarios in which the current approach performs poorly. This work provides a pathway for systematically improving natural orbital functionals to achieve reliable bond-breaking calculations within RDMFT.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a statistical physics framework to measure the elasticity and shape of soft nanoparticles by observing their translocation through a nanopore. It models the process using Brownian dynamics across an elastic barrier, deriving distinct velocity laws for low- and high-drive regimes. The key innovation is combining fits from both regimes to create a self-calibrating measurement that bypasses unknown experimental constants.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of non-equilibrium statistical mechanics (Fokker-Planck formalism) to extract mechanical parameters from time-dependent current measurements is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems where dissipation and thermal fluctuations dominate the dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a mathematical framework that converts rate measurements of a soft particle passing through a narrow pore into quantitative mechanical characterization of the particle's deformation energy and geometry.

**Main result.** By combining measurements from both low-drive (activated) and high-drive (frictional) regimes, the method achieves a self-calibrating readout to determine the dimensionless deformation energy ($\Delta/k_B T$) without needing to know the unknown force-to-voltage conversion factor.

**Method.** The problem is solved exactly by analyzing the steady-state Fokker–Planck equation governing one-dimensional over-damped Brownian dynamics across an elastic barrier, identifying distinct dynamical regimes based on applied drive strength.

**Model / system.** The system models a deformable soft particle translocating through a pore using a 'funnel model' potential. The dynamics are governed by the Langevin equation, analyzed via its steady-state probability flux.

**Key observables.** Mean translocation velocity ($v$), deformation energy ($\Delta/k_B T$), funnel length ($L_f$), and crossover force ($\psi_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Deformation energy ($\Delta$), pore geometry parameter ($L_f$), applied driving force ($\psi$), and thermal energy scale ($k_B T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the drive is constant, the process can be reduced to a single reaction coordinate (one-dimensional dynamics), and initially models the elastic potential as piecewise linear.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the one-dimensional potential landscape, the master velocity curve showing distinct regimes (linear/activated), and phase diagrams mapping these dynamical regions in parameter space.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model via the Langevin equation, solves the resulting Fokker-Planck equation to derive analytical expressions for velocity in different drive regimes, and then proposes a dual-readout fitting procedure that combines linear (frictional) and exponential (activated) fits to eliminate unknown constants.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A soft particle driven through a pore narrower than itself must deform to pass, and how quickly it does so is set by how hard it is to squeeze. We propose a mathematical framework for turning rate measurements of this driven, stochastic passage into quantitative mechanical measurements. Treating the entry of the particle as one-dimensional Brownian dynamics across an elastic barrier, we solve the transport problem exactly and identify two dynamical regimes: at low drive the passage is thermally activated and limited by the energy needed to deform the particle, and at high drive it is friction-limited. We propose a framework to extract the particle's deformation energy and relevant geometrical information by combining measurements in these two regimes. This method could be used in the context of nanopore sensing, where the drive is an applied voltage: the framework then provides a self-calibrating route---translocation elastometry---from a current-voltage measurement to the elasticity and shape of individual soft nanoparticles.

</details>

**Authors:** Aris Koulas-Simos, Pietro Metuh, Athanasios Paralikis, Kartik Gaur, Maximilian Klonz, Imad Limame, Bárbara L. T. Rosa, Chirag C. Palekar, Battulga Munkhbat, Stephan Reitzenstein

**Summary.** The research demonstrates a novel all-van der Waals nanobeam laser built from $	ext{WS}_2/	ext{MoSe}_2/	ext{WS}_2$ heterostructures. By combining structural engineering with advanced photon correlation measurements, the authors provide strong evidence for high-$eta$ lasing operation in these layered materials. This establishes a promising platform for scalable quantum-photonic circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the intersection of solid-state physics, cavity QED, and nonlinear optics by using layered materials to create highly controllable, integrated quantum light sources.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate an all-van der Waals (all-vdW) high-$eta$ nanobeam laser and establish these heterostructures as a viable platform for integrated coherent light sources.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated lasing in the $	ext{WS}_2/	ext{MoSe}_2/	ext{WS}_2$ structure, verified by observing a transition to Poissonian photon statistics ($g^{(2)}(0) 	o 1.07$) and characteristic high-$eta$ nonlinear input-output behavior.

**Method.** The study combines microphotoluminescence ($\mu	ext{PL}$) spectroscopy, finite-element simulations for optical confinement, and crucial quantum-optical measurements using photon autocorrelation ($g^{(2)}$).

**Model / system.** The physical system is an all-vdW heterostructure ($	ext{WS}_2/	ext{MoSe}_2/	ext{WS}_2$) patterned into a nanobeam cavity, where the $	ext{MoSe}_2$ monolayer provides optical gain coupled to the surrounding $	ext{WS}_2$-based resonator.

**Key observables.** Photon autocorrelation function $g^{(2)}(0)$ (decreasing from 1.28 to 1.07), soft S-shaped input-output curve, linewidth narrowing, and strong directional/linear polarization of emission.

**Important parameters / regimes.** High-$eta$ parameter ($\approx 0.986$), threshold power ($P_{	ext{th}} \approx 9-29 	ext{ kW cm}^{-2}$), cryogenic temperatures (operating near $T_{	ext{eff}} \approx 60 	ext{ K}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The authors argue that conventional signatures ($	ext{I/O}$ curve) are insufficient, necessitating quantum measurements ($g^{(2)}$) for unambiguous proof of high-$eta$ lasing.

**Figures summary.** Figures show $\mu	ext{PL}$ maps confirming mode confinement and spectral overlap; subsequent figures detail the power-dependent $g^{(2)}(	au)$ measurements showing the transition to Poissonian statistics, and the temporal broadening analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from demonstrating basic exciton-cavity coupling via $\mu	ext{PL}$ (Fig. 2), establishing classical high-$eta$ signatures ($	ext{I/O}$ curve) (Fig. 3), and finally providing definitive quantum verification through power-dependent photon autocorrelation measurements (Section 5).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Transition-metal dichalcogenides offer a promising platform for integrated coherent light sources, yet lasing has largely relied on hybrid photonic architectures without direct quantum-optical verification. Here, we demonstrate an all-van der Waals (all-vdW) high-$β$ nanobeam laser based on a WS$_2$/MoSe$_2$/WS$_{2}$ heterostructure, with the MoSe$_2$ monolayer directly integrated in the WS$_{2}$-based optical resonator for optimal gain-mode overlap. The devices exhibit efficient exciton-cavity coupling at cryogenic temperatures, strongly directional and linearly polarized emission, soft nonlinear input-output characteristics and linewidth narrowing, enabling lasing operation with $β$ near unity. Excitation-power-dependent photon-autocorrelation measurements reveal a transition from thermal to Poissonian photon statistics, with $g_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(2)}(0)$ decreasing from $(1.28\,\pm\,0.09)$ near threshold to $(1.07\,\pm\,0.07)$ above threshold, directly verifying lasing operation. Furthermore, temporal broadening of the autocorrelation uncovers fluctuation-dominated lasing dynamics. These results establish all-vdW heterostructures as a highly attractive platform for integrated coherent light sources in layered-material photonic architectures and scalable quantum-photonic circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically examines Exceptional Points in a Josephson junction modeled by double quantum dots coupled to reservoirs. It shows that while simple dissipation eliminates predicted EPs, incorporating spin-dependent loss from a ferromagnet stabilizes these singularities. This highlights the potential for controlling non-Hermitian physics using engineered magnetic and spin interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems dynamics (dissipation) coupled with superconducting phenomena, providing a detailed theoretical framework for controlling non-Hermitian degeneracies using magnetic fields and spin interactions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the emergence and persistence of Exceptional Points (EPs) in a superconducting nanostructure subjected to dissipation and magnetic flux.

**Main result.** Spin-selective dissipation from a ferromagnetic reservoir can stabilize second-order EPs in the finite-gap regime, while Josephson currents remain continuous across these singularities.

**Method.** The analysis employs Green's function techniques, deriving effective Hamiltonians (including surrogate models for finite gaps) and performing parity-resolved Fock-space analyses.

**Model / system.** The system is a parallel double-quantum-dot Josephson junction coupled to reservoirs, modeled using the Bogoliubov-de Gennes formalism. The physics is governed by superconducting phase difference ($\delta\phi$) and orbital flux ($\Phi_B$).

**Key observables.** Complex Andreev spectrum eigenvalues (EPs), Josephson current ($I_J$), and energy splitting behavior.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superconducting gap ($\Delta$), magnetic flux ($\Phi_B$), spin-independent vs. spin-dependent dissipation rates ($\gamma_{\uparrow}, \gamma_{\downarrow}$), and the superconducting phase difference ($\delta\phi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis compares results in the infinite-gap limit versus a finite-gap surrogate model, noting that finite-gap corrections alter EP coalescence predictions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the complex energy spectrum showing EP2s for spin-independent loss (Fig. 2a) and persistent EP2s under spin-selective dissipation with flux tuning (Fig. 4a).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the model in different limits, comparing infinite-gap vs. finite-gap descriptions, analyzing how dissipation type affects EPs, and finally confirming the robustness of physical observables like Josephson current across these non-Hermitian singularities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate exceptional points (EPs) in a parallel double-quantum-dot Josephson junction coupled to a dissipative reservoir. By integrating out the leads, we obtain a non-Hermitian Bogoliubov-de Gennes description wherein the superconducting phase difference and orbital flux govern the complex Andreev spectrum. For spin-independent dissipation, the second-order EPs identified within the infinite superconducting gap limit are eliminated when the finite superconducting gap is properly incorporated. In contrast, spin-dependent dissipation originating from a ferromagnetic reservoir, in conjunction with magnetic flux, gives rise to second-order EPs that persist in superconducting leads with finite gap. Moreover, flux tuning enables the coalescence of two second-order EPs into a third-order EP, whose eigenvalue splitting exhibits cubic-root scaling behavior. A many-body parity analysis establishes the connection between the contrasting finite-gap behavior and the spectral relationship between the even- and odd-parity sectors. Finally, Josephson currents calculated from both the free-energy derivative and the surrogate-model density matrix demonstrate consistency and remain continuous across the EPs. These findings establish spin-selective dissipation and interferometric flux as effective control parameters for robust non-Hermitian singularities in superconducting nanostructures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents two specialized Convolutional Neural Networks designed to automate the analysis of Charge Stability Maps (CSMs) from isolated double quantum dots. By combining a quality classifier with a line-counting network, the authors created a highly accurate and computationally efficient pipeline for determining electron occupancy. This breakthrough offers a practical path toward scalable characterization and tuning of solid-state spin qubits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering bottleneck in scaling solid-state quantum computing platforms by providing an automated diagnostic tool for qubit characterization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The manual analysis of Charge Stability Maps (CSMs) for tuning spin qubits in quantum dot arrays is inefficient, especially in the isolated-mode regime.

**Main result.** A combined ML pipeline achieved high accuracy (93.8% on clean images) and demonstrated scalability by running fast (under 60 ms) with minimal memory footprint (6.5 MB).

**Method.** The authors developed two specialized Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs)—CSMClassifier and ChargeLineNet—to automate image quality screening and charge-transition line localization, respectively.

**Model / system.** The work uses Double Quantum Dots (DQDs) fabricated on SiMOS devices, operated in an isolated mode. The system is characterized by measuring CSMs via automated cryogenic probing at $\sim 1$ K.

**Key observables.** Charge-transition lines, electron occupancy derived from line counts, and image quality metrics (Clean/Unstable/Unclear).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating temperature ($\sim 1$ K), number of parameters (< 1 million for each model), and cross-device generalization performance.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the near-vertical nature of charge transitions in isolated mode makes them ideal targets for compact, task-specific CNN models; classical counting methods are insufficient due to artifacts.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the DQD device structure, a measured CSM showing transition lines, and potential/gate voltage diagrams used for characterization.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of automated qubit tuning via CSM analysis. It details the two-stage ML pipeline (classification followed by line detection) using CNNs trained on experimental data, reports quantitative performance metrics against held-out sets, and concludes with a discussion of the practical scalability of the combined tool.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Scaling semiconductor quantum dot arrays toward fault-tolerant quantum computing requires efficient tuneup of spin qubits, a process that depends on the analysis of charge stability maps (CSMs) and remains largely manual. While machine learning has been widely applied to CSM analysis in reservoir-coupled devices, automated tuning in the increasingly important isolated-mode regime has received limited attention. In isolated-mode CSMs, charge transitions appear as near-vertical lines, making them well suited to compact, task-specific models. We present two convolutional neural networks with fewer than one million parameters, trained on CSMs collected from 32 silicon metal-oxide-semiconductor (SiMOS) double-quantum-dot devices measured at approximately 1 K using an automated cryogenic probing system. Sixteen devices were used for training and sixteen were held out to evaluate cross-device generalization against hand-labeled ground truth. CSMClassifier identifies charge instability and sensor artifacts, achieving 94% macro-averaged accuracy across three quality classes on 2,407 held-out images. ChargeLineNet localizes charge-transition lines and determines electron occupancy, achieving 95.3% exact line-count accuracy on 1,131 held-out images. Combined into a single pipeline, the models correctly determine electron occupancy for 93.8% of clean held-out images. Pre-training on synthetic images substantially improves label efficiency. Fine-tuning the pre-trained model on limited experimental data maintains over 90% accuracy, whereas training from scratch degrades significantly under the same conditions. Together, the two models occupy only 6.5 MB and process images in less than 60 ms on standard laboratory hardware, demonstrating a practical path toward scalable, automated characterization and tuneup of quantum-dot devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically explores how the combination of real and imaginary bond modulations within a square lattice superconductor induces sequential transitions into topologically non-trivial superconducting phases. By calculating the Chern number and predicting quantized thermal Hall conductivity plateaus, the authors establish this platform as a robust method for engineering topological superconductivity.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection between engineered charge order parameters (CDW) and robust topological superconductivity provides a concrete theoretical pathway for realizing exotic quantum states, which is highly relevant to understanding unconventional pairing mechanisms in correlated materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates topological superconducting phases arising from the interplay between superconductivity and a composite Charge Density Wave (CDW) in a square lattice.

**Main result.** The coexistence of real bond modulation (CBO) and imaginary hopping modulation (CFP) drives the system into two distinct, topologically nontrivial superconducting phases characterized by Chern numbers $C=+2$ and $C=-2$.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis using the Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) Hamiltonian in momentum space, calculating topological invariants like the Chern number via the Kubo formula.

**Model / system.** The model is a square-lattice superconductor subjected to a composite CDW consisting of CBO ($\lambda_{	ext{CBO}}$) and CFP ($\lambda_{	ext{CFP}}$). The Hamiltonian is analyzed in the Altland-Zirnbauer class D, breaking time-reversal symmetry.

**Key observables.** Quantized plateaus in the zero-temperature thermal Hall conductivity ($\kappa_{xy} \propto C$), Chern number ($C$), and gapless edge modes observed via spectral function analysis.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative strengths of $\lambda_{	ext{CBO}}$ and $\lambda_{	ext{CFP}}$, chemical potential ($\mu$), and temperature (analyzed in the zero-temperature limit).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The superconducting pairing is assumed to be an isotropic s-wave form, and the analysis relies on diagonalizing the BdG Hamiltonian.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically show the real-space CDW pattern (CBO/CFP) and illustrate topological phase diagrams showing transitions between $C=-2, 0, 2$ phases as parameters are varied.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the Hamiltonian incorporating CBO and CFP, analyzing the resulting band structure evolution through different parameter regimes, determining the topological invariants ($C$) at critical points via gap closing mechanisms (quadratic vs. linear touching), and finally predicting observable signatures like quantized $\kappa_{xy}$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The interplay between charge order and superconductivity offers a fertile ground for emergent quantum phases. Here we theoretically investigate a square-lattice superconductor coexisting with a composite charge density wave (CDW) consisting of a real bond modulation (charge bond order, CBO) and an imaginary hopping modulation (chiral flux phase, CFP) that breaks time-reversal symmetry. We uncover that, while CFP alone does not induce topology in square lattices, its coexistence with CBO drives the system into two topologically nontrivial superconducting phases with Chern numbers $C=+2$ and $C=-2$. The low-temperature thermal Hall conductivity $κ_{xy}$ exhibits quantized plateaus proportional to the Chern number, providing a clear experimental fingerprint. Our results establish the square lattice as a pristine platform for engineering topological superconductivity through the synergy of real and imaginary bond modulations.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates that supercurrents flowing in a CDW-intertwined superconductor like $	ext{NbSe}_2$ can actively control the symmetry of the charge density wave. By applying an in-plane magnetic field, the system undergoes a measurable phase transition ($C_{3v}$ to $C_s$) observable through spectroscopic measurements and confirmed by theoretical modeling.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an example of how external electromagnetic fields can be used as a tool to selectively tune quantum ground states (CDW order) by coupling to superconducting excitations, which is highly relevant for designing novel electronic materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how supercurrents, specifically Meissner currents induced by an in-plane magnetic field, modify the electronic structure of superconductors that are intertwined with Charge Density Wave (CDW) order.

**Main result.** The applied supercurrent induces a Doppler shift on Bogoliubov quasiparticles, which drives a symmetry breaking transition in the CDW modulation from $C_{3v}$ to $C_s$ within the superconducting gap. This demonstrates momentum-space engineering control over emergent electronic phases.

**Method.** The work combines experimental scanning tunneling spectroscopy (STS) measurements with theoretical modeling using tight-binding Hamiltonians and Bogoliubov-de Gennes equations incorporating Peierls substitution for supercurrent effects.

**Model / system.** The physical system is superconducting $	ext{NbSe}_2$, which exhibits both s-wave superconductivity ($	ext{T}_{	ext{c}} \approx 7.2	ext{ K}$) and a $3	imes3$ CDW phase. The model incorporates hopping terms, the CDW potential, and pairing potentials into the BdG Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Bogoliubov quasiparticle spectra (coherence peaks), CDW modulation symmetry ($C_{3v}$ vs $C_s$), field-dependent peak intensities in Fourier Transforms of LDOS maps.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Applied magnetic field strength and direction ($\Gamma-	ext{M}, \Gamma-	ext{K}$ directions); energy window within the superconducting gap ($0.2-0.6 	ext{ meV}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The authors assume that surface-vortex contributions are negligible during measurements, allowing the field effect to be attributed directly to the interplay between CDW order and Bogoliubov quasiparticles.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate CDW modulation patterns (real space/FFT) under applied fields, showing symmetry breaking ($C_{3v} 	o C_s$) in the superconducting gap region. Band structures and constant-energy contours confirm the field dependence of the electronic states.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework by coupling CDW order to superconductivity via a Hamiltonian. It then presents experimental evidence using STS/STS mapping under varying magnetic fields, correlating observed symmetry breaking in the CDW modulation with Doppler shifts calculated from quasiparticle spectra and model calculations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The energy-momentum (E-k) dispersion of quasiparticles constitutes a fundamental concept in condensed matter systems. The ability to modify the E-k dispersion, exemplified by supercurrent-induced Doppler shifts of Bogoliubov quasiparticle spectra in superconductors, enables manipulation of various emergent quantum properties. However, investigations into the supercurrent effect on superconductors intertwined with charge orders remain scarce. Here, we report that the Meissner current, generated by the diamagnetic response to an applied in-plane magnetic field, can tailor Bogoliubov quasiparticle excitations at the precursor charge density wave (CDW) vectors. Our scanning tunneling spectroscopic imaging reveals a field-driven symmetry breaking of CDW modulations, specifically a C3v-to-Cs transition, in superconducting NbSe2. Model calculations suggest that the observed anisotropy originates from a selective Doppler-shift-induced E-k dispersion reconstruction. Furthermore, altering the field direction enables on-demand tuning of anisotropic CDW modulations and visualization of their momentum-space distribution. These results highlight a novel mechanism for controlling emergent electronic phases through momentum-space engineering.

</details>

Blue curve shows the calculated conductance spectrum on bare NbSe2 (SC). c, Illustration of the moir´e modulation used for the theoretical results, and the calculated LDOS maps for spin spiral and ferromagnetic ground states for a triangular NiI2 island. d, STM image showing the edge contrast and its registry with the moir´e pattern (scale bar 4...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that coupling the multiferroic $	ext{NiI}_2$ monolayer to a superconducting $	ext{NbSe}_2$ substrate drives a fundamental change in its magnetic state, forcing it from a helimagnetic to a ferromagnetic configuration. This control is experimentally verified by observing characteristic Yu-Shiba-Rusinov states using low-temperature scanning tunneling spectroscopy.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to use superconducting proximity effects (a quantum phenomenon) to tune magnetic ordering in 2D materials provides a powerful platform for realizing controllable quantum magnets relevant to spintronics and topological physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to demonstrate experimental control over magnetic order, specifically driving a transition from a helimagnetic to a ferromagnetic state in monolayer $	ext{NiI}_2$ using proximity effects from a superconducting substrate.

**Main result.** The coupling of $	ext{NiI}_2$ to the $	ext{NbSe}_2$ substrate renormalizes exchange interactions, inducing a robust ferromagnetic ground state confirmed by spectroscopic signatures.

**Method.** The research combines low-temperature Scanning Tunneling Microscopy/Spectroscopy ($	ext{STM}/	ext{STS}$) with theoretical modeling using Bogoliubov-de-Gennes Hamiltonians to predict and interpret the electronic states.

**Model / system.** The system is a van der Waals heterostructure of monolayer $	ext{NiI}_2$ (a multiferroic) placed on a superconducting $	ext{NbSe}_2$ substrate. The physics involves coupling magnetic exchange interactions ($H_J$) to the intrinsic $s$-wave superconductivity ($H_{SC}$).

**Key observables.** Yu-Shiba-Rusinov ($	ext{YSR}$) states within the superconducting gap, reduction of coherence peaks in $	ext{STS}$ spectra, and spatial patterns of Local Density of States ($	ext{LDOS}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperature (e.g., $350 	ext{ mK}$), proximity coupling strength, and the transition between helimagnetic and ferromagnetic magnetic order.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed $	ext{YSR}$ states are directly attributable to the substrate-induced renormalization of the magnetic ground state in $	ext{NiI}_2$.

**Figures summary.** Figures show atomic structure, $	ext{STS}$ spectra comparing bare and coupled systems (showing gap filling/$	ext{YSR}$ bands), and theoretical predictions for $	ext{LDOS}$ patterns corresponding to different magnetic states.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical system ($	ext{NiI}_2/	ext{NbSe}_2$), uses theory ($	ext{DFT}/	ext{BdG}$) to predict the transition from spin spiral to ferromagnetic state, and validates this prediction using low-temperature $	ext{STS}$ measurements focusing on $	ext{YSR}$ states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multiferroicity arising from non-collinear spin textures and strong spin-orbit interactions offers a route to magnetoelectric functionality in the monolayer limit. Although theory predicts that the properties of monolayer multiferroics can be tuned by strain, gating, or proximity effects, experimental demonstrations of such control remain scarce. Here we show that the magnetic ground state of monolayer NiI$_2$, a prototypical two-dimensional multiferroic, is altered by proximity to a superconducting NbSe$_2$ substrate. Using low-temperature scanning tunnelling microscopy (STM) and spectroscopy (STS), we show that the metallic substrate renormalizes the exchange interactions within NiI$_2$ and drives it into a ferromagnetic ground state. This can be visualized by probing the Yu-Shiba-Rusinov (YSR) states within the superconducting gap of the NbSe$_2$ substrate. Our results establish YSR states as an in situ probe of two-dimensional magnetism and demonstrate substrate engineering as a means of controlling magnetic order in atomically thin materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study calculates the Tunnel Magnetoresistance (TMR) effect in a doped altermagnet $\mathrm{Ru}_{1-x}\mathrm{Cr}_x\mathrm{O}_2(110)$ junction. Using DFT, the authors demonstrate that a finite TMR signal emerges due to spin polarization originating from the crystal structure and interfacial effects. This work advances understanding of spintronics in antiferromagnetic oxides.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the calculation of electronic transport properties (TMR) using first-principles methods is foundational for understanding quantum phenomena in solid-state devices, which has parallels with open quantum system modeling.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the Tunnel Magnetoresistance (TMR) effect occurring in antiferromagnetic tunnel junctions using doped altermagnets.

**Main result.** A finite TMR effect is predicted and calculated for $\mathrm{Ru}_{1-x}\mathrm{Cr}_x\mathrm{O}_2(110)/\mathrm{TiO}_2(110)/\mathrm{Ru}_{1-x}\mathrm{Cr}_x\mathrm{O}_2(110)$ junctions, attributed to momentum-dependent spin polarization and interfacial magnetic structures.

**Method.** The research employs first-principles calculations (DFT) combined with the scattering theory method and Landauer–Büttiker formalism to model tunneling transport.

**Model / system.** The system is a Magnetic Tunnel Junction (MTJ) composed of $\mathrm{Ru}_{1-x}\mathrm{Cr}_x\mathrm{O}_2(110)$ altermagnets separated by a $\mathrm{TiO}_2(110)$ barrier. The material properties are derived from first-principles calculations using the virtual crystal approximation for Cr doping.

**Key observables.** Total spin polarization ($p_{	ext{tot}}$), momentum-dependent spin polarization $p(\mathbf{k}_{\parallel})$, total transmission ($\mathrm{T}_{	ext{P}}, \mathrm{T}_{	ext{AP}}$), and the TMR ratio.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cr doping concentration ($x$), crystal orientation ($(110)$ vs $(001)$), and Fermi energy ($E_{	ext{F}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the TMR effect arises from a combination of bulk spin-polarized current, momentum dependence, and interfacial magnetic structures.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structures for different orientations, show how total spin polarization changes with Cr concentration, map out the momentum dependence of spin polarization, and plot the calculated TMR ratio versus doping concentration and barrier thickness.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical basis by showing that the $(110)$ orientation allows for a finite bulk spin-polarization in the altermagnet. It then applies this to model the MTJ structure, calculating the TMR effect based on tunneling transport theory and analyzing its dependence on doping and barrier thickness.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Antiferromagnets can have a finite spin-polarization in the momentum space when their magnetic structure breaks the macroscopic time-reversal symmetry. This spin-polarization can produce a spin-polarized electric current even in antiferromagnets with vanishingly small net magnetizaton, which supports the antiferromagentic tunnel magnetoresistance (TMR) effect. In this paper, using first-principles calculations, we study the TMR effect with a doped altermagnet $\mathrm{Ru}_{1-x}\mathrm{Cr}_{x}\mathrm{O}_{2}$ with $(110)$ orientation, whose collinear antiferromagnetic structure breaks the time-reversal symmetry macroscopically. The momentum-dependent spin-polarization combined with the $(110)$ crystal orientation makes the electric current spin-polarized through bulk $\mathrm{Ru}_{1-x}\mathrm{Cr}_{x}\mathrm{O}_{2}(110)$. We further calculate the TMR effect in the $\mathrm{Ru}_{1-x}\mathrm{Cr}_{x}\mathrm{O}_{2}(110)/\mathrm{TiO_{2}}(110)/\mathrm{Ru}_{1-x}\mathrm{Cr}_{x}\mathrm{O}_{2}(110)$ tunnel junction and show that a finite TMR effect emerges. Based on the analysis of the tunneling transport, the TMR effect is attributed to the spin polarized tunneling transport with momentum dependence and the interfacial magnetic structures, as well as the spin-polarized electric current in a bulk form of $\mathrm{Ru}_{1-x}\mathrm{Cr}_{x}\mathrm{O}_{2}(110)$.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses STM/STS on $	ext{CrSBr}$ to investigate intrinsic defects, finding two distinct defect types. By comparing these experimental observations with DFT simulations, the authors propose that the Type 2 defect is structurally equivalent to a $b$-axis-aligned double sulfur vacancy ($	ext{V}_2	ext{S}$). This advances understanding of how native defects dictate the electronic structure and potential optical properties of layered semiconductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily condensed matter physics, the detailed mapping of localized electronic states induced by vacancies provides a model system for studying how point defects modify band edges and potentially enable tunable emission in 2D materials, relevant to quantum emitters.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand the microscopic structure, electronic properties, and nature of intrinsic defects within the layered magnetic semiconductor $	ext{CrSBr}$.

**Main result.** Experimental STM/STS measurements strongly suggest that a common defect corresponds to a $b$-axis-aligned double sulfur vacancy ($	ext{V}_2	ext{S}$), which is supported by qualitative agreement with DFT simulations.

**Method.** The research combines Scanning Tunneling Microscopy/Spectroscopy (STM/STS) experiments at atomic resolution with first-principles Density Functional Theory ($	ext{DFT}+	ext{U}$) calculations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is $	ext{CrSBr}$, a layered magnetic semiconductor. The analysis focuses on characterizing defect states by comparing experimental electronic signatures (dI/dV maps) against theoretical predictions for various vacancy types.

**Key observables.** Electronic features near the valence band edge, localized electronic states centered on defects, and topographic contrast changes observed across varying bias voltages.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The material exhibits a bulk band gap of $1.7 \pm 0.3 	ext{ eV}$. Key defect candidates include $b$-axis-aligned double sulfur vacancies ($	ext{V}_2	ext{S}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The comparison between simulated and experimental STM signatures is qualitative, and $	ext{DFT}+	ext{U}$ calculations are acknowledged to be approximations for weakly screened semiconductors.

**Figures summary.** Figures show bulk crystal structure, topographic maps identifying two defect types (Type 1 and Type 2), and comparisons of calculated band structures/simulated STM images against experimental measurements on the Type 2 defect.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically uses STM to map defects, analyzes electronic signatures via STS across bias ranges, and then employs DFT calculations to model specific vacancy types ($	ext{V}_2	ext{S}$) for direct comparison with the observed defect states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Chromium sulfur bromide (CrSBr) is a layered magnetic semiconducting material combining a rich magnetic phase diagram with axis-dependent electronic and optical properties. While defects in CrSBr have been shown to affect magnetic order and excitonic responses, their microscopic nature, atomic structure, and electronic properties are not yet fully understood. In this work, we use scanning tunneling microscopy/spectroscopy (STM/STS) to explore the structure and electronic signatures of two prominent defects in bulk CrSBr. Their structure reflects the symmetries of the underlying lattice, with electronic features near the valence band edge. By comparing experimental data with ab initio simulated STM images, we infer that a common defect corresponds to a b-axis-aligned double sulfur vacancy, in line with findings from a recent growth analysis study. This result advances our understanding of the role of intrinsic defects in shaping the electronic structure of CrSBr.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors fabricated superconducting multilayer resonators incorporating ferromagnetic layers to create magnetic memory elements. They experimentally demonstrated a non-volatile shift in the resonant frequency upon reversing the magnetization of the Co layers, confirming a spin-valve effect. This work establishes these structures as promising components for building tunable inductive circuits for neuromorphic and quantum computing applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges superconducting electronics with spintronics, creating tunable inductive elements whose state is controlled magnetically—a key concept for implementing artificial synapses in quantum/neuromorphic computing architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to demonstrate a non-volatile spin-valve effect on superconducting multilayer resonators, establishing them as viable field-programmable inductive elements for neuromorphic circuits.

**Main result.** A reproducible shift in the resonant frequency ($\Delta f$ up to 4 MHz) was observed upon magnetization reversal at zero applied magnetic field, confirming the magnetic memory of the structure.

**Method.** Microwave characterization using transmission coefficient measurements ($S_{21}(f)$) on fabricated split-ring resonators (SRRs).

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a superconducting multilayer resonator ($	ext{Nb}/	ext{Co}/	ext{Nb}/	ext{Co}/	ext{Nb}/	ext{Al}$) utilizing superconductor-ferromagnet hybrid structures. The physics is modeled using the Usadel equations.

**Key observables.** Resonant frequency shift ($\Delta f$), internal quality factor ($Q_i$), and kinetic inductance contrast between parallel and antiparallel magnetic states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating temperature (2.5 K), resonant frequency range (4–7 GHz), and the magnitude of the frequency shift ($\sim 4 	ext{ MHz}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed effects are governed by changes in kinetic inductance due to magnetic state, and that the inclusion of an Al overlayer improves reproducibility.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate temperature dependence ($T$ vs $f_{	ext{res}}, Q_l$) and field dependence ($B$ vs $S_{21}, f_{	ext{res}}$), highlighting FMR anticrossings, sharp jumps at low fields, and the memory effect upon controlled magnetization reversal.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from demonstrating the basic spin-valve effect via frequency shifts to quantifying the enhancement provided by an aluminum shunt layer, comparing results against theoretical models (Usadel equations) and control samples.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconductor-ferromagnet hybrid structures with tunable kinetic inductance are promising elements for neuromorphic and quantum computing circuits. We report the fabrication and microwave characterization of split-ring resonators based on Nb/Co/Nb/Co/Nb/Al spin-trigger multilayers and demonstrate a non-volatile spin-valve effect on their resonant properties. Reversal of the relative magnetization orientation of the cobalt layers produces a reproducible shift of the resonant frequency up to 4 MHz at zero applied magnetic field, corresponding to a change in the kinetic inductance of the structure. The incorporation of a proximitized aluminum overlayer is shown to enhance the inductance contrast between the parallel and antiparallel magnetic states by a factor of approximately three relative to structures without this layer. The experimental results are in quantitative agreement with a microscopic model based on the Usadel equations. The demonstrated magnetic memory of the resonant frequency at zero field establishes spin-trigger multilayers as viable field-programmable inductive elements for superconducting digital and neuromorphic circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors propose using finite-momentum circular phonon dichroism (CPD) as a highly sensitive and direct diagnostic tool to determine the orientation of the Néel vector in $d$-wave altermagnets. By exploiting symmetry constraints, they show that CPD's sign reverses when the magnetic order is reversed, offering a practical acoustic method for characterizing these complex antiferromagnetic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects macroscopic acoustic measurements (ultrasound) directly to microscopic magnetic order parameters ($\mathbf{N}$), providing a novel, non-magnetic readout mechanism relevant for spintronics applications in correlated materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a direct, measurable technique to determine the orientation of the Néel vector ($\mathbf{N}$) in altermagnets because their magnetic order is otherwise difficult to read out.

**Main result.** Finite-momentum circular phonon dichroism (CPD) acts as a direct and sign-sensitive probe for the Néel vector, showing that the signal reverses when $\mathbf{N}$ is flipped.

**Method.** The theory calculates CPD by analyzing the difference between left ($\gamma_L$) and right ($\gamma_R$) circular ultrasound amplitudes, relating it to the Hall-like component of the electron-phonon coupling tensor ($\eta_{	ext{CPD}} = -\gamma_A / \gamma_D$).

**Model / system.** The study focuses on two-dimensional ($d$-wave) altermagnets. The electronic structure is modeled using a minimal two-band Hamiltonian incorporating $d$-wave exchange splitting ($J$) and Rashba spin-orbit coupling ($\lambda$).

**Key observables.** Circular phonon dichroism asymmetry ratio ($\eta_{	ext{CPD}}$), which is expected to change sign upon Néel vector reversal.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The magnitude of the dichroic signal, exemplified by $|\eta_{	ext{CPD}}| = 37.3\%$, and the dependence on phonon wave vectors ($q$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on combining Onsager reciprocity with $C_{2z}$ lattice symmetry to predict the sign change of CPD upon Néel vector reversal.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the concept by showing unequal transmitted amplitudes ($\gamma_L 
eq \gamma_R$) for opposite $\mathbf{N}$ orientations, and quantify the dichroic asymmetry ratio across various momentum cuts.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a theoretical framework using electron-phonon coupling to define CPD. It derives symmetry constraints based on crystal and time-reversal symmetries, calculates the signal originating from interband transitions, and presents quantitative results confirming its utility as an orientation probe.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnets, a new class of collinear antiferromagnets, exhibit momentum-dependent spin splitting and offer compelling advantages for antiferromagnetic spintronics. However, the magnetic order is intrinsically difficult to read out, which hinders practical applications. We propose finite-momentum circular phonon dichroism as a direct probe of Néel vector in two-dimensional $d$-wave altermagnets. Combining Onsager reciprocity with $C_{2z}$ lattice symmetry, we find that the dichroic signal reverses sign when the Néel vector is flipped for the in-plane phonon wave vectors. Moreover, a channel-resolved decomposition identifies the circular phonon dichroism originates from the interband coherent transitions. Representative finite-momentum cuts show pronounced dichroic asymmetric ratio, with $|η_{\mathrm{CPD}}|=37.3\%$. Our work reveals that the circular-ultrasound absorption acts as a direct probe of the Néel vector of $d$-wave altermagnets.

</details>
