**Summary.** This work experimentally characterizes NiBi3 as a type-II superconductor by measuring its magnetic response and penetration depth. By fitting the temperature dependence of these quantities to theoretical models, the authors provide compelling evidence that the material possesses a fully opened superconducting gap with s-wave pairing symmetry. The results confirm its nature as a BCS-like superconductor in the dirty limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter superconductivity, the rigorous application of BCS theory and gap analysis provides a model for understanding superconducting pairing mechanisms that can be conceptually related to strongly interacting quantum systems studied in AMO physics or ultracold atomic gases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the physical characteristics, normal state, and superconducting properties of NiBi3 single crystals to determine its pairing symmetry.

**Main result.** The analysis strongly indicates that NiBi3 is a type-II, BCS-like superconductor exhibiting fully gapped s-wave superconductivity with moderately strong coupling. The gap structure rules out nodal superconductivity.

**Method.** Measurements include electrical resistivity, magnetization (ZFC/FC), and London penetration depth measurements across varying magnetic fields and temperatures.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the intermetallic compound NiBi3. The analysis relies on fitting temperature dependencies of superfluid density ($ho_s(T)$) and penetration depth ($\Delta\lambda(T)$) to BCS models, comparing them against linear or quadratic power-law fits.

**Key observables.** Superconducting Transition Temperature ($T_c \approx 4.0$ K), Upper Critical Field ($H_{c2}$), Coherence Lengths ($\xi$), Superconducting Gap Ratio ($2\Delta_0 / k_B T_c \approx 4.1$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Type-II superconductivity, BCS framework, dirty limit, s-wave pairing symmetry.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The superconducting state is modeled using the single-gap isotropic s-wave BCS theory; orbital pair breaking dominates over Pauli limiting effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures show XRD patterns confirming purity, temperature-dependent magnetization curves used to fit $H_{c2}$, and plots of $\Delta\lambda(T)$ vs. T which are best described by the exponential BCS function.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by characterizing the normal state (resistivity) and then systematically analyzing superconducting properties using magnetic measurements ($M(H)$, $H_{c2}$) and penetration depth ($\Delta\lambda(T)$) to constrain the pairing symmetry and gap structure, culminating in a full BCS characterization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the physical characteristics, normal state and superconducting properties of NiBi3 single crystals. Measurements of electrical resistivity, magnetization, and London penetration depth demonstrate a superconducting transition temperature, Tc=4.0 K, with a sharp transition in resistivity (Tc = 0.20 K) and RRR = 18, reflecting the high quality of NiBi3 single crystals. With both orientations H perpendicular b and H parallel b of NiBi3 single crystals, we estimated the upper critical field, Hc2, from the magnetization data. In both orientations, Hc2 is significantly smaller than the Pauli limit, suggesting the orbital pair breaking in superconducting state. The coherence length, xi(0) = 26.78 nm, electron-phonon coupling constant, lambda(e-p) = 0.81, and penetration depth, lambda0 = 181.8 nm, suggest type-II superconductivity in NiBi3. In the superconducting state, lambda(T) is best described by an s-wave BCS model and does not have linear or quadratic dependency with T, which is in line with the expectation for a node-less superconducting order parameter. The temperature evolution of the superfluid density, obtained from lambda(T), reveals a fully gapped superconductivity in NiBi3, with a superconducting gap = 4.07. All the results from the present study indicate that NiBi3 is a typical type-II, BCS-like, moderately strong coupled, and fully gapped superconductor in the dirty limit.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reviews the decades-long mystery surrounding the superconducting order parameter in $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$. By analyzing recent experimental data that challenge early spin-triplet models, the authors calculate signatures (like the Kerr effect) for a candidate chiral d-wave singlet state. The work aims to pinpoint the true pairing symmetry by connecting theoretical predictions of transport properties to measurable physical effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** The investigation into unconventional superconductivity symmetry in correlated oxides like $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$ is highly relevant as it touches upon exotic pairing mechanisms and the interplay between electronic correlations and superconducting ground states, which are key areas of study for many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining the precise symmetry of the superconducting order parameter in strontium ruthenate ($	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$), which remains unknown despite decades of research.

**Main result.** Calculations for a chiral d-wave $	ext{E}_g$ pairing state yield a non-zero Hall conductance, suggesting that the symmetry might be singlet rather than the previously hypothesized triplet state.

**Method.** The authors use theoretical calculations to predict measurable quantities like the Kerr effect and Hall conductance ($\sigma_{xy}(\omega)$) for various superconducting pairing symmetries.

**Model / system.** The system is $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$, a low-temperature superconductor with a well-behaved Fermi liquid normal state. The analysis involves modeling the superconductivity using multi-orbital Hamiltonians and considering different pairing symmetries (p-wave vs. d-wave, spin singlet vs. triplet).

**Key observables.** Hall conductance ($\sigma_{xy}$), Kerr effect signal, Knight shift, and spin susceptibility $\chi_s(T)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superconducting gap symmetry ($E_g$ state considered), temperature dependence below $T_c$, and the presence/absence of inter-orbital pairing.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on comparing theoretical predictions (e.g., sum rules for $\sigma_{xy}$) against experimental measurements, while acknowledging that simple models may not capture all complexities like spin-orbit coupling effects.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes; the discussion revolves around calculating and interpreting spectral functions related to the Hall conductance and Kerr effect.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews historical evidence supporting a chiral p-wave triplet state, presents recent experimental results (Knight shift/$\mu	ext{SR}$) challenging this view towards spin singlet pairing, and concludes by performing new theoretical calculations for a chiral d-wave $	ext{E}_g$ state to predict measurable signatures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Strontium ruthenate is a very interesting low temperature superconductor with relatively simple normal state and very puzzling superconducting state properties. Despite over thirty years of intensive research, even the precise symmetry of the order parameter is not known. In this paper we briefly review some of the key developments, focussing on some of the newer experiments, especially those which challenged the previous understanding that it is a spin triplet odd parity chiral p-wave superconductor. Since Sr$_2$RuO$_4$ now appears most probably a spin singlet superconductor we have calculated the Kerr effect for one candidate $d$-wave pairing state. We find that for the chiral $d$-wave $E_g$ pairing state the calculated Hall conductance is non-zero and qualitatively similar to that found earlier under the assumption of a chiral p-wave $E_u$ order parameter. We characterize the Hall conductance in relation to two sum rules, one related to Berry curvatures in the Bogoliubov quasiparticle states, and the other $f$-sum rule indicating the presence of inter-orbital pairing.

</details>

**Summary.** Researchers used advanced momentum-resolved STEM-EELS to measure exciton dispersion in hBN films. They demonstrated that the characteristic linear (massless) exciton behavior observed in monolayers systematically changes—compressing and increasing group velocity—as the film thickness increases toward bulk values. This establishes a powerful experimental tool for understanding low-dimensional excitonic physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an unprecedentedly precise experimental probe into quasiparticle dynamics in 2D materials, offering insights into dimensionality crossover effects that are crucial for designing next-generation optoelectronic devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge was achieving direct, quantitative characterization of exciton dispersion evolution across different thicknesses, especially in the ultralow-momentum (q < 0.02 Å^-1) regime.

**Main result.** The study successfully resolved layer-dependent exciton dispersion in hBN, showing that increasing thickness compresses the nonanalytic linear-dispersion regime and increases the low-q group velocity towards a bulk limit.

**Method.** Defocus-engineered momentum-resolved electron energy-loss spectroscopy (STEM-EELS) was employed to achieve ultrahigh momentum resolution ($0.0002 	ext{ Å}^{-1}$).

**Model / system.** The system under study is excitons (electron-hole pairs) in freestanding hexagonal boron nitride (hBN), investigated as a function of film thickness and environmental coupling (e.g., temperature, adjacent graphene).

**Key observables.** Exciton band structure E(q), characteristic crossover momentum ($q_{cc}$), and low-q group velocity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ultralow-momentum regime ($q < 0.02 	ext{ Å}^{-1}$); thickness variation (monolayer to 25 layers).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the ability of defocus engineering to control momentum resolution, overcoming limitations of conventional STEM-EELS.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup comparing conventional vs. defocus methods; they show thickness evolution of exciton band structure and $\omega-q$ maps under varying environmental conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the advanced experimental methodology (defocus engineering) to achieve high momentum resolution, presents quantitative measurements showing layer-dependent changes in linear dispersion, and concludes by examining how external factors tune this fundamental low-dimensional exciton physics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exciton dispersion, which governs the propagation, scattering and radiative decay of electron-hole pairs, is essential to optoelectronics and quantum materials. In two-dimensional systems, weakened dielectric screening and long-range electron-hole exchange are predicted to induce nonanalytic exciton dispersion in the long-wavelength limit. However, direct quantitative characterization of its dimensional evolution remains lacking, especially in the ultralow-q regime (q < 0.02 $Å^{-1}$). Here we employ defocus-engineered momentum-resolved electron energy-loss spectroscopy in scanning transmission electron microscopy, achieving an ultrahigh momentum resolution of 0.0002 $Å^{-1}$. Using freestanding hBN as a prototypical platform, we resolve layer-dependent exciton dispersion and quantify its characteristic crossover momentum and group velocity in the long-wavelength limit. With increasing thickness, the nonanalytic linear-dispersion regime is progressively compressed, manifested by a reduction in characteristic crossover momentum q_c from $1.82 \times 10^{-1} Å^{-1}$ in the monolayer to $3.0 \times 10^{-1} Å^{-1}$ in 25 layers. Meanwhile, the low-q group velocity increases from $2.0 \times 10^{-3} c$ to $2.9 \times 10^{-2} c$, before the dispersion ultimately approaches the bulk-like parabolic limit. We further examine how the exciton band structure of monolayer hBN responds to its surrounding environment, including temperature, adjacent graphene layers, and interlayer twist in BN/graphene heterostructures. These findings uncover the fundamental physics of low-dimensional excitons, deliver valuable guidance for modulating exciton transport, diffusion and quasiparticle coupling in layered quantum materials, and establish a powerful experimental route to explore low-dimensional exciton physics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a real-space theoretical model to understand interface states in graphene nanostructures formed by connecting triangulenes and AGNRs. It derives a universal rule linking the number of localized node orbitals to the end states and zero-energy modes of the constituent parts. This framework proves that these junctions can be engineered to create artificial nanoribbons with highly controllable flat subbands for quantum transport applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on localized, geometrically determined states (CLS) provides a concrete, real-space mechanism for engineering electronic properties in low-dimensional carbon systems, which is highly relevant for solid-state quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to elucidate the microscopic origin of interface states in complex graphene architectures, moving beyond purely bulk topological descriptions.

**Main result.** The research establishes a universal relation governing the number and chirality of localized node orbitals formed at junctions, demonstrating that these sites act as elementary building blocks for constructing nanoribbons with tunable flat subbands.

**Method.** A real-space theoretical framework using a tight-binding Hamiltonian is developed to model electronic structure evolution by tuning inter-building block couplings.

**Model / system.** The system involves artificial quantum materials based on graphene, specifically Y-junctions formed by connecting $n$-triangulenes and armchair graphene nanoribbons (AGNRs). The analysis uses a single-$p_z$ orbital tight-binding model.

**Key observables.** Number and chirality of node orbitals ($	ext{N}_{	ext{node},\delta}$), flat subbands near the Fermi energy, and tunneling spectra.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strength between graphene building blocks ($t_{es}$) is a key tuning parameter; an applied longitudinal electric field (Stark effect) is used as a diagnostic tool.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the interface states can be understood by analyzing the hybridization of end states and zero-energy modes, providing a real-space picture over bulk topological invariants.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structural components (triangulenes, AGNRs) and device configurations used for calculating electronic properties and transport spectra.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the physical system and theoretical framework, then analyzes specific junction geometries (e.g., 3T-GNR/7y-AGNR/3T-GNR), derives the universal relation for node orbitals, and concludes by demonstrating their utility in designing tunable quantum transport elements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Artificial quantum materials based on atomically precise graphene nanostructures provide an ideal platform for exploring quantum phenomena arising from localized electronic states. Here, we develop a real-space theoretical framework to elucidate the microscopic origin of interface states in graphene architectures composed of $n$-triangulenes and armchair graphene nanoribbons (AGNRs). By continuously tuning the coupling between graphene building blocks, we reveal the evolution of triangulene zero-energy modes and AGNR end states into compact localized node orbitals at three-arm junctions. The number and chirality of these node orbitals obey a universal relation, $N_{node,δ}=|N_{es,t,A(B)}-N_{tri,0,B(A)}|$, where $N_{es,t}$ denotes the total number of AGNR end states contributed by the three AGNR arms at the junction, and $N_{tri,0}$ is the number of zero-energy modes of the attached triangulene. The chirality of the node orbitals is determined by the dominant constituent: when $N_{es,t}>N_{tri,0}$, they inherit the sublattice chirality of the AGNR end states ($δ=A(B)$), whereas for $N_{tri,0}>N_{es,t}$ they inherit that of the triangulene zero-energy modes ($δ=B(A)$). This real-space picture provides a transparent understanding of compact localized state (CLS) formation beyond conventional bulk topological descriptions. Using experimentally synthesized triangulene nanographenes as representative examples, we further explain the emergence of their zero-energy modes and quantitatively reproduce their tunneling spectra within an extended Anderson model. Finally, we demonstrate that these node orbitals can serve as elementary building blocks for constructing artificial graphene nanoribbons with highly tunable flat subbands near the Fermi energy. The resulting CLSs exhibit controllable degeneracy and strongly anisotropic quantum transport.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates a superconducting diode effect in an asymmetric NbN nanoring using only geometric asymmetry. By applying an external magnetic field, the device exhibits switchable nonreciprocity in its critical currents, confirming that curvature effects can induce one-way supercurrent transport. This establishes a minimal design principle for next-generation superconducting electronics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a simple, lithographically scalable platform for realizing non-reciprocal superconducting transport, which is crucial for building low-power cryogenic electronic components that rely on directional current flow.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to demonstrate a superconducting diode effect, characterized by nonreciprocal critical currents, using a structurally minimal platform.

**Main result.** Pronounced and polarity-switchable critical-current nonreciprocity is achieved solely by introducing geometric asymmetry in an NbN nanoring when subjected to an out-of-plane magnetic field.

**Method.** Transport measurements were performed using a standard four-probe method, measuring current-voltage (I-V) characteristics under varying perpendicular magnetic fields and temperatures.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an asymmetric nanoring fabricated from a single material, NbN. The nonreciprocity arises from the interplay of curvature-induced current crowding, Meissner screening currents, and asymmetric vortex-entry barriers.

**Key observables.** Critical currents ($I_{c+}$ and $I_{c-}$), nonreciprocity ($\Delta I_c$), diode efficiency ($\eta$), and retrapping current ($I_r$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (2 K to 8 K), perpendicular magnetic field strength, and the geometric asymmetry of the nanoring.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The nonreciprocity is attributed to directional modulation of the superconducting switching instability, distinct from thermal effects or uniform superconductivity suppression.

**Figures summary.** Figures show SEM images and I-V curves demonstrating field-dependent, switchable nonreciprocity. Other figures plot magnetic field dependence of $I_c$ and $\eta$, and temperature dependence comparing results to BCS gap/superfluid density models.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the superconducting diode effect in an asymmetric nanoring by measuring critical current asymmetry ($\Delta I_c$) under applied magnetic fields. It systematically analyzes the field and temperature dependence of this nonreciprocity, contrasting it with reciprocal effects like retrapping currents to pinpoint the geometric origin.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconducting diodes, which exhibit nonreciprocal critical currents, are promising building blocks for low-power cryogenic electronics and superconducting circuits. Existing superconducting diode platforms commonly rely on Josephson junctions, multilayer heterostructures, ferromagnetic elements, gate-difined structures. Here, we demonstrate a geometrically induced superconducting diode effect realized in a structurally minimal, single-materials NbN nanoring, where inversion-symmetry breaking is introduced solely by the asymmetric geometry. The device exhibits pronounced and polarity-switchable critical-current nonreciprocity. Systematic magnetic-field and temperature-dependent measurements reveal that, at low fields, the applied magnetic field redistributes the critical current asymmetrically between opposite bias directions without significantly reducing the overall superconducting current-carrying capability. Moreover, the maximal nonreciprocity and diode efficiency exhibit distinct temperature dependence: the maximal diode efficiency follows the evolution of the energy gap, whereas the maximal nonreciprocity is more closely associated with the superfluid density. These results establish asymmetric superconducting nanorings as a minimal geometric platform for studying nonreciprocal superconducting transport and provide a simple design principle for future superconducting electronics.

</details>
